# Research Bank — Chapter 30: School and Education (`education`)

**Eras 1-5 (before-1500 to 1750-1800) researched 2026-09-08.** Eras 6-10 belong to later agents; material found for them while doing eras 3-5 is parked in PART A-FWD, and material parked here by other chapters is in PART B.

Sourcing rule in force: every fact below carries its source. Where sources disagree, the disagreement is stated rather than resolved. Where the record does not settle a question, that is said plainly and the claim is kept out of the outline.

---

# PART A — Eras 1-5, researched (before-1500 through 1750-1800)

## 1. Before 1500

**What the outline claims, and what backs it.** There were no schoolhouses, grades, school years, or full-time teachers in the land that became the United States. Teaching happened inside families and communities, tied to the seasons and the work. There was no writing system in use north of Mexico; the Cherokee syllabary, the first writing system devised for a language spoken in what is now the United States, came in 1821 (parked note below, from `native-nations`; Britannica, "Sequoyah"). Everything that had to be kept was kept in memory, in speech, and in objects made to prompt memory.

- **Wampum as a memory record (Haudenosaunee).** The Onondaga Nation, wampum keeper for the Haudenosaunee Confederacy, describes the practice in its own words: "The speaker puts the words of the agreement into the wampum as the strings or belts are woven together," and "Each speaker thereafter uses the wampum to remember the initial agreement and the history that has happened to date." The Nation calls the belts "our living history," and says Onondaga was made Wampum Keeper "at the founding of the Great Peace." (Source: Onondaga Nation, "Wampum," https://www.onondaganation.org/culture/wampum/ .) **This is the concrete, checkable example of learning-by-memory for the era: a person is trained to hold and recite a record, and the object is the prompt.**
- **The date of the Great Law of Peace is disputed and must be stated as disputed.** Haudenosaunee oral tradition and wampum place its origin between about 1000 and 1400 CE; scholars working from written accounts have generally put it around 1450; some argue for the late 1100s. (Sources: Onondaga Nation cultural pages; Encyclopaedia Britannica, "Iroquois Confederacy"; the range as reported at Great Law of Peace overviews.) **Do not pick one.**
- **Oral tradition is a teaching system, not a substitute for one.** The Smithsonian's National Museum of the American Indian, in its Native Knowledge 360° resource "Celebrating Native Cultures Through Words: Storytelling and Oral Traditions," states that "the oral tradition continues to be an integral part of Indigenous teaching and learning." (Source: NMAI NK360, https://americanindian.si.edu/nk360/resources/storytelling-and-oral-traditions .)

**What is NOT known / what to keep out.** No individual pupil or teacher in this land before 1500 is documented by name. The outline says so and names nobody. Any "typical child" here would be an invented person (hard-subjects-policy §4). There is also no reliable count of anything — no enrollment, no literacy figure — because there was nothing being counted and no one counting.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): the "no writing north of Mexico" claim, checked against the one known challenge

- **The outline's claim** ("Nobody north of Mexico wrote anything down, because no writing system was in use there yet") has one scholarly challenge a writer should know about. The Mi'kmaq of the northeast coast used drawn symbols (called komqwejwi'kasikl) that the French missionary Chrestien Le Clercq adapted from 1677 to write prayers. Scholars disagree on whether the symbols Le Clercq found were a full writing system or a memory aid. In 1978 Ives Goddard and William Fitzhugh of the Smithsonian's anthropology department argued that the pre-missionary symbols were only a memory aid and could not be used to write new texts. No example of the symbols from before the missionaries survives. (Source: Wikipedia, "Mi'kmaw hieroglyphs," https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi%27kmaw_hieroglyphs , summarizing Goddard and Fitzhugh, *Science* 1978, which this pass did not read. **Tertiary source only.**)
- **Where that leaves the prose.** The symbols are known only from 1677 onward, and from Le Clercq's mission on the Gaspé Peninsula in New France, which is in what is now Canada (same source). So the outline's sentence stands for the land that became the United States before 1500, provided the writer says "no writing system", not "no marks or symbols". Wampum (above) is itself a system of made objects that carry a record, which is why the safer wording is about writing, not about records.
- **Land:** none taken in this era within this chapter's angle. Nothing to record.

---

## 2. The 1500s

**Era claim: there were no schools in this land in the sense the rest of the chapter means.** The only European teaching was religious instruction attached to Spanish missions, and its purpose was conversion.

- **When mission teaching began in Florida.** Efforts to convert Native people in Florida began within a few years of Pedro Menéndez de Avilés's 1565 founding of St. Augustine; by the 1580s Franciscan friars had set up a system of missions across Florida. The mission of San Juan del Puerto, on Fort George Island, was established "sometime in the 1570s" and became one of the largest. (Source: National Park Service, Timucuan Ecological and Historic Preserve, "Missions in Northeast Florida," https://www.nps.gov/timu/learn/historyculture/missions_fgi.htm .) Note the softness of the date: NPS gives a decade, not a year.
- **What was taught.** Catholic doctrine, European customs, farming, and reading and writing — in Spanish and in Timucua. (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **Who says Native people learned to read, and how much weight it carries.** The claim comes from the friar who did the teaching. Father Francisco Pareja, the Franciscan at San Juan del Puerto, reported that "many Indian men and women have learned to read in less than six months, and they write letters to one another in their own language." (Source: NPS, "Missions in Northeast Florida," quoting Pareja.) **This is a missionary's own account of his own success and should be attributed to him in the text, not stated flatly as a fact.**
- **Pareja's books are 1600s, not 1500s.** He published three catechisms, a confessional, and a grammar of the Timucua language; the *Confessionario* is dated 1613. His works are the only source now existing for the Timucua language — no speakers remain. (Source: NPS, as above.) **Boundary rule: the books belong in the 1600s cell, the teaching in the 1500s cell.**
- **New Mexico.** Juan de Oñate's colonizing expedition reached the upper Rio Grande in 1598; Franciscan missions and their doctrina teaching in New Mexico begin from that year, at the very end of the era. (Source: National Park Service, El Camino Real de Tierra Adentro / Salinas Pueblo Missions historical pages.)

### The one documented named pupil of the 1500s: Paquiquineo (Don Luís de Velasco)

Everything here is from Encyclopedia Virginia (Virginia Humanities), "Don Luís de Velasco / Paquiquineo (fl. 1561–1571)," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/don-luis-de-velasco-paquiquineo-fl-1561-1571/ , with the mission's end also in the Virginia Places and Virtual Jamestown summaries.

- A young man from the Chesapeake — possibly Kiskiack or Paspahegh, about seventeen — left North America on the Spanish caravel *Santa Catalina* in late June 1561. **Historians dispute whether he went willingly or was kidnapped; Encyclopedia Virginia states the dispute rather than settling it, and so must this chapter.**
- He reached Seville on September 9, 1561, was in Madrid by the end of October 1561, met King Philip II, sailed from Cádiz in late May 1562, and arrived in Mexico on August 10, 1562.
- He was baptized in Mexico City in 1562, **"on the brink of death"** from illness, and took the name of the viceroy of New Spain, Don Luís de Velasco.
- From 1566 to 1570 he studied in Seville with the Jesuits. **The record does not say what he was taught** — Encyclopedia Virginia gives no curriculum, no statement about reading, writing, or Latin. Say that it does not say. Do not fill it in.
- He landed on the James River in September 1570 with Father Juan Bautista de Segura's Jesuit party — the Ajacán mission, begun about September 10, 1570. He rejoined his own family. On February 4, 1571 he killed three of the Jesuits (Quirós, Gabriel de Solís, Juan Baptista Méndez) and then attacked the mission itself, killing Father Segura and the rest. The only survivor was the altar boy, Alonso de Olmos.

**Why he is the story here and not "a mission pupil."** The seed asked for a mission pupil and warned that if none could be verified we should say so. No named pupil of a 1500s Florida or New Mexico mission school could be verified in this pass. Paquiquineo is the one person in this era whose schooling by Europeans in connection with a mission to this land is documented by name and by date. He is not a composite and not a stand-in.

**Movie:** none verified. Do not add a Movie line.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): land, the Spanish reprisal of 1572, and why nobody speaks Timucua

**Land: who lived where the missions were built.**
- **St. Augustine was built in a Timucua town.** In September 1565 Pedro Menéndez de Avilés and Chief Seloy of the Timucua met at what is now St. Augustine. Menéndez and about 800 colonists (including 26 women and an unknown number of enslaved Africans) made their first settlement at Seloy's town and used Seloy's council house as the first Spanish fort. (Source: Florida Museum of Natural History, University of Florida, "St. Augustine: America's Ancient City," timeline, "First Contacts," https://www.floridamuseum.ufl.edu/staugustine/timeline/first-contacts/ .) Another summary gives 24 women (search summary of a trolley-tour page, not used). **Use 26, from the Florida Museum.** The Florida Museum page does not say what happened to Seloy's people after the Spanish moved in. Say that it does not say.
- **San Juan del Puerto** was on Fort George Island among the Mocama, the coastal Timucua-speaking people (NPS, "Missions in Northeast Florida," as in §2 above).
- **Ajacán (1570-71)** was on the James and York rivers, in the country of the Powhatan chiefdoms. Historians have suggested Paquiquineo came from a Paspahegh, Chiskiack (Kiskiack) or Kecoughtan family and do not settle which. (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, "Don Luís de Velasco / Paquiquineo," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/don-luis-de-velasco-paquiquineo-fl-1561-1571/ .)

**The 1572 reprisal, which the outline's Paquiquineo story does not yet tell.** (Named actor, act, count.)
- A relief ship found no trace of the Jesuits. The Spanish governor of Florida, Pedro Menéndez de Avilés, sent a military expedition to the James River in **August 1572**. The Spanish captured some of Paquiquineo's people in a fight. "Several of them were found guilty of the Jesuits' murder and hanged from the ship's yardarms." One of the men hanged wore a paten (the metal dish used at Mass) around his neck. The Powhatan people returned the altar boy Alonso de Olmos to the Spanish. Paquiquineo "was nowhere to be found," and he "disappeared from European records and is not mentioned in any verifiable oral tradition." (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)
- **The count differs by source.** Encyclopedia Virginia says "several" were hanged and does not give a number. Wikipedia's "Ajacán Mission" entry says Menéndez's men baptized and hanged **eight** men and killed **about 20** in the attack (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajac%C3%A1n_Mission , tertiary, not used as the main source). **Write "several men, at least eight by one count" only if the writer names the weaker source. The safe sentence is "Spanish soldiers hanged several of them from the ship's yardarms."** Whether Menéndez gave the hanging order in person or a subordinate did is not stated in Encyclopedia Virginia's summary. **SEARCHED, NOT FOUND** folded here: the names of the men hanged. No source reached names them. How the prose can say it: "The Spanish records do not give the names of the men they hanged."

**Why nobody speaks Timucua now (the outline states the fact without the cause).**
- About 200,000 Timucua-speaking people lived in what is now northern Florida and southern Georgia in the 1500s. By the 1650s about 2,000 remained. Epidemics of diseases the Spanish brought caused most of the loss. After the English settled Charles Town (1670), Carolina slave traders and Native groups allied with them raided the Spanish missions again and again for captives. When Spain gave Florida to Britain after the Seven Years' War (1763), Spanish officials took all the remaining Timucua to Cuba. The last Timucua of full descent died in Cuba in 1767. (Source: Matthew Holt Jennings, "Timucua," *Dictionary of American History*, at Encyclopedia.com, https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/dictionaries-thesauruses-pictures-and-press-releases/timucua .)
- The 200,000 figure is an estimate of a population nobody counted. Say "about" and say whose estimate. Other summaries give 50,000 by 1595 and 1,000 by 1700 (Wikipedia, "Timucua," tertiary, not used).
- **Owner:** the destruction of the Timucua belongs to `native-nations`. This chapter's slice is one sentence: Pareja's books are the only record of the language because the people who spoke it died of disease, were taken in raids, or were shipped to Cuba in 1763.

---


## 3. The 1600s

**Era claim: in New England, schooling becomes a legal duty within a generation of landing, and the reason given is reading the Bible. Everywhere else on the coast, learning stays a family and apprenticeship matter.**

### 3a. Boston Latin School — the town meeting, the date, and the first schoolmaster

- **Founded 23 April 1635.** Boston Latin School opened on 23 April 1635 and is the oldest school in the United States still running, and the first public school in what became the country. (Sources: Boston Latin School Association, https://www.bls.org/ ; The Freedom Trail Foundation, "Boston Latin School Site / Benjamin Franklin Statue," https://www.thefreedomtrail.org/trail-sites/boston-latin-school-sitebenjamin-franklin-statue ; Teachers College, Columbia University library, "Today In History: Boston Latin School Is Founded," https://library.tc.columbia.edu/blog/content/2025/april/today-in-history-boston-latin-school-is-founded.php .)
- **The town meeting came first, on 13 April 1635** — ten days before the school opened. The townsmen of Boston met on public notice and agreed to ask Philemon Pormort to be schoolmaster. **The town record's own wording:** "On the 13th of the second month, 1635...At a Generall meeting upon publique notice...it was...generally agreed upon that our brother Philemon Pormort shall be intreated to become scholemaster for the teaching and nourtering of children with us." (Sources: as above, quoting the Boston town records; also the Boston Public Latin School historical marker, https://www.hmdb.org/m.asp?m=18509 .)
  - **Note on the date wording for the writer:** "the second month" is April, not February. The English calendar of 1635 began the year on 25 March, so March was the first month. Say this plainly if the quote is used; a reader will otherwise read it as February.
- **The first classes met in Pormort's own house**, not in a schoolhouse. (Source: Teachers College library, as above.)
- **What was taught:** Latin and Greek, plus elementary subjects. The school was modelled on the free grammar school of Boston, England. (Source: Boston Latin School Association, as above.)
- **What is NOT settled:** these are institutional and local-history sources, not the manuscript town record itself. The 13 April meeting and the 23 April opening are consistently given, but this pass did not reach a scan of the original Boston town records. State the facts; do not claim to have read the manuscript.

### 3b. The New England Primer — the schoolbook, and what is actually known about it

- **Who made it and when.** The English printer Benjamin Harris made *The New England Primer* for American colonial readers **between 1687 and 1690**. That range is the American Antiquarian Society's, and the AAS says the knowledge of the book's origins comes only from publishers' and booksellers' advertisements — the production records do not survive. The first notice of the book is an advertisement Harris placed in 1690. (Source: American Antiquarian Society, "'To Which is Added': The History, Structure, and Conservation of New England Primers at the American Antiquarian Society," https://pastispresent.org/2024/fellowsfinds/to-which-is-added-the-history-structure-and-conservation-of-new-england-primers-at-the-american-antiquarian-society/ .)
- **No copy printed before 1727 survives.** The AAS states plainly: "No copies of the text dated before 1727 are known to survive." The AAS holds a 1727 edition. So the most-used schoolbook of colonial America is known in its earliest form only from advertisements. **This is a good honest fact for the chapter, not a hole to paper over.** (Source: AAS, as above.)
- **What was in it.** Lessons in reading, spelling, and morality, with woodcut illustrations of the alphabet. In the 1727 edition the illustrated alphabet is followed by the instruction: "Now the Child being entered in his Letters and Spelling, let him learn these and such like Sentences by Heart." Children were expected to recite and memorise it, and the point of the memorising was Christian discipline as much as reading. (Sources: AAS, as above; the surviving-editions summary at History of Information, https://www.historyofinformation.com/detail.php?entryid=3680 .)
- **Do not state a print run or a sales figure.** The AAS article gives none, and none was verified in this pass.

### 3c. "Spare the rod and spoil the child" — where the sentence actually comes from

The outline seed asked for this and warned against asserting it without a source. It is older than the Bible translation people usually credit, and the exact modern wording is not biblical at all.

- **Proverbs 13:24 is the root idea, not the phrase.** The verse says that whoever spares the rod hates their children. The words "spoil the child" are not in it. (Source: the origin study at Word Histories, "origin of 'spare the rod and spoil the child'," https://wordhistories.net/2017/10/27/spare-the-rod-origin/ .)
- **Ælfric of Eynsham, an English monk who lived about 950 to 1010**, wrote the oldest known English version, in a homily: "He who spareth his rod hateth his child; and he who loveth it, teacheth it soundly." (Source: Word Histories, as above.)
- **The Wycliffe Bible of 1382** renders Proverbs 13:24 in Middle English: "Who spareth to the ȝerde, hatith his sone; who forsothe looueth hym, bisili techeth." ("ȝerde" is a rod or stick.) (Source: Word Histories, as above.)
- **John Skelton**, about 1529–30, wrote in *Magnyfycence* of sparing "the rod Of correccyon." (Source: Word Histories, as above.)
- **The modern shape of the phrase is Samuel Butler's, in the mock-heroic poem *Hudibras*:** "Love is a Boy by Poets stil'd; Then spare the Rod, and spill the Child" — "spill" later became "spoil". **The date is disputed and must be stated as disputed.** *Hudibras* came out in three parts, in 1663, 1664 and 1678, and the line is in the second part; sources variously give 1662, 1663 and 1664 for that part. Do not pick one silently. (Source: Word Histories, as above; the three-part publication is given at the same source.)
- **Why it belongs in this chapter.** It was a household and schoolroom rule, not a proverb people quoted at a distance. The rod was standard schoolroom equipment. See §3d.

### 3d. The two Massachusetts school laws — 1642 and 1647

- **1642 — the duty is put on parents and masters, not on towns.** The law ordered parents and masters to "indeavour to teach by themselves or others, their children & apprentices so much learning as may enable them perfectly to read the English tongue, & knowledge of the Capital Lawes," and to "breed & bring up their children & apprentices in some honest lawful calling, labour or employment, either in husbandry, or some other trade." Selectmen visited households to check. A household that failed could be fined, and children or apprentices could be taken away and placed with another master who would teach them. (Source: Paul Revere House, "That Old Deluder Satan: Puritan Emphasis on Compulsory Education," https://www.paulreverehouse.org/that-old-deluder-satan-puritan-emphasis-on-compulsory-education/ , quoting the law.)
  - **Note for the writer:** this is a reading law, not a schooling law. It required no school and no teacher. It is the first law in this land ordering that children be taught to read at all.
- **1647 — the "Old Deluder Satan" act, and why it has that name.** Five years later the General Court passed a second law because the first was widely ignored. Its preamble names the reason: it is "one of the chief projects of that old deluder Satan to keep men from the knowledge of the Scriptures." Every town of 50 households had to "forthwith appoint one within their town to teach all such children as shall resort to him to write and read, whose wages shall be paid either by the parents or masters." Every town of 100 families had to set up "a grammar school to instruct youth so far as they may be fitted for the university" — meaning Harvard. A town that did not comply paid £5 a year to the nearest school until it did. (Sources: Paul Revere House, as above; Middle Tennessee State University, First Amendment Encyclopedia, "Old Deluder Satan Act of 1647," https://firstamendment.mtsu.edu/article/old-deluder-satan-act-of-1647/ ; Massachusetts Teachers Association, "Schools took root early in Mass.," https://www.massteacher.org/Founding-of-the-MTA .)
  - **The reason stated in the law is religious, and the chapter should say so rather than smoothing it into "the value of education."** The men who wrote it wanted children able to read the Bible for themselves.
  - Other New England colonies passed similar laws; Rhode Island did not. (Source: MTSU, as above.)
  - **Fine amount:** £5 yearly, from the MTA and Paul Revere House accounts. This pass did not reach the manuscript statute, so state the figure with its source rather than as a reading of the original.

### 3e. Harvard, 1636, and the Indian College — the first college, and the first Native students

- **Harvard was founded in 1636**, and the 1647 law's "fitted for the university" means it. (Sources: MTSU First Amendment Encyclopedia, as above; Harvard University Native American Program, https://hunap.harvard.edu/history .)
- **Harvard's 1650 charter promised to educate Native students.** Its wording pledges the college to "the education of the English and Indian youth of this Country." (Source: Harvard University Native American Program, "History," https://hunap.harvard.edu/history .)
- **The Indian College building stood in Harvard Yard from 1655**, where Matthews Hall now stands. **The sources disagree on when it came down:** Harvard Magazine says it "fell down in 1695"; the Harvard University Native American Program says the building stood until 1698. State the disagreement; do not pick one. (Sources: Harvard Magazine, "For Caleb Cheeshahteaumuck and Successors," https://www.harvardmagazine.com/sites/default/files/html/1997/07a/jhj.caleb.html ; HUNAP, as above.)
- **The printing press housed in the Indian College printed the first Bible published in North America**, in the Algonquian language, in 1663. (Source: Harvard Magazine, as above.)
- **What happened to the students of the Indian College.** Harvard Magazine names the cohort: Caleb Cheeshahteaumuck, who graduated; his classmate Joel Iacoomes, who "perished in a shipwreck just before Commencement"; "two others who died of disease before completing their courses of study, and one who left to become a mariner." (Source: Harvard Magazine, as above.) **Say this plainly. Of the handful of Native students brought to Harvard in the 1600s, one finished, one drowned days before finishing, two died of disease, and one left.**

### 3f. The praying towns and the schools in them

- **Natick, 1651, was the first "praying town."** John Eliot worked with Native leaders — Waban and Cutshamekin of the Massachusett, and John Speen (Qualalanset) of the Nipmuc — to set it up on a bend in the Charles River. The town built a meetinghouse with a school in it, **and the schoolmaster was a Massachusett man named Monequassun.** Eliot went on to help set up more than a dozen other praying towns in eastern and central Massachusetts. (Source: Natick Historical Society, "The Algonquian and English Roots of Natick," https://www.natickhistoricalsociety.org/algonquian-and-english-roots-of-natick .)
  - **Monequassun is a named Native schoolmaster and belongs in the chapter by name.** The Natick Historical Society gives no birth or death date and no account of what he taught, so the chapter should not supply either.
  - The same source records that Job Nesuton, the Massachusett man who taught Eliot the language, was killed in King Philip's War.
- **Eliot's schoolbooks.** His printing in America began with an Algonquian catechism in 1653; his *Indian Primer* of 1669 gathered a longer and a shorter catechism, the Apostles' Creed, and a confession of faith. (Sources: Natick Historical Society, as above; Jesus College, Cambridge, "John Eliot," https://www.jesus.cam.ac.uk/john-eliot ; the 1669 *Indian Primer* text, https://biblicalelearning.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/09/John_Eliot_IndianPrimer_Logic_Primer.htm .)
  - **Say what the books were for.** They taught reading in the Massachusett language, and every one of them taught Christian doctrine at the same time. Reading and conversion were the same lesson.

### 3g. Who was NOT yet forbidden to learn — and when that changed

The 1600s carry no law in these colonies making it a crime to teach an enslaved person to read or write. **That law comes in 1740, in the era after next, and this chapter must not backdate it.** See §4f. What the 1600s do carry is that schooling cost money — the 1647 law made parents or masters pay the schoolmaster's wage — and that the Latin grammar schools took boys only.

### 3h. The named schoolmaster for this era: Ezekiel Cheever (1615–1708)

- Born in London on 25 January 1615; died in Boston on 21 August 1708. (Source: Rutgers University, Database of Classical Scholars, "Cheever, Ezekiel," https://dbcs.rutgers.edu/all-scholars/8603-cheever-ezekiel .) **Some sources give 1614 for the birth year**, because England's calendar then began the year on 25 March; state 1614 or 1615 with the reason, or give the death year alone.
- He taught Latin and Greek for **70 straight years — "longer than anyone else in the history of this country,"** in the Database of Classical Scholars' words. He ran the public schools at New Haven from 1638, taught later at Charlestown from 1661, and on **29 December 1670 was invited to head the Boston Latin School**, where he taught for his last 38 years. He was still teaching when he died at 93. (Sources: Rutgers DBCS, as above; Boston Latin School Association news page, https://www.bls.org/apps/news/show_news.jsp?REC_ID=527694&id=0 .)
- **His classroom, described by the same source:** a smoky fireplace, poor light, and "a bundle of birch rods beside his desk." **That is the rod of "spare the rod" as actual furniture — not a saying, a bundle of sticks kept within reach of the desk all day.** (Source: Rutgers DBCS, as above.)
- **His book.** *A Short Introduction to the Latin Tongue*, known as Cheever's *Accidence*, was published in Boston in 1708 and stayed in use through many editions for nearly 200 years — longer than any other Latin textbook printed in this country. (Source: Rutgers DBCS, as above.) **Note the date: the printing is the year he died.** Sources calling it "probably the earliest American schoolbook" are describing its content, not claiming an earlier printing; this pass did not verify a pre-1708 edition, so do not claim one.
- Cotton Mather, who had been his pupil, preached his funeral sermon and called him a teacher "than whom New England had known no better." (Source: Rutgers DBCS, as above.)
- **Movie:** none verified. Do not add a Movie line.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): a NAMED beaten child at Boston Latin, John Barnard (DECISIONS #4)

This fills the ruling that `education` should name a beaten child where a source supports one. The source is the boy's own account, written as an old man.

- **Who.** John Barnard, born in Boston on **6 November 1681**, later the minister at Marblehead, died **24 January 1770**. He wrote an autobiography at about 85, at the request of Ezra Stiles of Yale (Stiles's letter is dated 3 October 1767). It was printed in the *Collections of the Massachusetts Historical Society*, Third Series, vol. 5, in **1836**. (Source: the headnote and extract "New England Schooldays" in *Colonial Prose and Poetry*, ed. William P. Trent and Benjamin W. Wells, at Bartleby, https://www.bartleby.com/lit-hub/colonial-prose-and-poetry/john-barnard/ , read in full through curl on 2026-09-27. WebFetch returned 403.)
- **When he went to Cheever's school.** "In the spring of my eighth year I was sent to the grammar school, under the tuition of the aged, venerable and justly famous Mr. Ezekiel Cheever." The spring of his eighth year is **1689**, when he was seven. He left after a few weeks, spent a year and a half at another private school, and went back to Cheever, who put him in the lowest class and moved him up. So the beatings below fall in about **1690 to 1696** (he entered Harvard at fifteen, same source). **Era: the 1600s.**
- **What Cheever did, in Barnard's own words.** Cheever told him openly: "You Barnard, I know you can do well enough if you will; but you are so full of play that you hinder your classmates from getting their lessons; and therefore, if any of them cannot perform their duty, I shall correct you for it." When a classmate could not say his lesson, "our master beat me." The classmate saw this and kept neglecting his book on purpose, "for which I was still corrected, and that for several days." Barnard then beat the other boy after school ("drubbed him heartily"), and that boy "never came to school any more."
- Barnard also wrote: "Though I was often beaten for my play, and my little roguish tricks, yet I don't remember that I was ever beaten for my book more than once or twice." One of those times Cheever set the class to turn Aesop's Fables into Latin verse. Barnard could not do it, "for which master corrected me, and this he did two or three days going," until Barnard told him he had tried his hardest, and Cheever stopped asking it of him. (Same source.)
- **What the source does NOT say:** it does not name the implement. "Beat" and "corrected" are the words. The birch rods beside Cheever's desk are documented (§3h, Rutgers DBCS), but Barnard does not say which he was beaten with. **Write "beat him" and do not add the rod to Barnard's account.** It also does not say how many blows, or that he was injured.
- **Other chapters:** no other outline or manuscript tells Barnard (grep of `outlines/` and `manuscript/`, 2026-09-27). Suggested story slug if a writer wants a block: `john-barnard`. Movie: none.
- **Style note for the writer:** "corrected" is the period's word for a beating. Define it at first use, or use "beat," which is also Barnard's own word.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): "the oldest school in the country" is disputed

- **The outline says Boston Latin School "is still open, which makes it the oldest school in the country."** Collegiate School in Manhattan disputes that. Dutch colonists founded it in New Amsterdam. For years it gave its founding date as 1638. In 1982 its officials changed the date to **1628**, the year the Dutch Reformed minister Jonas Michaelius arrived, citing two letters in which Michaelius wrote that he began teaching in Manhattan in 1628. (Source: *The Harvard Crimson*, "Manhattan's Collegiate Says It's Oldest," 15 October 1984, https://www.thecrimson.com/article/1984/10/15/manhattans-collegiate-says-its-oldest-pin/ .) The *Crimson* piece reports a Collegiate official saying there was "an informal setting where education was taking place before" the 1638 date, so the 1628 date rests on teaching, not on a founded school.
- **How the prose can say it without picking a side:** "Boston Latin School opened in 1635 and is the oldest public school in the country still open. Collegiate School in New York, founded by Dutch colonists, dates itself to 1628, and the two schools' claims to be oldest of all have never been settled." Boston Latin's claim to be the oldest **public** school still running is not disputed in any source reached.
- **Land.** Boston was built on the Shawmut peninsula, which the Massachusett called Mushauwomuk, "the boat landing place." It lay inside the territory of the Massachusett nation, whose sachem Chickataubut had seen about three quarters of his people die of disease before John Winthrop's company of about 1,000 settlers arrived in 1630. (Source: The West End Museum, "An Early History of the Shawmut Peninsula," https://thewestendmuseum.org/history/era/new-fields/an-early-history-of-the-shawmut-peninsula/ , via search summary, **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.) Natick was set up on Massachusett and Nipmuc land with Massachusett and Nipmuc leaders (§3f, Natick Historical Society). New Amsterdam, where Collegiate began, was on Manhattan, Lenape land (not re-sourced in this pass).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): what happened to the people of the praying towns in 1675 (Deer Island)

The outline tells Natick's school (1651) and stops. The same people were imprisoned on an island twenty-four years later. A reader who learns about the school and not about Deer Island gets a false picture. **No outline in the book tells Deer Island (grep of `outlines/`, 2026-09-27).** `native-nations` leads on the war. This chapter's slice is that the families of the first Native school were the ones interned. Parked for `native-nations` in the checkpoint's TO PARK list.

- **Who ordered it and when.** King Philip's War (Metacom's war) began in 1675. "Just five months after the war began, colonists set up an internment camp on Deer Island to relocate 'Christianized' Indigenous people whom they believed could turn against the English and join the rebellion." (Source: National Park Service, Boston Harbor Islands, "Deer Island," https://www.nps.gov/places/deer-island.htm .) The Massachusetts authorities issued the order on **13 October 1675**, and it began with the people of Natick. (Source: Partnership of Historic Bostons, "From paradise to prison," https://historicbostons.org/blog-1/deer .) The Natick people were taken out to the island by boat near midnight on **30 October 1675** with little clothing or food (search summary of the same page and of Indian Country Today, "Deer Island: A History of Human Tragedy Remembered," https://ictnews.org/archive/deer-island-a-history-of-human-tragedy-remembered/ , **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**). The body that gave the order is called "the General Court" in one summary and "the English Council" in the NPS page. **Say "the men who governed the Massachusetts colony ordered" unless a later pass reads the order itself.** Individual names of the men who voted it: SEARCHED, NOT FOUND (below).
- **How many.** NPS: "Those interned numbered about 500 to 1,100 people," and "Women and children made up the majority." Historic Bostons: "up to 1,000." Indian Country Today (search summary): about 500 from Natick at first, and "well over 1,000" by December as other praying towns were brought in. **Give the range, about 500 to 1,100, and say it is the Park Service's.**
- **How many died.** NPS: "Due to starvation, freezing temperatures and lack of shelter, very few survived internment." Historic Bostons: "More than half the people interned on Deer Island died over the winter." A search summary gives about 500 dead. **Nobody counted. Say "more than half, by one historical society's estimate," or quote the Park Service.** The island's owner, Daniel Henchman of Boston, forbade cutting trees, hunting and lighting fires on the island (search summary of the Historic Bostons page, **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**).
- **Release and sale.** "Colonists released survivors in May 1676. An unknown number of victims were smuggled off the island and sold as enslaved people in the West Indies or Tangier." (NPS, as above.)
- **What people did.** "Every year, descendants of the Tribal Nations involved in King Philip's War host a commemoration of their ancestors' suffering on Deer Island." (NPS, as above.)

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-261a): which named men voted the Deer Island order, and whether Monequassun, Natick's schoolmaster, was among those interned
Sources checked: NPS "Deer Island" (says "colonists" and "the English Council," no names); Partnership of Historic Bostons (says "the Massachusetts authorities"); Natick Historical Society (names Monequassun as schoolmaster, says nothing of 1675 except that Job Nesuton was killed in the war); search summaries of Indian Country Today and the Nipmuc Nation history page (no names of officials, no roll of internees).
How the prose can say it: "The records reached here do not name the men who signed the order. They also do not say what became of Monequassun, the Massachusett man who taught Natick's school."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): the colleges and slavery, with counts

**Harvard.** The Presidential Committee on Harvard and the Legacy of Slavery, chaired by Tomiko Brown-Nagin, released its report on **26 April 2022**. It found records of "at least 79 people who were enslaved by Harvard presidents, overseers, and faculty and staff members before the practice was outlawed in Massachusetts in 1783." Named examples: Jane, enslaved by the Harvard steward Andrew Bordman, who "owned at least eight people"; Cicely, enslaved by William Brattle, a tutor, fellow and treasurer; and Titus, Venus, Juba and Bilhah, who lived and worked at Wadsworth House for two Harvard presidents. (Source: *Harvard Magazine*, "Harvard's Slave Legacy," April 2022, https://www.harvardmagazine.com/2022/04/harvard-legacy-of-slavery-report .) Harvard's own initiative and the press summarized the count as "more than 70" (search summaries of legacyofslavery.harvard.edu and *The Washington Post*, 26 April 2022). **Use "at least 79," from the report as quoted by Harvard Magazine.** The span is 1636 to 1783, so this runs across eras 3, 4 and 5. Which two presidents used Titus, Venus, Juba and Bilhah is not named in the article read. Do not guess.

**William & Mary, founded 1693.** Its royal charter of 1693 set three purposes, the third being to teach Native students "in the Christian Religion." Rents from Brafferton Manor in Yorkshire, bought in 1695 with £5,400 from the estate of the scientist Robert Boyle, paid for the Indian school. (Source: William & Mary, Historic Campus, "The Early Decades of the Brafferton Indian School," https://www.wm.edu/sites/historiccampus/brafferton/indianschool/ .) What the Indian school did with captive children is in the 1700 to 1750 patch below. In **1718** the college bought the Nottoway Quarter, a tobacco plantation of 2,119 acres, for £150, and "for an additional £476 the college purchased seventeen enslaved people" to work it. Enslaved workers built the Brafferton building (1723). The college's Lemon Project is named for Lemon, an enslaved man who worked at the college from December 1779 until his death in 1817. (Source: Terry L. Meyers, "Slavery at the College of William & Mary," Encyclopedia Virginia, https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slavery-at-the-college-of-william-and-mary/ .) **Era placement: charter 1693 here, Nottoway 1718 in 1700-1750, Lemon 1779 in 1750-1800.**

---

## 4. 1700 to 1750

**Era claim: most children still learned at home or on the job. The schools that existed mostly took boys whose families could pay, and the biggest new thing in the half-century is a law making it a crime to teach an enslaved person to write.**

### 4a. The dame school and the hornbook — where a small child actually started

- **What a dame school was.** A woman ran a small school in her own house, taking children from about age three or four for a few hours a day, for a small fee. She taught letters, reading, the catechism and the Lord's Prayer; girls were also taught sewing. The teacher was often a widow or an unmarried woman who needed the income, and she was often paid in goods and work rather than cash — eggs, firewood, washing and ironing. (Source: Encyclopedia.com, "Dame School," drawing on the *Dictionary of American History*, https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/dictionaries-thesauruses-pictures-and-press-releases/dame-school ; Sage, *Encyclopedia of Educational Reform and Dissent*, "Dame Schools," https://sk.sagepub.com/ency/edvol/educationalreform/chpt/dame-schools .)
- **The dame school was the gate to the town school, and the gate had a rule.** In New England the town schools would not take a boy until he could "stand up and read words of two syllables and keep their places." Somebody had to teach him that first, and it was usually a woman in her kitchen. (Source: Encyclopedia.com, "Dame School," as above.) **This matters for the teaching-as-a-job thread: women were already teaching in this era, unpaid or barely paid, before teaching was a job a woman could hold in a town school.**
- **The hornbook — the first "book" most children touched.** It was not a book. It was a wooden paddle about the shape of a table-tennis bat, with a single printed sheet tacked to it and a thin sheet of transparent animal horn laid over the top to keep the paper from wearing out. A leather thong through the handle let a child hang it from a belt so it would not be lost. The sheet carried a cross, then the alphabet in large and small letters, then the vowels and their combinations with consonants, then the Lord's Prayer. (Sources: Smithsonian National Museum of American History, object record "Eighteenth Century Horn Book," https://americanhistory.si.edu/collections/object/nmah_1320130 and https://www.si.edu/object/eighteenth-century-horn-book:nmah_1320130 ; Fraunces Tavern Museum, "The Role of Hornbooks in Early American Education," https://www.frauncestavernmuseum.org/the-role-of-hornbooks-in-early-american-education .)
  - Because of the cross at the top, the alphabet row was called the "Christ's Cross row," shortened to "crisscross row." (Source: Fraunces Tavern Museum, as above.)
  - **The Smithsonian holds a real one, and it is an 18th-century object — so this is a checkable thing, not a picture from a storybook.**

### 4b. Charity schools — who ran them (clears the outline's "[VERIFY] which societies ran them")

- **The Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts (SPG)** got its royal charter from King William III on **16 June 1701**. The Reverend Dr. Thomas Bray pushed it into being, with Bishop Henry Compton and others. Its stated job was to send priests and schoolteachers to the colonies. Across the 1700s it paid for **more than 300 clergy and schoolmasters** to go overseas, and more than 400 agents in all. (Sources: Encyclopedia.com, "Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts," https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/united-states-and-canada/us-history/society-propagation-gospel-foreign-parts ; USPG online exhibition, Bodleian Libraries, https://emlo-portal.bodleian.ox.ac.uk/exhibition/uspg/items/show/46 ; Library of Congress research guide, "Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts," https://guides.loc.gov/british-manuscripts/society-for-the-propagation-of-the-gospel .)
- **The Associates of Dr. Bray**, a separate group founded in **1724**, set up schools whose purpose was Christian teaching through reading the Bible. (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, "Literacy and Education of the Enslaved in Virginia," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slave-literacy-and-education-in-virginia/ .) Their best-documented school is the Williamsburg Bray School, which belongs in the next era — see §5e.
- **What "charity school" meant: the church paid, and the lesson was religion.** These were not town schools and not free schools in the later sense. A society in London paid a schoolmaster, and reading was taught so that the pupil could read the catechism and the Bible.

### 4c. The Charleston school of 1743 — a school for Black children run three years after the law banning it

- **In 1743 Commissary Alexander Garden opened a school in Charleston, South Carolina, for Black pupils.** It was put in the charge of **two young Black men named Harry and Andrew**, who had been trained as teachers at the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel's expense. It ran as an evening school for people who worked in the day, and as a training school for Black pupils who would go on to teach others. (Sources: C. E. Pierre, *The Work of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts among the Negroes in the Colonies*, in UNC's Documenting the American South, https://docsouth.unc.edu/church/pierre/pierre.html ; Carter G. Woodson, *The History of the Negro Church*, Documenting the American South, https://docsouth.unc.edu/church/woodson/woodson.html .)
- **How the Society got Harry and Andrew is stated differently by different sources and must not be smoothed over.** Woodson's *History of the Negro Church* says only that they were "two young men of color, who had been thoroughly instructed in the rudiments of education and in the doctrines of the church." Pierre says they "had been trained as teachers at the cost of the Society." Other summaries of the SPG's work state that the Society **bought** the two as slaves in order to train them as schoolmasters. **This pass did not reach the SPG's own records, so the chapter should say what is agreed — they were Black, they were trained at the Society's expense, and they ran the school — and say that whether the Society purchased them is stated in some accounts and not in others.**
- **It closed in 1763**, and Woodson gives the reasons: one of the two teachers had died, and the Society judged the other ineffective. (Source: Woodson, as above.) Some accounts give 1764. State 1763 with its source.
- **Read this against §4f.** South Carolina had made it a crime to teach an enslaved person to write in 1740. The school opened three years later and taught for twenty years.

### 4d. Literacy — what the numbers are and what they actually measure

**How historians count.** There is no colonial literacy survey. Historians count signatures on wills and deeds: a person who could write signed, and a person who could not made a mark. It is the best evidence there is and it is not proof — someone could be taught to read but never to write, which was common for girls, so this method undercounts readers. **Say the method in the chapter; a reader should know where a number comes from.** (Source: as summarized with the figures below.)

- **White New England men:** about **60 percent** could sign between 1650 and 1670; **85 percent** between 1758 and 1762; **90 percent** between 1787 and 1795. These are Kenneth Lockridge's counts in *Literacy in Colonial New England* (1974). (Source: figures as reported and discussed in Foundation for Economic Education, "Did Public Schools Really Improve American Literacy?", https://fee.org/articles/did-public-schools-really-improve-american-literacy/ , summarizing Lockridge.)
- **New England women, by the decade they were born:** **27 percent** for those born 1650–69, **41 percent** for 1670–89, **48 percent** for 1690–1709, then **76 percent** for 1710–29 and **81 percent** for 1730–49. (Source: as reported in the same literature summarizing Lockridge and his successors, https://fee.org/articles/did-public-schools-really-improve-american-literacy/ .)
- **Town and country differed:** in New England towns about 35 percent of women signed in 1660 and 55 percent by 1790; in the countryside 30 percent in 1660 and 45 percent by 1790. **These two sets of figures do not line up neatly with the birth-cohort figures above, because they count different groups in different ways. State the gap rather than averaging them.**
- **Regional gap:** literacy was higher in New England and the middle colonies than in the South, higher in towns than in the country, and higher among traders and shopkeepers than among farmers. Lockridge tied the rise in male literacy before 1775 partly to more town schools as places filled up. (Source: as above; also the History of Education Quarterly literature summarized at https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/history-of-education-quarterly/article/abs/schooling-of-girls-and-changing-community-values-in-massachusetts-towns-17501820/1CB373E6E8A8A84F1FF5E6B33AA912DD .)
- **Enslaved people in Virginia:** by 1775 **as many as 5 percent** may have been able to read. The check on that estimate is the runaway advertisements in the *Virginia Gazette*: of about 1,000 placed between 1736 and 1776, **55 — just over 5 percent — described the person as literate.** (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, "Literacy and Education of the Enslaved in Virginia," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slave-literacy-and-education-in-virginia/ .) **Note what the source of that number is: advertisements placed by enslavers trying to catch people who had run away.**

### 4e. Franklin's academy — the dates, and the boundary

- **Franklin published *Proposals Relating to the Education of Youth in Pensilvania* in October 1749.** He argued for teaching in English rather than Latin and Greek, and for subjects like natural history, geography, and modern languages. (Sources: University of Pennsylvania Archives, "Penn in the 18th Century," https://archives.upenn.edu/exhibits/penn-history/18th-century/ ; the digitized *Proposals*, https://archives.upenn.edu/digitized-resources/docs-pubs/franklin-proposals/ ; National Humanities Center reprint, https://nationalhumanitiescenter.org/pds/becomingamer/ideas/text4/franklinproposals.pdf .)
- **The Academy and Charity School of Philadelphia opened in 1751 and was chartered in 1753** by the proprietors Thomas and Richard Penn. The College of Philadelphia was chartered in 1755, and its first class graduated in 1757. (Source: University of Pennsylvania Archives, as above.)
- **Boundary rule (AGENT-BRIEF §2): the *Proposals* are 1749 and belong to this era; the academy opening is 1751 and belongs to 1750–1800.** The outline seed put the whole thing in 1700–1750. Split it.
- The English school's courses included English grammar, composition, handwriting, drawing, rhetoric, history, geography, astronomy, science, natural history, agriculture, accounting, and mechanics. (Source: the Academy and College of Philadelphia entry, https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/news-wires-white-papers-and-books/academy-and-college-philadelphia ; UPenn Department of Classical Studies, "The Latin School and the College of Philadelphia, 1751–1791," https://www.classics.upenn.edu/about/department-history/latin-school-and-college-philadelphia-1751-1791 .)

### 4f. The law that made teaching a crime — South Carolina, 10 May 1740

This is the hard subject of the era and the chapter leads on the schooling side of it (`slavery-freedom` leads on learning to read as resistance).

- **The act:** "An Act for the Better Ordering and Governing Negroes and Other Slaves in This Province," passed **10 May 1740** in South Carolina, after the Stono Rebellion of 1739.
- **Section XLV, in the law's own words:** "That all and every person and persons whatsoever, who shall hereinafter teach or cause any slave or slaves to be taught, to write, or shall use or employ any slave as a scribe in any manner of writing whatsoever, hereafter taught to write, every such person and persons, shall, for every such offense, forfeit the sum of one hundred pounds current money." (Source: text of the 1740 South Carolina slave code, No. 670, US History Scene, https://ushistoryscene.com/article/excerpts-south-carolina-slave-code-1740-no-670-1740/ ; passage date and context also at the Equal Justice Initiative calendar, https://calendar.eji.org/racial-injustice/may/10 , and the Zinn Education Project, https://www.zinnedproject.org/news/tdih/south-carolina-negro-act-passed/ .)
- **Be exact about what it banned: writing, not reading.** The 1740 act made it an offence to teach an enslaved person to write, or to use one as a scribe. It did not forbid teaching reading. The reason usually given is that a person who could write could forge a pass and travel. Reading-only bans come later and in other colonies and states. **Do not write "banned teaching enslaved people to read and write" for 1740 — that is a different, later law.**
- **The penalty fell on the teacher, not on the pupil:** £100 current money for each offence, paid by the person who taught.
- **The first such law in these colonies is this one.** Nothing in the 1600s made teaching an enslaved person a crime (§3g).

### 4g. Girls' schooling begins as an institution — Bethlehem, 1742

- **In May 1742 Countess Benigna von Zinzendorf organized classes for twenty-five girls at Germantown, near Philadelphia.** The school moved to Bethlehem, Pennsylvania in 1745 and settled there in 1748, mainly boarding the daughters of travelling Moravian church workers. It is generally called the first Protestant boarding school for girls in what became the United States. It later became the Moravian Female Seminary, and in 1863 Moravian College. (Sources: Moravian University Magazine, "Moravian Church Settlements," https://magazine.moravian.edu/moravian-church-settlements/ ; Moravian Archives finding aid for the Bethlehem Female Seminary records, http://bdhp.moravian.edu/education/mafindingaid/BethlehemFemSem.pdf ; Moravian Academy, "Our History," https://www.moravianacademy.org/about-us/our-history .)
- **Keep the claim narrow.** "First Protestant boarding school for girls" is the claim the Moravian institutions make for it. It is not a claim that no girl was schooled before 1742 — girls were being taught in dame schools all along, and Catholic Ursuline nuns were teaching girls in New Orleans from 1727 (not verified in this pass; leave it out unless a later agent sources it).

### 4h. The one-room school day this early — what is and is not documented

The outline seed carried "[VERIFY how well documented this is for this early]". **Honest answer: the detailed one-room-schoolhouse picture — recitation benches, the stove, slates, boarding round, the two-term calendar — is documented for the 1800s, not for 1700–1750.** The material already parked in this bank from `home-family` on the two-term rural calendar and boarding round is sourced to descriptions of district schools "before ~1900" and to 1890s accounts, not to the early 1700s. What is documented for this era is thinner and should be told thinner: a town appointed one man to teach, the parents or the master paid his wages under the 1647 law and its imitators, and pupils of different ages sat together because there was only one teacher. **Do not move 1800s classroom detail back into this cell.** That is the exact way an invented fact enters.

### 4i. The named schoolmaster for this era: Christopher Dock (in Pennsylvania by 1710–14; died 1771)

He is the right story for this era because he is the one colonial teacher who wrote down what he actually did in the room, and because what he wrote is largely about how he punished children.

- He came to Pennsylvania between 1710 and 1714 and opened a school at Skippack **not later than 1718**. He taught ten years, farmed from about 1728 to 1738, then went back to teaching in 1738 and taught for the remaining 33 years of his life, running two schools — Skippack and Salford — on alternate days. (Sources: Martin G. Brumbaugh, *The Life and Works of Christopher Dock* (1908), full text at Wikisource, https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Life_and_Works_of_Christopher_Dock/2_Life_of_Christopher_Dock ; Global Anabaptist Mennonite Encyclopedia Online, "Dock, Christopher (d. 1771)," https://gameo.org/index.php?title=Dock,_Christopher_(d._1771) .)
- **He finished his *Schul-Ordnung* ("School Management") on 8 August 1750.** He did not want it printed in his lifetime. Christopher Saur printed it anyway in **1769**, with a second edition in **1770**. **Sources disagree on the first printing: Brumbaugh gives 1769 and 1770; other reference works give 1770 for the first edition.** State the disagreement. It went on being reprinted in German as late as 1861 and in English into the 1900s. (Sources: Brumbaugh, as above; the Christopher Dock entry summarized from Britannica in search results — Britannica itself returned HTTP 403 to this pass and was not read directly.)
- **It is the first book about how to teach written in this country.** (Sources: Brumbaugh, as above; Christianity.com, "America's 1st Book on Teaching Method," https://www.christianity.com/church/church-history/timeline/1701-1800/americas-1st-book-on-teaching-method-11630282.html .)
- **What he did with children, in Brumbaugh's summary of Dock's own text.** He sorted pupils by what moved them. A child who wanted praise, he praised. A child who wanted money, he "gave a penny when they studied diligently." A child who wanted sweets, he "gave a sugar pretzel, when they learned well." And: **"The ill-intentioned, who were not affected by the promise of a penny or a cooky, he threatened with whipping."** (Source: Brumbaugh, as above, quoting and paraphrasing Dock.)
  - **Do not turn Dock into the kind teacher who never hit anyone.** He is milder than the schoolmasters around him and he still used a whipping and the threat of one. Other punishments recorded of him include putting a yoke on a child who swore, and making a child sit alone. Say both halves.
- **His death.** One evening in the autumn of 1771 he did not come home from school at the usual time. In Brumbaugh's words: "A search was made and he was found in his schoolroom on his knees — dead." (Source: Brumbaugh, as above.)
- **Movie:** none verified. Do not add a Movie line.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): the Brafferton Indian school at William & Mary, and the captive boys who were its first pupils

The outline's era has no Native schooling at all between Harvard's Indian College (1600s) and Dartmouth (1769). The Brafferton school is the largest documented case, and its first pupils were bought as captives. **Hard subject: actor, act, count.**

- **The first pupils were captives, bought.** Governor Francis Nicholson tried to recruit Native pupils in 1700. When that failed, the first students, about **1702**, were "procured by Indian traders, who purchased or acquired four young Indian captives from the Catawba Nation." The traders were **Robert Hicks (or Hix) and John Evans**, who "secured the four captives alongside the deerskins and fur pelts." The four boys were "likely destined to be sold by the Catawba as forced laborers." Their names are not given. (Source: William & Mary, Historic Campus, "The Early Decades of the Brafferton Indian School," https://www.wm.edu/sites/historiccampus/brafferton/indianschool/ .) The Meherrin Indian Nation's account puts it this way: "William & Mary resorted to buying their pupils from local Native Americans who captured the boys from other tribes." (Source: Meherrin Indian Nation, "William and Mary Indian School," https://meherrinnation.org/history-culture/history/william-and-mary-indian-school/ .) **The W&M page's wording about the Catawba is itself careful: "captives from the Catawba Nation" can be read as captives held by the Catawba. Keep the quotation rather than rewording which nation the boys came from.**
- **Numbers.** By 1712 the college had "twenty tributary students, plus the four wards," which was one third to one half of the 40 to 80 pupils in the whole college. (If "tributary" is used, define it. The W&M page does not define it. It is the colonial term for Native nations bound to the Virginia colony by treaty, which is a working gloss, not a sourced definition.) (W&M Historic Campus, as above.) Across the school's life, the Brafferton building (finished **1723**) housed "more than 125 Native students from at least 26 different tribes between 1723 and 1776." (Source: William & Mary Libraries, "Remembering: William & Mary's Brafferton Indian School 1723-2023," https://libraries.wm.edu/exhibits/remembering-william-marys-brafferton-indian-school-1723-2023 .)
- **What was taught:** "reading and writing, arithmetic, literature, and mechanical arts," with the Book of Common Prayer and the catechism. (W&M Historic Campus, as above.)
- **Enslaved people built the Brafferton** (Encyclopedia Virginia, "Slavery at the College of William & Mary," in the 1600s patch above).
- **Deaths.** A search summary says the Native students "frequently died while enrolled" **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. No count was found. See SEARCHED, NOT FOUND below.
- **Close of the story.** The Meherrin Nation's account says the school "failed to convert many pupils to Christianity" and that students who learned English in Williamsburg could later "assist their tribes in defending their way of life against the English colonists." (Meherrin, as above.) That is the documented close. When and why the school ended is not stated in the pages read (the Revolution cut off the Boyle rents, according to general summaries not read here).

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-261a): how many Brafferton pupils died at the school, and the names of the four captive boys of about 1702
Sources checked: W&M Historic Campus (names the traders, not the boys, gives no deaths); W&M Libraries exhibit (125 students, 26 tribes, no deaths); Meherrin Nation (no count); Colonial Williamsburg's Brafferton page (HTTP 403).
How the prose can say it: "The college's records, as published, do not give the boys' names or say how many of the school's pupils died there."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): Harry and Andrew were bought. Woodson says so plainly.

This settles part of the dispute in §4c above.
- Carter G. Woodson, *The Education of the Negro Prior to 1861* (1915): the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel "hit upon the plan of purchasing two Negroes named Harry and Andrew, and of qualifying them by thorough instruction in the principles of Christianity and the fundamentals of education, to serve as schoolmasters to their people." Under Alexander Garden, "a building costing about three hundred and eight pounds was erected in Charleston," and "in the school which opened in this building in 1744 Harry and Andrew served as teachers." It began with "about sixty young students." Woodson adds that it is "highly probable that after 1740 this school was attended only by free persons of color," because of the 1740 law. (Source: Project Gutenberg text, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/11089/pg11089.txt , read word for word 2026-09-27.)
- **So:** Woodson's 1915 book says they were purchased. His 1921 *History of the Negro Church* (quoted in §4c) does not mention it. Pierre says trained "at the cost of the Society." **The prose can now say "the Society bought two young Black men, Harry and Andrew, and trained them as teachers," naming Woodson as the source.**
- **Dates now disagree:** 1743 (Pierre, §4c) and 1744 for the opening of the schoolhouse (Woodson 1915). A search summary gives 1742 for the building. Closing: 1763 (Woodson 1921, §4c) or 1764 (search summary of the same Woodson 1915 text; the Gutenberg text was not searched for the closing year). **Say "opened in 1743 or 1744."**
- **Pupils:** about sixty at the start (Woodson 1915).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): the Ursuline school in New Orleans, 1727 (clears the unverified note in §4g)

- Nuns of the Order of Saint Ursula arrived in New Orleans from France in **1727** and founded a school for girls. The school today says it is "both the oldest, continuously-operating school for girls and the oldest Catholic school in the United States," and that the Ursulines taught the "first classes for female African-American slaves, free women of color and Native Americans." (Source: Ursuline Academy of New Orleans, "History & Heritage," https://www.uanola.org/about/history-heritage .) The number of nuns is given as twelve by the school and as eleven in a search summary of another page. **Use "a group of Ursuline nuns" or cite the school's twelve.**
- **Boundary and place:** New Orleans was a French colony in 1727 and became part of the United States in 1803. The Ursulines' "oldest" claims are the school's own. The claim fits beside the Moravian school's narrower claim (§4g): Bethlehem was the first **Protestant** boarding school for girls, the Ursulines were Catholic and earlier.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): land in this era's school stories

- **Germantown and Skippack** (the Moravian girls' school and Christopher Dock's schools) were in the homeland of the Lenape, which covered southeastern Pennsylvania, central and southern New Jersey and Delaware. William Penn bought large tracts from Lenape sachems in a series of deeds signed on 23 June 1683. The Perkiomen Creek, on which Skippack stands, takes its name from a Lenape word meaning "cranberry place." (Sources: Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, "Colonial Era," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/time-periods/colonial-philadelphia/ , and Preservation Alliance for Greater Philadelphia, "The Original People: The Lenape on the Poquessing," https://preservationalliance.com/the-original-people-the-lenape-on-the-poquessing/ , both **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.) The 1737 Walking Purchase, which took Lenape land north of Philadelphia, belongs to `native-nations`.

---

## 5. 1750 to 1800

**Era claim: men who had just made a new country argued that it needed schools, wrote plans for them, and mostly did not pay for them. The schools that actually opened in this half-century were private academies, and one charity school in Williamsburg for Black children whose stated purpose was to make them content to be enslaved.**

### 5a. Jefferson's plan for Virginia — what it said, and that it did not pass

- **The bill.** Thomas Jefferson drafted "A Bill for the More General Diffusion of Knowledge" and it was presented to the Virginia House of Delegates on **18 June 1779**. (Source: *The Papers of Thomas Jefferson*, vol. 2, doc. 79, Founders Online / National Archives, https://founders.archives.gov/documents/Jefferson/01-02-02-0132-0004-0079 .)
- **What it proposed, in Jefferson's own description in *Notes on the State of Virginia* (1784).** Divide "every county into small districts of five or six miles square, called hundreds, and in each of them to establish a school," with "every person in it entitled to send their children three years gratis, and as much longer as they please, paying for it." The district schools would teach "reading, writing, and arithmetic." Above them, grammar schools would teach "Greek, Latin, geography, and the higher branches of numerical arithmetic." (Source: Jefferson, *Notes on the State of Virginia*, Queries 14 and 19, in *The Founders' Constitution*, University of Chicago Press, https://press-pubs.uchicago.edu/founders/print_documents/v1ch18s16.html .)
- **How poor boys were to be picked, in his words.** Visitors were "to chuse the boy, of best genius in the school, of those whose parents are too poor to give them further education," so that "twenty of the best geniusses will be raked from the rubbish annually." (Source: as above.)
  - **Quote that line and do not soften it.** "Raked from the rubbish" is Jefferson's own phrase for the children of poor families. It is the plainest evidence in this era of what the men writing school plans thought of most people's children, and a reader should have it.
- **Girls are not in the plan.** The text of *Notes on the State of Virginia* Queries 14 and 19 contains no mention of girls; every reference is to boys. (Source: as above.) **State this as an absence in the document, which is what it is.**
- **Enslaved children are not in it either.** Jefferson's plan is for free children. (Same source; the bill is a plan for the free population.)
- **It did not pass.** The bill was put to the Virginia legislature repeatedly from 1778 onward and was not enacted; James Madison presented it again while Jefferson was in Paris. A much-changed version became law in **1796** as an "Act to Establish Public Schools," and it left the decision to each county's court, so most counties did nothing. (Sources: Founders Online editorial apparatus, as above; Monticello encyclopedia entry, "A Bill for the More General Diffusion of Knowledge," https://www.monticello.org/encyclopedia/bill-more-general-diffusion-knowledge — **note: monticello.org returned HTTP 403 to this pass and was not read directly; the 1796 act and the repeated failures are from the Founders Online record and the standard summaries. If a later agent can read Monticello directly, tighten this line.**)

### 5b. The Land Ordinance of 1785 — one square mile in every township, for schools

- **Passed by Congress on 20 May 1785.** Its text: "There shall be reserved the lot N 16, of every township, for the maintenance of public schools, within the said township; also one third part of all gold, silver, lead and copper mines, to be sold, or otherwise disposed of as Congress shall hereafter direct." (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, primary document, "Land Ordinance of 1785 (May 20, 1785)," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/primary-documents/land-ordinance-of-1785-may-20-1785/ ; also Indiana Historical Bureau, https://www.in.gov/history/about-indiana-history-and-trivia/explore-indiana-history-by-topic/indiana-documents-leading-to-statehood/land-ordinance-of-1785/ .)
- **What that meant on the ground.** Surveyors cut the western land into six-mile-square townships of 36 numbered sections. Section 16 sat near the middle. It was not to be sold to a settler; the money it made was to keep a school going. When a territory became a state, the state took title to those sections. (Sources: as above.)
- **Say what the land was.** This was land taken from Native nations and sold to settlers, and a share of the proceeds paid for the settlers' schools. `native-nations` leads on the taking; this chapter's slice is that the American school system's first money came out of it.

### 5c. The academies — the new kind of school between the town school and the college

- **Franklin's academy opened in 1751** as the Academy and Charity School of Philadelphia, and was chartered in 1753 by Thomas and Richard Penn. The College of Philadelphia was chartered in 1755 and its first class graduated in 1757. (Source: University of Pennsylvania Archives, "Penn in the 18th Century," https://archives.upenn.edu/exhibits/penn-history/18th-century/ .) **The 1749 *Proposals* belong to the previous era; see §4e.**
- **Phillips Academy, Andover, Massachusetts: founded 25 April 1778, opened 30 April 1778, incorporated 4 October 1780.** Samuel Phillips (1751–1802) started it. Paul Revere cut its seal and John Hancock signed the act of incorporation. (Source: *The Encyclopedia Americana* (1920), "Phillips (Andover) Academy," Wikisource, https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Encyclopedia_Americana_(1920)/Phillips_(Andover)_Academy ; Phillips Academy, "History," https://www.andover.edu/about/history .)
- **Phillips Exeter Academy, New Hampshire: act of incorporation dated 3 April 1781, signed by John and Elizabeth Phillips; the school opened on 1 May 1783 with one teacher and 56 students.** (Source: Phillips Exeter Academy, "History & Traditions," https://exeter.edu/history-traditions/ .)
  - **One teacher and 56 students is the useful, checkable number for a reader.** It shows what "a school" meant.

### 5d. Schools for girls — the first ones with charters and buildings

- **The Young Ladies' Academy of Philadelphia** was founded by John Poor on **4 June 1787** and chartered on **7 January 1792**, which makes it the first school for women's higher education in the United States to be recognized by a government. Its subjects were reading, writing, English grammar, mathematics, geography, rhetoric, composition, chemistry and natural philosophy. Benjamin Rush was among the men who sponsored and supervised it. (Sources: Historical Society of Pennsylvania catalogue record for the Academy's papers, https://opac.hsp.org/Record/marc-156932 ; Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, "Women's Education," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/essays/womens-education/ .)
- **The Litchfield Female Academy, Litchfield, Connecticut, founded 1792 by Sarah Pierce (born 1767).** Over about 30 years it took **more than 2,000 students from 17 states and territories, plus Canada and the West Indies**; the Litchfield Historical Society has identified 1,848 of them by name. **More than 80 percent came from out of town and boarded with Litchfield families under Pierce's supervision.** The course began with English, ancient and European history, geography, arithmetic and composition, and later added Latin, moral philosophy, logic, rhetoric and natural philosophy, with art, needlework, music and French taught by hired instructors. Girls who finished the full course were given diplomas. Pierce ended her connection with the school in 1833 after 41 years. (Source: Litchfield Historical Society, Litchfield Ledger, "Female Academy History," https://ledger.litchfieldhistoricalsociety.org/ledger/studies/history_lfa .)
- **The argument used to justify these schools.** Sarah Pierce took up the post-Revolution argument that women were responsible for the early moral and intellectual training of their children and that this mattered to the survival of the country. She believed the sexes were intellectually equal, and she did not argue for women entering the men's colleges or professions. (Source: Litchfield Historical Society, as above.) **Say both halves. The first argument for teaching American girls was an argument about their future sons.**

### 5e. The Williamsburg Bray School, 1760–1774 — a school for Black children, and what it was for

This is the era's hard subject and the chapter leads on it as schooling.

- **It ran from 1760 to 1774 in Williamsburg, Virginia.** The Associates of Dr. Bray, an Anglican charity in London, set it up **at Benjamin Franklin's recommendation**, with the backing of William & Mary's president, the Reverend Thomas Dawson, who was also rector of Bruton Parish Church. (Source: William & Mary, Bray School Lab, "Williamsburg Bray School Initiative," https://www.wm.edu/sites/brayschool/about/bray-initiative/ .)
- **One teacher for the whole life of the school: Ann Wager.** (Same source; Encyclopedia Virginia spells it Anne Wager.)
- **How many children.** William & Mary says Wager taught **between 300 and 400** enslaved and free Black children across the school's fourteen years. Encyclopedia Virginia says **about 200**, in classes of 20 to 30, split about evenly between girls and boys. **The two institutional sources disagree; give the range and say they disagree.** (Sources: W&M Bray School Lab, as above; Encyclopedia Virginia, "Literacy and Education of the Enslaved in Virginia," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slave-literacy-and-education-in-virginia/ .)
- **What was taught.** The children learned "the true Spelling of Words" and how to pronounce and "read distinctly." Lessons started with reciting and memorising the Apostles' Creed, the Lord's Prayer, and the catechism. Girls were also taught sewing. (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)
- **What it was for, in the institutions' own present-day words.** William & Mary states that the school's "faith-based curriculum justified slavery," and that "by teaching the practice of literacy, it may have also seeded agency." Encyclopedia Virginia and Colonial Williamsburg describe the purpose as teaching enslaved children to accept their enslavement as God's will. **Both halves are true and both go in: the school was built to teach Black children that slavery was right, and it taught them to read.** (Sources: W&M Bray School Lab, as above; Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)
- **The building still exists.** Colonial Williamsburg and William & Mary confirmed in **2020** that a building on the William & Mary campus was the schoolhouse. It had been a private home and then student housing. In **February 2023** it was moved to Colonial Williamsburg's Historic Area at 331 Francis Street West. It is described as the oldest surviving schoolhouse for Black children in the United States. (Sources: W&M Bray School Lab, as above; *Smithsonian* magazine, "Oldest Schoolhouse for Black Children Moves to Colonial Williamsburg," https://www.smithsonianmag.com/smart-news/18th-century-school-for-black-children-moves-to-new-permanent-location-180981668/ .)
- **A second Bray school, at Fredericksburg, Virginia, ran from 1765 to 1769 or 1770** under the merchant Fielding Lewis, with about 40 to 50 pupils. It closed because enrollment fell and local enslavers were hostile to it. (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)
- **Named children:** the descendant-research work at William & Mary is ongoing and this pass did not verify any individual pupil's name. **Do not name a Bray School child.** Tell the children unnamed, in `hb-zoom` prose (hard-subjects-policy §4), and name Ann Wager, who is documented.

### 5f. Noah Webster (1758–1843) — the story slot already marked verified, now sourced

**The outline carried Noah Webster as `status="verified"` with nothing in this bank behind him. This section is what backs that tag.**

- Born **16 October 1758** in the West Division of Hartford, Connecticut, now West Hartford. Died **28 May 1843** in New Haven. (Source: Connecticut History, a CT Humanities project, "Noah Webster and the Dream of a Common Language," https://connecticuthistory.org/noah-webster-and-the-dream-of-a-common-language/ .)
- **He was a schoolteacher first.** After Yale in 1778 he taught at schools in Glastonbury, Hartford and West Hartford, and in 1782 took a teaching post in Goshen, New York. (Source: as above.) **This is the documented example for the "teaching as a job" thread in this era — a college graduate teaching school in several towns in a few years, while heading for another career.**
- **The speller.** He published the first volume of *A Grammatical Institute of the English Language* in **1783**. It is the book people call the Blue-Backed Speller. He wrote it because he thought the new country needed its own schoolbooks and a settled way of spelling to hold it together. It "did not notably alter spellings but applied many reforms that had been inconsistent in previous dictionaries." (Source: as above.)
- **Sales: between 1783 and the early 1900s the speller is estimated to have sold nearly 100 million copies.** Other summaries give about 60 million over the first hundred years. **State it as an estimate and give the range; nobody counted.** (Sources: Connecticut History, as above, for the ~100 million estimate; the 60 million figure appears in general reference summaries and was not verified against a primary count in this pass.)
