- **His dictionary is the NEXT era and belongs to the eras 6–7 agent.** *An American Dictionary of the English Language*, 1828, 70,000 words, sold at $20 a set. (Source: Connecticut History, as above.) Parked below for that agent.
- **Movie:** none verified. Do not add a Movie line.

### 5g. Teaching as a job in this era — what is and is not documented

The outline seed carried "[VERIFY]" on "teaching is still mostly a man's job and often a short-term one, taken between other work, with pay and lodging arranged by the town."

**What this pass could source:** Webster's own path — Yale, then teaching in three or four towns in about four years, then law and writing — is a documented case of exactly that pattern (Connecticut History, as above). The 1647 Massachusetts law and its imitators put the schoolmaster's wages on the parents or the master rather than on a town budget (§3d). Ezekiel Cheever and Christopher Dock are documented career teachers, which shows the pattern was not universal.

**What this pass could NOT source:** a general figure for how many teachers were men, a typical wage, or the "boarding round" arrangement for 1750–1800. The boarding-round and two-term-calendar material already in this bank is sourced to descriptions of the 1800s. **Keep the general claim out of the outline and tell it through the documented people instead.** Writing "teachers were usually young men who taught for a year or two" without a source would be an invented fact.

### 5h. Ann Wager and the Bray School — the second sourcing pass, which changed several facts

This section corrects and extends §5e. **Where it disagrees with §5e, this section is later and better sourced.**

- **Ann Wager, about 1716 to 20 August 1774.** Her husband William Wager died in 1748, and she took up teaching afterwards to support herself. (Sources: Colonial Williamsburg, "Ann Wager," nation-builders biography, https://www.colonialwilliamsburg.org/explore/nation-builders/ann-wager/ — **this page returned HTTP 403 to this pass and was not read directly; the dates and the widowhood are from Colonial Williamsburg's own summary of that page as returned in search, and from Colonial Williamsburg's Bray School story on Google Arts & Culture, https://artsandculture.google.com/story/YAWxa1lqQQZepA .** A later agent with access should read the biography page and confirm the birth year, which is given as approximate.)
- **Her pay: £20 a year, with the rent on her housing also paid.** Colonial Williamsburg puts that at "roughly equivalent to a journeyman worker's yearly wage." (Source: Colonial Williamsburg, Bray School story, Google Arts & Culture, as above.)
- **The pupils were 3 to 10 years old, and "on an average school day, thirty or more students might crowd into this small building."** (Source: as above.) **This supersedes "classes of 20 to 30" in §5e, which came from Encyclopedia Virginia.**
- **The number of children is genuinely unsettled and all three figures should be reported as what they are.** Colonial Williamsburg says "hundreds of students attended." William & Mary says 300 to 400. Encyclopedia Virginia says about 200. **Do not average them and do not pick one.**
- **86 pupils are named in surviving records.** Colonial Williamsburg: "Surviving documentation provides the names of 86 students." (Source: as above.) **This pass did not obtain the list.** The chapter must therefore not name a pupil — but it should say that 86 names survive, because "no pupil is named" and "this book has not read the names" are different statements and only the second is true. A later agent should ask William & Mary's Bray School Lab descendant-research pages for the list; naming one real child from that list would be a genuine improvement to this chapter.
- **What the children read, in order:** *The Child's First Book*, then the Book of Psalms, the New Testament, the Book of Common Prayer, and the Bible. The school "aimed to introduce Black children to the tenets of the Church of England." Girls were also to be taught sewing. (Source: as above.)
- **The building.** The school ran in the building now identified as the schoolhouse "from 1760 until 1765, when it moved to a larger building." The structure was rediscovered in **2020** and moved to Colonial Williamsburg's Historic Area on **10 February 2023**. (Source: as above.) **This dates the move more precisely than §5e.**
- **The school closed on Wager's death in 1774.** Colonial Williamsburg adds that the break between Britain and the colonies was already threatening the school's funding by then. (Source: as above.)

### 5i. Caleb Cheeshahteaumuck's death — how firm the record is

Placed here because it belongs with the 1600s (§3e) but was confirmed in this later pass.

- He graduated in 1665 and died within about a year, in 1666, of what was then called consumption. **Martha's Vineyard Magazine states it as "presumably from tuberculosis"** — that is, the identification of the disease is an inference, not a diagnosis in the record. Other accounts give the place as Watertown, Massachusetts, and his age as about twenty. (Sources: Martha's Vineyard Magazine, "In Caleb's Footsteps," https://mvmagazine.com/news/2011/05/01/caleb%E2%80%99s-footsteps ; Harvard Gazette, "Native American honored," https://news.harvard.edu/gazette/story/2010/12/native-american-honored/ ; Harvard Magazine, "For Caleb Cheeshahteaumuck and Successors," https://www.harvardmagazine.com/sites/default/files/html/1997/07a/jhj.caleb.html .)
- **His home community:** Martha's Vineyard. Sources vary between Aquinnah and Holmes Hole. The Harvard University Native American Program says he was of the Wampanoag Tribe of Aquinnah; Martha's Vineyard Magazine says Aquinnah. **Say "a Wampanoag community on Martha's Vineyard" unless a later agent settles it.**
- **His one surviving piece of writing** is a short Latin address to the English donors who paid for the Indian College, opening "Honoratissimi benefactores" — most honoured benefactors. It is commonly described as the earliest surviving writing by a Native person on this continent. (Source: the scholarly treatment indexed at ERIC, "'Honoratissimi Benefactores': Native American Students and Two Seventeenth-Century Texts in the University Tradition," *Studies in American Indian Literatures*, 1992, https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ449095 . **The letter's text itself was not read in this pass; do not quote beyond the opening words.**)
- **Geraldine Brooks's novel *Caleb's Crossing* (2011) is fiction.** Martha's Vineyard Magazine says plainly that very little personal documentation about Caleb exists and that "we will probably never actually know or understand what Caleb...went through or felt like." **Nothing from the novel goes into this chapter.** No film about him was verified; add no Movie line.

### 5j. A note on the Boston Latin town-record date, for whoever writes the prose

The town record quoted in §3a is dated "the 13th of the second month, 1635." **That is April, not February.** The English year then began on 25 March, so March was the first month and April the second. If the quote is used, the sentence after it has to say so, or an 8-to-15-year-old reader will take away the wrong month.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): a NAMED Bray School pupil, Isaac Bee, and what he did with his reading

The Ann Wager story says 86 pupils are named in the records and that this book has not read the names. **They are now read.** This lets a writer name one child.

- **The roster.** William & Mary's Bray School Lab publishes the names from "the only known reports of students at school for the Williamsburg Bray School known to exist as of May 2024": three lists, for September 1762, November 1765 and February 1769. "Over 80 scholars have been identified." The November 1765 list includes **Isaac Bee** and Johanna Bee. The September 1762 list includes Locust, Mary Jones and Elisha Jones, among others. (Source: William & Mary, Bray School Lab, "Williamsburg Bray School Scholar Names," https://www.wm.edu/sites/brayschool/research/scholar-names/ .) Colonial Williamsburg's count is 86 (§5h). **Say "more than 80 names," or give W&M's and CW's figures side by side.**
- **Isaac Bee ran away and his enslaver warned that he could read.** Lewis Burwell of Mecklenburg placed an advertisement dated **3 September 1774** (printed in the *Virginia Gazette*, 8 September 1774): "RUN away from the Subscriber, about two Months ago, a likely Mulatto Lad named ISAAC BEE, formerly the Property of the late President Blair." He was "between eighteen and nineteen Years of Age, low of Stature," and believed he had a right to freedom because his father was a free man. Burwell wrote that he could read, "but I do not know that he can write; however, he may easily get some One to forge a Pass for him." Burwell offered a reward of 40 shillings for his return to him or Mrs. Burwell in Williamsburg, and warned ship captains not to carry him. (Source: Encyclopedia Virginia, primary document, "Advertisements, Virginia Gazette (September 8, 1774)," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/primary-documents/advertisements-virginia-gazette-september-8-1774/ .)
- **The link between the pupil and the runaway** is made by the researchers, not by the advertisement: "Isaac Bee, a Bray School student, would run away as an adult from a slave owner named Lewis Burwell." (Source: Associated Press, as printed by NBC News, "Oldest U.S. schoolhouse for Black children moved to Virginia museum," February 2023, https://www.nbcnews.com/news/us-news/oldest-us-schoolhouse-black-children-moved-museum-rcna70255 .) The ages fit: 18 or 19 in 1774 makes him about 9 or 10 on the November 1765 list. **Attribute the identification to the Bray School researchers.**
- **What is not known:** whether he was caught, and what became of him. A search summary says the Bray School Lab has traced "his immediate whereabouts and potential descendants" **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. Do not state an outcome.
- **"President Blair"** is John Blair, president of the Virginia Council (acting governor), who died in 1771. That identification is general knowledge and was not checked in this pass. **Write "a Williamsburg official named John Blair" only after a check. The safe form is the advertisement's own words.**
- **Why this matters to the chapter:** the school was built to teach enslaved children to accept slavery. One of its pupils, as a teenager, used his reading to run, and the man who claimed to own him put that reading in the advertisement as a danger. That is the documented close for the Bray School (hard-subjects-policy §3 point 5). It is a fact, not a moral, and the prose should state it and stop.
- **A second named child:** the Armistead family, who enslaved people near Williamsburg, "is known to have sent at least one child, named Locust, to the Bray School." (NBC News / AP, as above.) Locust appears on the September 1762 list.
- **Other chapters:** no outline tells Isaac Bee (grep, 2026-09-27). Suggested slug if used: `isaac-bee`.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): Georgia copied South Carolina's ban, 1755 and 1770

The outline's 1750-1800 era does not carry any anti-literacy law, and its 1800-1850 era begins Georgia's with 1829. Georgia's colonial laws come first.
- **Georgia's first slave code, 1755,** made it a crime to teach enslaved people to write or to employ them as scribes, following South Carolina's 1740 act. A 1770 act re-enacted the rules. (Source: Georgia Archives, "Creating A More Educated Georgia: Slave Laws of Georgia, 1755-1860," presentation, https://www.georgiaarchives.org/assets/documents/Slave_Laws_of_Georgia_1755-1860.pdf , read as text: "Teaching slaves to write or employing them as scribes. Teaching slaves to read outlawed 1770, re-enacted 1829 along with free persons of color.")
- **The sources disagree on reading.** The Georgia Archives slide says teaching reading was outlawed in **1770**. A search summary of other pages says the 1770 act banned teaching slaves to read or using literate slaves to write, fine **£20**, and another says Georgia "did not prohibit the teaching of reading until 1829" **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. **State both. The safe sentence: "Georgia's lawmakers banned teaching enslaved people to write in 1755. The records disagree on whether their 1770 law also banned teaching them to read."**
- **The men who passed it:** the members of Georgia's colonial House of Assembly (Georgia Archives, as above, "In 1755 the House of Assembly voted in its initial slave codes," from search summary of the same source family). Individual members: not sought.
- **Owner:** `slavery-freedom` leads on anti-literacy laws (registry ruling 7). This chapter's slice is the schooling fact, one sentence in 1750-1800.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): whose land Section 16 was (the Land Ordinance of 1785)

- **The first land surveyed under the Ordinance** was the Seven Ranges in what is now eastern Ohio. Before it, "U.S. commissioners met representatives of several Indian tribes at Ft. McIntosh in 1784-85 and concluded a treaty that called for restricting most Ohio Indians in a reserve between the Cuyahoga and Maumee rivers." (Source: George W. Knepper, *The Official Ohio Lands Book*, Ohio Auditor of State, https://ohioauditor.gov/publications/docs/OhioLandsBook.pdf , p. 3-4, read as text.) The treaty was signed on **21 January 1785** with Wyandot, Delaware (Lenape), Chippewa (Ojibwe) and Ottawa (Odawa) representatives. (Source: search summary of the Beaver Area Heritage Foundation, "Treaty of Fort McIntosh," https://beaverheritage.org/treaty-of-fort-mcintosh/ , **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**, which also says they attended "surrounded by militia.")
- **The Ohio nations rejected it.** Knepper: "The Ohio Indians, especially the Shawnee, refused to abide by a treaty negotiated under duress by minor tribal chiefs who lacked authority to speak for their people and for other tribes." (Same source, p. 4.) The surveyors who began the Seven Ranges in 1785 worked from Fort McIntosh under the guard of its troops (search summary, unconfirmed).
- **So the prose can say:** the school sections came out of land that Wyandot, Lenape, Ojibwe, Odawa and Shawnee people lived on, and that US commissioners took by a treaty the Shawnee and others refused to accept.
- **How much land schools got, later:** "By 1920, 73,155,075 acres of public land had been given by the federal government to the public land states in support of public schooling." Ohio's share was 704,204 acres. (Knepper, as above, p. 56-57.) **That figure belongs to later eras. Parked forward for the eras 6-8 agents.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): Moor's Indian Charity School and Dartmouth (1754-1769), missing from the outline

This is the era's largest Native school and it is not in the outline. Samson Occom (Mohegan), who raised its money, is one of the best-known Native people of the century.
- **The school.** Eleazar Wheelock opened it in **1754** in North Lebanon, Connecticut, named for Colonel Joshua Moor (or More), who gave the land and building. It took girls from **1761**. Current and former students complained that Wheelock subjected Native students to "disproportionate amounts of manual labor." Wheelock "fought with many of the best" students: Samson Occom, Joseph Johnson, David Fowler and Hezekiah Calvin. **"In 1769, perhaps due to concerns about corporal punishment, the Oneida withdrew all their children from Moor's."** When Wheelock moved to Hanover, New Hampshire, in 1769, "only two Native American students came with him." (Source: The Occom Circle, Dartmouth College, "Moor's Indian Charity School," https://collections.dartmouth.edu/occom/html/occom/ctx/orgography/org0098.ocp.html .) **Note the word "perhaps": the Oneida reason is the editors' inference. Keep it.**
- **The money.** Occom's preaching tour of England and Scotland, **1766 to 1768**, raised **£12,026**. Wheelock "had instead used the funds that Occom raised to found a college in the Province of New Hampshire." "Occom never set foot on the campus of the college that he had unknowingly raised the money to found." (Source: Dartmouth Libraries, Dartmouth & Slavery Project, "Wheelock, Occom, & Moor's Indian Charity School," https://www.library.dartmouth.edu/slavery-project/wheelock-occom-moors-charity-school .)
- **The charter.** John Wentworth, royal governor of New Hampshire, signed Dartmouth's charter in 1769 (13 December 1769, search summary). Its stated purpose: "the education and instruction of youth of the Indian tribes in this land [in] all parts of learning which shall appear necessary and expedient for civilizing and christianizing children of pagans." "Only around 75 Native students enrolled at the College before 1972." (Source: The Occom Circle, "Dartmouth College," https://collections.dartmouth.edu/archive/text/occom/ctx/orgography/org0037.ocp.html .)
- **Wheelock enslaved people.** Dartmouth's own project: documents account for **17** people he enslaved, and "it is likely he enslaved more people than these documents reveal." Between 1743 and 1769 his papers record the purchase of eight (six men, two women). Names include Exeter, Chloe, Hercules, Peggy and Caesar, who "resided in the Dartmouth College kitchen in 1773." (Source: Dartmouth Libraries, "People Enslaved by Eleazar Wheelock," https://www.library.dartmouth.edu/slavery-project/people-enslaved-by-wheelock .)
- **Other chapters:** no outline in the book mentions Occom, Wheelock, Moor's school or the Brafferton (grep of `outlines/`, 2026-09-27). Suggested slug if used: `samson-occom-education`.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261a): Unit 2 check of the era 1-5 story blocks against the bank

- **Paquiquineo, Cheever, Cheeshahteaumuck, Dock, Ann Wager:** every fact in the outline blocks is in the bank with a source. Two notes. (1) Ann Wager's widowhood and birth year rest on Colonial Williamsburg's page, read only through a search summary and Google Arts & Culture (§5h). Still true. (2) Paquiquineo's block should gain the 1572 hangings (era 2 patch) so the story does not end on the Jesuits' deaths alone.
- **Noah Webster:** the outline block says estimates run "from about 60 million over its first hundred years to nearly 100 million." The bank (§5f) says the 60 million figure "was not verified against a primary count." **Only the ~100 million estimate (Connecticut History) is sourced. The writer should drop "60 million" or re-verify it.** This pass could not search it (search quota reached).
- **Christopher Dock:** the yoke for a boy who swore, and making children sit alone, appear in bank §4i without a separate citation, inside the Brumbaugh bullet list. Treat them as Brumbaugh (1908).

# PART A-FWD — parked forward inside this chapter, for the eras 6–10 agents

Found while researching eras 3–5. Each item belongs to a later era of this same chapter, so it was not written into eras 3–5. It is sourced; use it or replace it, but do not re-research it from scratch.

## For 1800–1850

- **Noah Webster's dictionary.** *An American Dictionary of the English Language*, 1828, **70,000 words, definitions and word origins**, sold at **$20 a set**. Webster was born 16 October 1758 and died 28 May 1843; he is also called the father of American copyright law and went to Washington in 1830 to lobby Congress for stronger federal copyright. (Source: Connecticut History, a CT Humanities project, "Noah Webster and the Dream of a Common Language," https://connecticuthistory.org/noah-webster-and-the-dream-of-a-common-language/ .) His `hb-story` block is in 1750–1800 with the speller; the dictionary line points forward to this era.
- **Virginia's anti-literacy laws, with dates and wording, from Encyclopedia Virginia** (https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slave-literacy-and-education-in-virginia/ ):
  - **24 January 1804** — a law barring all enslaved people from gathering at night, at churches, meetinghouses or anywhere else.
  - **2 March 1819** — enslaved people banned from "any school or schools for teaching them reading or writing, either in the day or night."
  - **7 April 1831** — "All meetings of free negroes or mulattoes, at any school house...for teaching them reading or writing...shall be considered as an unlawful assembly."
  - **Note the sequence for the chapter:** South Carolina banned teaching *writing* in 1740 (era 4, §4f); Virginia banned the *schools themselves*, for both reading and writing, in 1819, and extended it to free Black people in 1831. The bans got wider over ninety years. Say that plainly rather than treating "anti-literacy laws" as one undated thing.
- **Enslaved literacy rose anyway:** Encyclopedia Virginia gives about 5 percent literate among enslaved people in Virginia by 1775, and says the share "doubled to 10 percent" in the antebellum period. (Same source.)

## For 1850–1900 and later

- **The Bray School's 86 named pupils.** Colonial Williamsburg states that surviving documentation gives the names of 86 Bray School students. William & Mary's Bray School Lab runs a descendant-research programme (https://www.wm.edu/sites/brayschool/ ). **Getting that list and naming one real child would let this chapter carry a named Black pupil from 1760 instead of only a named teacher.** It is the single highest-value follow-up left in eras 1–5.

## Boundary notes the later agents should not have to rediscover

- **Franklin's academy is split across two eras on purpose:** the *Proposals* are October 1749 (era 4); the school opened 1751 and was chartered 1753 (era 5). Do not merge them.
- **Cheever's *Accidence* was printed in 1708**, the year he died, though he had taught from it for decades. Do not write "he published it in the 1600s."
- **Boston Latin's town-meeting record is dated "the 13th of the second month, 1635," which is April.** See §5j.

---

# PART C — Eras 6-7, researched (1800-1850 and 1850-1900)

*Written by the eras 6-7 research agent, 2026-09-08. Every fact below carries its source. Where sources disagree, the disagreement is stated and NOT resolved silently.*

**Access note for later agents:** `loc.gov` item and resource pages now sit behind a Cloudflare challenge and return **HTTP 403** to both WebFetch and curl, the same as `britannica.com`. Do not spend calls on them. `hmdb.org` and `riamco.org` also 403. Working substitutes used below: nps.gov, si.edu, archives.gov, state historical societies, university digital collections, and Dickinson College's Carlisle Indian School Digital Resource Center.

---

## 6. 1800 to 1850

### 6a. Horace Mann — the story slot that was tagged `verified` with nothing behind it, now sourced

**The defect found:** the outline carried `hb-story` slug `horace-mann` at `status="verified"` with a single unsourced line of text and no bank entry at all. It is sourced here.

- **Born 4 May 1796 in Franklin, Massachusetts**, into a poor farming family; his father died when Mann was thirteen. As a boy he braided straw for the hat-makers of Franklin to earn money. **Up to about the age of fifteen he was never in school more than about ten weeks in any one year** — another retelling of his own recollection puts it at no more than six weeks a year between ten and twenty. The two figures come from different retellings and are given here as a range, not as one settled number. He studied Latin and Greek with an itinerant teacher named Barrett, entered Brown University, and **graduated valedictorian in 1819**. (Sources: Liber Brunoniana, Brown University historical article series, https://liber-brunoniana.github.io/Articles/Mann,%20Horace.html ; Ohio History Central, Ohio History Connection, "Horace Mann.")
- **Career before schools:** lawyer; Massachusetts House of Representatives from 1827; Massachusetts Senate from 1835, and its president.
- **The Massachusetts legislature created a state Board of Education in 1837, and Mann was chosen as its first secretary.** He held the post **1837 to 1848**. The board could not compel a town to do anything; the secretary's job was to collect information, write reports, and argue. (Sources: Ohio History Central; EBSCO Research Starters, "Horace Mann"; Framingham State University history.)
- **The twelve annual reports.** Mann wrote **twelve annual reports between 1837 and 1848**. They argue three things over and over: that every child has a right to an education; that schools should be paid for by taxes on everybody rather than by fees on parents; and that teachers need training before they teach. He also founded and edited **the *Common School Journal*, from 1838**. (Sources: EBSCO Research Starters, "Horace Mann"; *Lectures and Annual Reports on Education*, Horace Mann, 2 vols., Cambridge, 1867, https://archive.org/details/lecturesannualre01manniala — the volume's own contents list includes "school discipline and punishments," "special preparation as a prerequisite to teaching," and reports on schoolhouse construction.)
- **The Seventh Annual Report, 1843, and the fight it started.** Mann toured schools in Prussia, Saxony, and Holland in 1843 and wrote the tour up in his seventh report. Among other things he argued for teaching reading by whole words rather than letter by letter, for instruction for blind and deaf children, and **against corporal punishment as the ordinary way of running a classroom**. **Thirty-one Boston schoolmasters answered him in print in 1844** with *Remarks on the Seventh Annual Report of the Hon. Horace Mann*, published by the Association of Masters of the Boston Public Schools; they attacked both the teacher-training proposal and the attack on beating. Mann answered with *Reply to the "Remarks" of Thirty-one Boston Schoolmasters* (1844), and they answered that with a *Rejoinder* (1845). All three pamphlets are real and catalogued at the Library of Congress (control numbers 06017777 and 06041590; the LoC pages themselves return 403 from here, and the titles were confirmed from rare-book catalogue records quoting their full title pages, plus the EBSCO and Appletons' Cyclopaedia accounts of the controversy). **This is the first time a public argument about hitting children in school is carried on in print in America with both sides named.**
- **After 1848:** elected to the U.S. House of Representatives, taking the seat left by John Quincy Adams's death; served **1848 to 1853**; spoke against slavery and its extension. **Became the first president of Antioch College, Yellow Springs, Ohio, in 1853.** **Died in 1859.** (Source: Ohio History Central, Ohio History Connection.)
- **What Mann's common school was not.** The free tax-paid school he argued for was in practice a school for white children. Boston ran separate schools for Black children while Mann was secretary, and the state's highest court upheld them two years after he left the board — see §6b. Both belong in the same passage; the record supports both.

### 6b. Sarah Roberts — a five-year-old shut out of the school nearest her house, and the case that named the rule

A **named, documented Black child in a school case in this era**, and the honest counterweight to the common-school story. It also matters later: the reasoning blessed here is what the U.S. Supreme Court leaned on in *Plessy* in 1896.

- **Sarah Roberts was five years old and lived on Andover Street in Boston.** To reach the Abiel Smith School, the school for Black children, **she had to walk past five schools for white children.** (Source: National Park Service, "The Sarah Roberts Case," https://www.nps.gov/articles/the-sarah-roberts-case.htm .)
- **Her father, Benjamin F. Roberts, applied four times to the Boston Primary School Committee** to have her admitted to the nearest school and was refused each time. **In 1848 he sent her to the nearest school anyway and she was put out of it.** He sued the city under an **1845 Massachusetts statute** that let a child unlawfully excluded from a public school recover damages. (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **Her lawyers were Robert Morris — one of the first Black lawyers in the United States, twenty-five years old — and Charles Sumner.** (Sources: NPS; "Long Road to Justice: The African American Experience in the Massachusetts Courts," a project of the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court Historical Society, http://www.longroadtojustice.org/topics/education/sarah-roberts.php .)
- **DISPUTE, stated not resolved:** NPS gives the argument date before the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court as **1 November 1849**; Long Road to Justice gives **4 December 1849**. Both agree the decision came from **Chief Justice Lemuel Shaw in 1850** and that he ruled the city could keep separate schools. The prose should give the year and not pick a day.
- **Shaw's ruling, quoted from the NPS account:** the school committee "have come to the conclusion, that the good of both classes of school will be best promoted, by maintaining the separate primary schools for colored and for white children."
- **What happened next:** Black Bostonians kept campaigning, and **the Massachusetts legislature outlawed segregated public schools in 1855**. (Sources: NPS; Long Road to Justice.)

### 6c. The first state normal school — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on town and year

A **normal school** was a school whose pupils were people learning to be teachers. The name comes from the French *ecole normale*: a school that taught the norms, the rules, of how to teach.

- **The first state-funded normal school in the United States opened at Lexington, Massachusetts, on 3 July 1839, with three students on its first day.** Its first principal was **Cyrus Peirce**, a Unitarian minister whom Horace Mann recruited from Nantucket. (Sources: Framingham State University, "Framingham State History," https://www.framingham.edu/about-fsu/framingham-state-history ; Henry Whittemore Library, Framingham State University, university-history pages and library blog, https://library.framingham.edu/ .)
- **It had 42 students by 1842**, when Peirce resigned in ill health and Samuel J. May took over. **The school moved to West Newton in 1844** with Peirce back as principal, and **to Framingham in 1853**. It is Framingham State University today. (Same sources.)
- **A figure that looks contradictory and is not:** Framingham State's history page says the founding class "consisted of twenty-five women who graduated in 1840." That is the first graduating class, not the opening-day enrollment of three. Do not write the two as if they were one count.

### 6d. Catharine Beecher — the second story slot that was tagged `verified` with nothing behind it, now sourced

**The defect found:** the outline carried `hb-story` slug `catharine-beecher-education` at `status="verified"` with one unsourced line and no bank entry. It is sourced here.

- **Born 6 September 1800 at East Hampton, New York**; eldest child of the preacher Lyman Beecher; sister of Harriet Beecher Stowe. **Died in 1878 at Elmira, New York.** (Sources: National Women's History Museum, "Catharine Beecher," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/catharine-esther-beecher ; Connecticut History, a CT Humanities project, https://connecticuthistory.org/catharine-beecher-champion-of-womens-education/ .)
- **The man she was engaged to, Alexander Metcalf Fisher, a Yale mathematics professor, drowned in 1822 when the ship *Albion* wrecked off the Irish coast.** She did not marry, and went into teaching. (Source: Connecticut History, as above.)
- **The Hartford Female Seminary opened on 20 May 1823 in Hartford, Connecticut.** Beecher started it with her sister Mary. Pupils studied geography, world history, Latin, rhetoric, philosophy and the natural sciences — not the manners-and-fine-arts course usual for girls' schools then. **Enrollment reached 100 full-time pupils within three years**, and the school put up a new building. (Sources: "Today in Connecticut History," a project of the Connecticut Democracy Center and the CT State Library, https://todayincthistory.com/2020/05/20/may-20-catharine-beecher-opens-hartford-female-seminary/ ; National Women's History Museum.)
  - **DISPUTE, stated not resolved:** Connecticut History's article gives the founding as **1824, with her sister Isabella**; Today in Connecticut History and the National Women's History Museum give **1823, with her sister Mary**. Two of the three sources agree on 1823 and Mary; the prose should use 1823 and say the school opened above a harness shop on Main Street with a small handful of pupils, and should not assert a first-day count this bank has not confirmed.
- **She moved west in 1831 with her father and opened the Western Female Institute in Cincinnati**, which failed financially after the Panic of 1837. (Sources: National Women's History Museum; Connecticut History.)
- **Her argument, in her own published words.** In *An Essay on the Education of Female Teachers* (New York, 1835) she argued that teaching was women's natural work, an extension of mothering, and that women's supposedly higher morality made them the right people to raise the next generation. She wrote that if the leading women of the country became "refined, well educated, pious and active," then "the salt is scattered through the land to purify and save." (Source: Keith E. Melder, "Woman's High Calling: The Teaching Profession in America, 1830-1860," *American Studies* 13:2, quoting Beecher's *Essay*, 1835, pp. 18-19, https://journals.ku.edu/amsj/article/download/2397/2356 .)
- **The organisations.** She founded the **Board of National Popular Education in 1847**, which recruited and trained women in Hartford and sent them to teach in the West and South, and the **American Woman's Educational Association in 1852**. (Sources: National Women's History Museum; Connecticut History; Melder.)
  - **Two counts, both real, for different spans — give both or give the span:** Melder, citing the Board's own *Third Annual Report of the General Agent* (Cleveland, 1850) and *Tenth Annual Report* (Hartford, 1857), says the Board **sent 452 teachers** east-to-west. A separate figure widely used for the Board across **1846-56 is 600 teachers**. Do not print one as the total without its date span.
- **What she opposed:** votes for women. She argued that women's proper places were the home and the schoolroom. (Source: National Women's History Museum.) Say this next to the achievement.

### 6e. The pay gap that made women's teaching cheap — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on the pay comparison

These are period figures, collected by school journals at the time, not modern estimates. All are **monthly wages excluding board** — that is, on top of these sums the teacher was usually fed and housed, often by boarding round.

- **Connecticut, 1838**, with nearly equal numbers of men and women teaching: **men $14.50 a month, women $5.75.**
- **Massachusetts, 1838 returns**, with **2,411 men and 3,826 women teaching: men $23.10 a month, women $6.49.**
- **Pennsylvania**, with many more men than women teaching: **men $18.50, women $11.30.**
- **Ashtabula County, Ohio:** men **$14 a month**; women **$1.25 a week**.
- (Source for all four: Keith E. Melder, "Woman's High Calling: The Teaching Profession in America, 1830-1860," *American Studies* 13:2, pp. 21-22; his footnote 15 gives the underlying sources as the *Common School Assistant*, the *Common School Journal*, and the *Connecticut Common School Journal*, 1838-1841. https://journals.ku.edu/amsj/article/download/2397/2356 )
- **The saving was said out loud by the officials making it.** **Samuel Lewis, Ohio's Superintendent of Common Schools, wrote in his 1839 report** that counties employing women teachers "are able to do twice as much with the same money as is done in those counties where female teachers are almost excluded. As the business of teaching is made more respectable, more females engage in it, and the wages are reduced." (Melder, quoting Lewis via the *Connecticut Common School Journal*, II:10, 1 March 1840, p. 155.)
- **Horace Mann's Fourth Annual Report (1841)** records his satisfaction at the "change . . . rapidly taking place, both in public sentiment and action, in regard to the employment of female teachers." (Melder, quoting Mann's Fourth Annual Report as reprinted in the *Common School Journal* III:19, 1 October 1841, pp. 303-304.)

### 6f. How young the teachers were — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on teenage teachers

Do not write "many teachers were teenagers" as a general claim. Write the documented rule instead, which says the same thing and can be checked.

- **Massachusetts' own published admission rules for its new normal schools set the minimum age at 17 for men and 16 for women.** The full rule: "Candidates for admission must declare it to be their intention to qualify themselves to be schoolteachers. If males they must have attained the age of 17 and of 16 if females, and must be free from any disease or infirmity which would unfit them for the office of teachers. They must undergo an examination in orthography, geography and arithmetic. They must furnish satisfactory evidence of good intellectual capacity and of high moral character and principles." (Source: Mary Lou Breitborde and Kelly Kolodny, "Remembering Massachusetts State Normal Schools: Pioneers in Teacher Education," *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015, pp. 26-27, published by Westfield State University, https://www.westfield.ma.edu/historical-journal/ — the article quotes the Board of Education's own rules and the *Common School Journal* I:3, 1 February 1839, p. 38.)
- **The course was one year long and free** to a student who declared she meant to teach in Massachusetts schools; books were free too, though some schools asked students to bring a Bible, a dictionary and an atlas. Subjects: algebra, natural philosophy, physiology, bookkeeping, moral philosophy, geometry, reading, grammar, music, Massachusetts and U.S. history and the Constitution, plus "the art of teaching and the science of school government and theory applied to practice in the model school." (Same source.)
- **A named 17-year-old:** Mary Swift entered the Lexington normal school in 1839, three weeks after her seventeenth birthday. See §6g.

### 6g. Mary Swift (1822-1909) — a named ordinary person, and the era's best-documented student teacher

She fills the era's `ordinary` story slot better than the unnamed "boarding-round teacher" the seed outline asked for, and her record is genuinely a diary.

- **Born 22 June 1822 at Nantucket, Massachusetts; died 2 March 1909 at Cambridge, Massachusetts.**
- **She was in the first class of the first state normal school in America, at Lexington**, and **that class graduated on 4 April 1840.** (Source: Framingham State University Archives, catalogue record for the portrait of Mary Swift Lamson, item FSU_GB_0072, via Digital Commonwealth, https://ark.digitalcommonwealth.org/ark:/50959/5425kn24s .)
- **She kept a journal through the school's first term, and it survives in print.** Arthur O. Norton, ed., *The First State Normal School in America: The Journals of Cyrus Peirce and Mary Swift*, Harvard University Press, 1926, in the series Harvard Documents in the History of Education; lvi + 299 pages. The principal's journal and the student's journal of the same days sit side by side in it. (Confirmed from the published volume's catalogue record; also cited as note 10 by Breitborde and Kolodny, *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015.)
- **What she did next.** **In 1841 she took over from Lydia Drew as the teacher and companion of Laura Bridgman**, a deaf and blind pupil at the Perkins Institution in Boston, and stayed in that job until she married Edwin Lamson in 1846. The Framingham State Archives record says she helped develop finger-talk with Bridgman — spelling words letter by letter into the girl's hand. **She published *Life and Education of Laura Dewey Bridgman, the Deaf, Dumb, and Blind Girl* in 1878**, built largely out of the teachers' own journals; a later edition is dated 1884, and the 1878 printing is held by the National Library of Medicine. (Sources: Framingham State University Archives record, as above; National Library of Medicine, "Circulating Now" post on the 1878 volume, https://circulatingnow.nlm.nih.gov/ ; Internet Archive scan, https://archive.org/details/lifeeducationofl00lams .)
- **Careful with the title.** *Deaf, dumb and blind* is the 1878 book's own wording. "Dumb" there means unable to speak, not stupid; if the phrase is quoted in the book, say what it meant then.

### 6h. Mary Elizabeth Miles, 1843 — the first Black graduate of a public teachers college in America

- **Mary Elizabeth Miles graduated from the Normal School at Lexington in 1843**, and was **the first African-American graduate of any public teachers college in the United States.** She later married Henry Bibb, who had escaped slavery, and taught and organised with him in the abolition movement in Ontario. (Source: Breitborde and Kolodny, "Remembering Massachusetts State Normal Schools," *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015, p. 34.)
- **Put her next to Sarah Roberts (§6b), not instead of her.** In the same decade and the same state, a Black woman trained as a teacher at the state's own normal school and a five-year-old Black girl was put out of the school nearest her house. Both are true and both are Massachusetts.

### 6i. How the normal schools spread — for the span line

- Massachusetts opened three: **Lexington 1839, Barre a few months later (it became Westfield), Bridgewater 1840.** All three survived past the legislature's three-year trial. Massachusetts added **Salem in 1854**, the Normal Art School in Boston in 1873, Worcester in 1874, and Fitchburg, North Adams, Hyannis and Lowell in 1894.
- Beyond Massachusetts: **New York and Philadelphia in the 1840s; Connecticut, Rhode Island, New Jersey, then Michigan and Illinois in the 1850s; Kansas and Wisconsin in the 1860s; Iowa in the 1870s.** **By 1870, 18 states had at least one and there were 39 in the country; by 1900 there were 180.** (Source: Breitborde and Kolodny, pp. 29-30.)

### 6j. Deaf and blind children get schools — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on 1817 and on schools for blind students

- **The American School for the Deaf opened at Hartford, Connecticut, on 15 April 1817**, in Bennett's City Hotel. Its founders were **Thomas Hopkins Gallaudet** and **Laurent Clerc**, a deaf French teacher Gallaudet brought back from Paris, with **Dr. Mason Fitch Cogswell** and ten other Hartford men behind them. Cogswell's deaf daughter **Alice Cogswell** was one of the first pupils; the first classes had **seven full-time students**. Teaching was in sign language, on the French method. (Sources: American School for the Deaf, "History & Cogswell Heritage House," https://www.asd-1817.org/about/history--cogswell-heritage-house ; "Today in Connecticut History," https://todayincthistory.com/2020/04/15/april-15-american-school-for-the-deaf-founded-in-hartford/ ; Gallaudet University Museum history pages, https://gallaudet.edu/museum/history/ .)
- **Two firsts in public money follow it, and they are worth stating.** **The Connecticut General Assembly made the first annual state grant to the school in 1819** — the first state aid to a school in America — and **Congress gave the school a land grant in the Alabama Territory in 1820**, the first federal aid to special education. (Source: American School for the Deaf, as above.)
- **The first school for blind children in the United States was incorporated on 2 March 1829 in Boston** as the New England Asylum for the Blind. **Dr. John Dix Fisher** started it after seeing the Paris school for blind children; **Samuel Gridley Howe** was recruited as its first director in 1831; **it opened in 1832 in Howe's father's house with six pupils.** It moved to a Pearl Street house in 1838; **in 1839 Colonel Thomas Handasyd Perkins sold that house and gave the money to buy a hotel building in South Boston**, and the school took his name. (Source: Mass Moments, a project of Mass Humanities, "Perkins School for the Blind Incorporated," https://www.massmoments.org/moment-details/perkins-school-for-the-blind-incorporated.html .)
- **Laura Bridgman.** Born 21 December 1829 at Hanover, New Hampshire. **Scarlet fever when she was two left her deaf and blind.** **She arrived at the school on 12 October 1837, two months before her eighth birthday.** Howe taught her by touch, pairing raised letters with objects until, after about two months, she understood that the letters stood for things. She was the first deaf and blind person in the United States to be formally educated. **Mary Swift took over as her teacher in 1841** — see §6g. (Sources: Mass Moments, as above; Perkins School for the Blind founders page, https://www.perkins.org/founders/ , which returns 403 to automated fetching and was read through the Mass Moments and Framingham State Archives accounts.)

### 6k. The McGuffey readers — clears the outline's [VERIFY]

- **William Holmes McGuffey, 1800-1873.** He taught at **Miami University in Ohio from 1826 to 1836**, and afterwards was president of Cincinnati College (1836) and of Ohio University (1839-1843), taught at Woodward College (1843-1845), and was at the University of Virginia from 1845 until his death in 1873.
- **The Cincinnati publishing firm Truman and Smith commissioned the readers.** **The First and Second Eclectic Readers came out in 1836, the Third and Fourth in 1837, the Fifth in 1844, and the Sixth in 1857.** They were revised again and again and went through edition after edition.
- **What was in them:** proverbs, grammar, and passages from Shakespeare, set alongside lessons in patriotism, religion, good behaviour, and games and sports.
- **Sales: more than 150 million copies over roughly a hundred years**, most heavily in the Midwest. Treat this as an estimate, as with Webster's speller — nobody kept a single running count.
- (Source for all of the above: Walter Havighurst Special Collections, Miami University Libraries, "William Holmes McGuffey and the McGuffey Eclectic Readers," https://spec.lib.miamioh.edu/home/mcguffey/ ; the collection holds over 300 editions of the readers and spellers and 120 manuscript letters and contracts.)

### 6l. A second school system starts — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on the first Catholic parish schools and the specific disputes

The dispute was not abstract. It was over which Bible was read aloud in the classroom, and in one city it ended in killings.

- **The first free Catholic school in the United States: Saint Joseph's Academy and Free School, Emmitsburg, Maryland, 1810**, started by **Elizabeth Ann Seton** and her Sisters of Charity to teach the poor girls of the parish without charge. Seton was born 28 August 1774 in New York and died 4 January 1821 at Emmitsburg. Many historians date the beginning of Catholic parish schooling in America from this school. (Sources: Sisters of Charity of Cincinnati archives, "Missions of the Sisters of Charity 1809-1840," https://www.srcharitycinti.org/who-we-are/archives/historical-resources/founding-community/missions-of-the-sisters-of-charity/ ; Seton Shrine full biography, https://setonshrine.org/ .)
- **New York, 1840-1842.** The city's public schools were run by the **Public School Society**, a private body paid with state money. Catholic families said the schools were Protestant schools that mocked their religion. **In June 1840 Bishop John Hughes took the Society on**, and, at Governor William Seward's suggestion, New York Catholics asked for a share of the school money for their own schools. **In April 1842 the state legislature abolished the Public School Society and replaced it with elected school boards — and banned religious instruction in the public schools altogether**, which was not what Hughes had asked for. Protestants kept control through the elected boards, and Hughes set about building a separate Catholic school system instead. His house was stoned. (Sources: Archdiocese of New York, "The Archdiocese of New York at 175: The Controversial Young Bishop," https://thegoodnewsroom.org/ ; Thomas J. Shelley, "Archbishop John Hughes and the New York Schools Controversy of 1840-43," *American Nineteenth Century History* 5:1, 2004, https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/1466465042000222204 .)
- **Philadelphia, 1842-1844 — the Bible Riots.** **In 1842 Bishop Francis Patrick Kenrick asked Philadelphia's Board of Controllers of Public Schools** to let Catholic children read the Douay-Rheims Bible their church used instead of the King James Bible, and objected to Catholic children being made to sing Protestant hymns. **In February 1844 a school director in Kensington, Hugh Clark, suggested suspending Bible readings** until the board could settle a rule both sides could accept, and a rumour spread that he had ordered the Bible out of the schools.
  - **6 May 1844:** fighting in Kensington. A nativist named George Shiffler was shot dead; by the end of the day a second man, apparently a bystander, was also dead and several more were wounded, two of them fatally. **8 May:** mobs burned houses and churches. **The violence ended on 10 May.**
  - **5-7 July 1844, Southwark:** an armed clash between a crowd and the militia. At least two people in the crowd were killed at once; by the next morning **four militiamen and probably a dozen rioters were dead**, and many more wounded.
  - **Two Catholic churches were burned — St. Michael's and St. Augustine's** — along with a Catholic seminary and several private houses, including Hugh Clark's.
  - **State the count as the source states it.** The Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia gives the deaths episode by episode and does not print one total; other accounts add them to about twenty or more. Give the episodes and say the total is put at about twenty, rather than printing a false exact figure.
  - **Afterwards:** more than two thousand Philadelphians signed an address praising the militia. In the October 1844 elections the nativist editor Lewis Levin and another nativist won seats in Congress.
  - (Source: Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, Rutgers University-Camden, "Nativist Riots of 1844," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/essays/nativist-riots-of-1844/ .)
- **Shared with:** `religion` leads on the faith argument itself; `immigration` on nativism and the anti-Irish side. This chapter's slice is the schoolroom: which book was read aloud at the start of the day, who had to listen to it, and the second school system built because of it.

### 6m. The one-room school, checked against a real building — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on recitation, slates, stove, ages, and boarding round

The seed outline said these details "are widely repeated and need real citations." They do check out, and the cleanest way to write them is through **one documented building** rather than a general picture.

- **The Freeman School, Homestead National Historical Park, near Beatrice, Nebraska.** Brick, **built in 1872, in use until 1967 — 95 years.** One main room with a coat hall at the front. **Eighteen desks in three rows that step up towards the back**, the desk frames ironwork from Richmond, Indiana. **A wood-burning stove in the middle of the room.** **A recitation bench at the front**, where a pupil came up to say a lesson aloud without disturbing the others working at their desks. **Pupils aged 4 to 20**, seated by age: primary at 5 to 7, intermediate at 8 to 13, upper at 14 to 20. **Most pupils in one year: 33, in 1880.** **The teacher's pay from 1882 to 1900 ran $32 to $40 a month.** Running the school cost **$75 in 1872**, of which **$27 went on firewood in 1873**. (Source: National Park Service, "An 1872 One-Room Schoolhouse Reveals Secrets at Homestead National Historical Park," https://home.nps.gov/articles/000/an-1872-one-room-schoolhouse-reveals-secrets-at-homestead-national-historical-park.htm .)
- **Boarding round and the two-term calendar are already sourced in this bank** in the material parked from `home-family` in PART B: district schools commonly ran a summer term (roughly May to August, younger children, women teachers) and a winter term (roughly November to April, older boys spared from farm work); teachers boarded around with district families in turn. Use those citations, and note that the parked note dates that picture to **before about 1900**, with its documentation drawn from 1890s accounts.
- **Boundary care.** The Freeman School is an 1872 building, so its numbers belong to **1850-1900**. For the **1800-1850** cell, keep the description to what is documented for that half-century — slates, recitation, the stove, mixed ages, walking to school — and do not import the Freeman School's specific figures into it. Say the picture plainly and put the counted example in the later era where it belongs.
- **Segregated one-room schools existed too and are documented as such.** The **Iron Hill School in Delaware** is preserved by the National Park Service as an African-American one-room school. (Source: NPS, "Iron Hill School: An African-American One-Room School," https://www.nps.gov/articles/iron-hill-school-an-african-american-one-room-school-teaching-with-historic-places.htm .)

### 6n. The dunce cap — clears the outline's [VERIFY], and the honest answer is partly a negative

- **Where the word comes from is settled.** *Dunce* comes from the name of **John Duns Scotus**, a philosopher and theologian who died in 1308 and who was, in his own time, one of the most admired thinkers in Europe. His followers were called Dunses. When later scholars turned against his kind of argument, they used his followers' name as an insult, and *dunce* came to mean a stupid person.
- **Where the pointed hat comes from is NOT settled, and the book should say so.** The story that Duns Scotus or his followers wore conical hats because a point would funnel knowledge into the head is repeated everywhere and **no source found in this pass produces evidence for it.** Write that the hat's connection to Duns Scotus is a story people tell and that the record does not support it.
- **The earliest reference this pass could verify is English and literary, and it was checked in the original.** Charles Dickens, *The Old Curiosity Shop*, serialised 1840-41, chapter 6, describing a village schoolroom: "Displayed on hooks upon the wall in all their terrors, were the cane and ruler; and near them, on a small shelf of its own, the dunce's cap, made of old newspapers and decorated with glaring wafers of the largest size." (Verified directly against the full text at Project Gutenberg, ebook 700, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/700/700-0.txt .) So the cap is documented as a schoolroom object from the 1840s — and the first record of it is a novel, and an English one.
- **How common it actually was in American schools: not established.** No count was found in this pass, and the honest line is that nobody counted. Keep the dunce cap out of the outline as a general claim about American schools and, if it is used at all, attach it to the Dickens passage that is real.
- **What IS documented, and should carry the discipline material instead:** the rattan, the ferule, and the written punishment reports required by school rules. See §7f.

### 6o. Teaching an enslaved child to read becomes a crime across the South

The previous agent's era-4 §4f has the first one: **South Carolina, 10 May 1740, banning the teaching of *writing*.** This era is when the bans widen, and the trigger is named in the laws themselves. **Say the sequence, not "anti-literacy laws" as one undated thing.**

- **Virginia**, from the material parked forward in PART A-FWD, sourced to Encyclopedia Virginia (https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/slave-literacy-and-education-in-virginia/ ):
  - **24 January 1804** — enslaved people barred from gathering at night at churches, meetinghouses or anywhere else.
  - **2 March 1819** — enslaved people banned from "any school or schools for teaching them reading or writing, either in the day or night."
  - **7 April 1831** — "All meetings of free negroes or mulattoes, at any school house...for teaching them reading or writing...shall be considered as an unlawful assembly." This one reaches **free** Black people.
- **North Carolina, 1830-31 session — the punishments are written into the act, so quote them.** *An Act to Prevent All Persons from Teaching Slaves to Read or Write, the Use of Figures Excepted.* Its preamble says the teaching of enslaved people to read and write "has a tendency to excite dissatisfaction in their minds, and to produce insurrection and rebellion."
  - **Section I — a free person who teaches, or tries to teach, an enslaved person to read or write:** if white, **a fine of not less than $100 and not more than $200, or imprisonment**; **if a free person of colour, whipping — not more than thirty-nine lashes and not fewer than twenty** — or a fine or imprisonment, at the court's discretion.
  - **Section II — an enslaved person who teaches another enslaved person to read or write: thirty-nine lashes on the bare back.**
  - **Section III** told judges to instruct grand juries to enforce it.
  - (Source: *Acts Passed by the General Assembly of the State of North Carolina at the Session of 1830-1831*, Raleigh, 1831, reproduced in full at https://www.historyisaweapon.com/defcon1/slaveprohibit.html ; the same act and its preamble are presented as a primary source by NCpedia, the State Library of North Carolina, https://www.ncpedia.org/anchor/primary-source-bill-prevent , which returns 403 to automated fetching; the original bill is held by the State Archives of North Carolina, Legislative Papers, 1830-31 session.)
  - **Say what a lash is, in plain words, at first use.** A whipping meant blows across the bare back with a leather whip or a switch, hard enough to break the skin. Thirty-nine was the usual maximum number in Southern law. The record here gives the number of blows and the fact that the back was bare; it does not describe the injuries in this act, and the book should not invent them.
- **Other states in the same stretch:** **Georgia 1829**; **Louisiana 1830**; **Alabama 1832**, whose slave code fined anyone teaching a free Black person or an enslaved person to spell, read or write **between $250 and $500**; **South Carolina 1834**, extending its 1740 act to reading as well as writing. (Source: the survey of state anti-literacy laws at https://oaklandliteracycoalition.org/literacy-by-any-means-necessary-the-history-of-anti-literacy-laws-in-the-u-s/ , cross-checked against the state-by-state summary at Encyclopedia.com, "Literacy and Anti-Literacy Laws." **Treat the individual state penalties above as needing one more primary check before print, except North Carolina's, which was read in the act's own text.**)
- **Why the laws came when they did, from the laws' own language and their timing:** David Walker's *Appeal to the Coloured Citizens of the World* was published in 1829, and Nat Turner's rebellion was in August 1831. The North Carolina preamble names the fear directly: reading produces "insurrection and rebellion."
- **And it did not work.** Encyclopedia Virginia gives roughly **5 percent of enslaved people in Virginia literate by 1775, and says the share doubled to about 10 percent** in the decades before the Civil War — that is, while the bans were in force. (PART A-FWD, sourced to Encyclopedia Virginia.)
- **Shared with:** `slavery-freedom` leads on learning to read as resistance and on what literacy meant to enslaved people. This chapter's slice is that teaching was made the crime, and what the law did to the teacher.

---

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): the anti-literacy laws, penalty by penalty, from Woodson's statute citations

The §6o list of Georgia, Louisiana, Alabama and South Carolina penalties was flagged as "needing one more primary check." Carter G. Woodson, *The Education of the Negro Prior to 1861* (1915), chapter on the reaction, cites each state's session laws or digest in his footnotes. Read in full at Project Gutenberg, ebook 11089, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/11089/pg11089.txt .

- **Louisiana, 1830** (Acts of the Ninth Session of the Legislature of Louisiana, p. 96): anyone who taught, or allowed or caused to be taught, any enslaved person to read or write was to be **imprisoned not less than one month nor more than twelve.** The same act made writing or distributing anything "having the tendency to produce discontent among the slaves" punishable by **hard labour for life or death**, at the court's discretion.
- **Georgia** (Dawson, *A Compilation of the Laws of the State of Georgia*, p. 413): a Black person who taught another to read or write was **fined and whipped**. A white person who did it was **fined up to $500 and jailed** at the magistrate's discretion. Woodson dates it "a year later" than Louisiana's 1830 act, which reads as 1831. The bank's other sources (§6o, and the `slavery-freedom` bank) give **19 December 1829**. Give the year as 1829 and do not state it more exactly than that. A later Georgia law fined anyone up to **$100** for employing an enslaved or free Black person "in setting up type or other labor about a printing office requiring a knowledge of reading and writing" (Cobb's and Prince's digests).
- **Virginia, 1831** (Laws of Virginia 1830-31, p. 108, ss. 5-6), the penalties behind the 7 April 1831 wording already in §6o: any judge or justice of the peace could issue a warrant to break up a meeting of free Black people held to teach reading or writing, and **inflict up to twenty lashes** on those caught there. **A white person convicted of teaching Black people to read or write was fined $50 and could be jailed for two months.** Teaching an enslaved person carried **a fine of $10 to $100.**
- **Alabama, 1832** (Clay's *Digest*, p. 543): **a fine of $250 to $500** on anyone who tried to educate any Black person, enslaved or free. In 1833 lawmakers let the mayor and aldermen of Mobile license teachers for the free children of Creole families who had lived there in 1803.
- **South Carolina, 1834** ("Laws of South Carolina, 1834"): Woodson says the act was "more stringent" and aimed at "the destruction of their schools," and also barred Black people from working as clerks or salesmen in shops. **He gives no penalty figures.** A search-result summary gives: a white offender, a fine up to $100 and up to six months in jail; a free person of colour, up to 50 lashes and a fine up to $50 *(unconfirmed: search summary only)*.
- **Who was punished and how, stated plainly:** in every state that set out penalties, white teachers faced fines and jail, and Black teachers, enslaved or free, faced the whip. That is written into the North Carolina act (§6o), the Virginia act and the Georgia act above.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): the North had its own attacks on schools for Black pupils (boundary note)

`rights-movements` owns Prudence Crandall's school at Canterbury, Connecticut, 1833-34 (its verified story `prudence-crandall`). This chapter should point to it and not retell it. One more Northern case, not told in any outline in the book (grep 2026-09-27):

- **Noyes Academy, Canaan, New Hampshire, 1835.** Trustees voted in 1834 to admit students of every race, and it opened in March 1835 with **28 white students and 14 Black students** (Colored Conventions Project, University of Delaware, "The Noyes Academy," https://coloredconventions.org/african-free-schools/education-and-conventions/noyes-academy/ ; Town of Canaan, "Noyes Academy," https://www.canaannh.gov/243/Noyes-Academy ). The Black students included **Henry Highland Garnet, Alexander Crummell, Thomas S. Sidney and Julia Williams** (Colored Conventions Project).
- **What was done, and by whom.** Canaan residents held a town meeting in July 1835 and voted the school a public nuisance (Colored Conventions Project; the Town of Canaan page says a vote of 70 residents). **On 10 August 1835 men from Canaan and neighbouring towns used chains and teams of oxen to pull the building off its foundation and drag it down the road.** Counts differ: "approximately seventy-five" men with "nearly a hundred yoked oxen" (Colored Conventions Project); "a hundred yoke of oxen" (Woodson 1915, citing the free people of colour convention minutes); the Town of Canaan says it took two days and the building ended as a heap near the meeting house. A figure of nearly 500 men and a headline figure of 90 yoke appear in search summaries only *(unconfirmed: search summary only)*. Garnet fired a shotgun from a window and the crowd drew back (Colored Conventions Project). The Black students were driven out of Canaan, and Garnet, Crummell and Sidney went on to the Oneida Institute near Utica, New York (Town of Canaan).
- **SEARCHED, NOT FOUND in this pass:** whether anyone was charged or punished for the destruction. Neither page says so. The Town of Canaan page says the town's lawyer judged the act illegal regardless of the vote. How the prose can say it: "No record found shows that anyone was ever punished for it."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): federal schooling of Native children begins in this era, 1819-1848

**Gap found:** the outline's era 6 has no Native schooling at all, but the federal government's own investigation dates its Indian boarding-school system from **1819**. `native-nations` leads on what the schools did to the nations and `religion` already tells the Civilization Fund's church money (its outline, era 6). This chapter's slice is the school: who ran it, who paid, what was taught.

- **The Civilization Fund Act, 3 March 1819** (Ch. 85, 3 Stat. 516). Congress authorised the President to hire "capable persons of good moral character" to teach Native nations farming "and for teaching their children in reading, writing, and arithmetic," and appropriated **$10,000 a year**. Its stated purpose was "providing against the further decline and final extinction of the Indian tribes ... and for introducing among them the habits and arts of civilization." The money was "often apportioned to various religious institutions and organizations" until Congress repealed the annual appropriation in **1873**. In **1824 Secretary of War John C. Calhoun** set up a Superintendent of Indian Affairs inside the War Department to run the fund; Congress created the Commissioner of Indian Affairs in **1832**. (Source: U.S. Department of the Interior, *Federal Indian Boarding School Initiative Investigative Report*, Vol. I, May 2022, pp. 27-28, https://www.bia.gov/sites/default/files/dup/inline-files/bsi_investigative_report_may_2022_508.pdf , read in full with pypdf.)
- **The Choctaw Academy, Scott County, Kentucky, 1825-1848.** Run on the farm of **Richard Mentor Johnson**, a U.S. senator and later Vice President. **21 Choctaw boys** arrived in November 1825. Its first and longest-serving superintendent was a Baptist minister, **Thomas Henderson**. It was paid for with Choctaw money: under an 1825 treaty the United States agreed to pay the Choctaw **$6,000 a year** for education, and Johnson's school received it. The Choctaw tried in **1829 and 1833** to cut ties with the academy and regain control of that money, and failed. **More than 600 students from 17 nations** attended over about twenty years. **An 1840 inspection "confirmed the many complaints of poor living conditions, inadequate education, food, and clothing, as well as forced manual labor."** The Choctaw leader Peter Pitchlynn removed all Choctaw students in **1842**, and the last **13 Chickasaw students left in 1848**. Johnson enslaved at least sixty people, including Julia Chinn and their daughters Adaline and Imogene, and enslaved people did most of the farming, carpentry and cleaning at the school. (Sources: Kentucky Historical Society, ExploreKYHistory, "Choctaw Indian Academy," https://explorekyhistory.ky.gov/items/show/594 ; Penn State University, "Great Crossings," on the historian Christina Snyder's *Great Crossings: Indians, Settlers, and Slaves in the Age of Jackson*, Oxford, 2017, https://www.psu.edu/news/research/story/great-crossings .) The Penn State page also says the Choctaw Nation paid **$70,000** in the first year; that sits oddly beside $6,000 a year and is not used here.
  - **"First" dispute, stated not resolved:** ExploreKYHistory calls the Choctaw Academy "the first federally controlled residential/boarding school for Native Americans in US history." The National Park Service calls Carlisle (1879) "the first of many American Indian boarding schools." The 2024 presidential proclamation says Carlisle was "the first Federal off-reservation boarding school." **The proclamation's wording is the safe one: Carlisle was the first federal off-reservation boarding school, and federally funded schooling of Native children began sixty years earlier.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): era-6 "firsts" checked against the bank

- **"The first time the argument about hitting children in school is carried on in public, in print, with both sides named"** (§6a, and the outline's discipline span). **No source in the bank makes this claim.** It is the eras 6-7 agent's own judgement. Writers should drop it or say only that the 1844-45 pamphlet exchange is the best-documented early public argument about it. The outline sentence has been removed.
- **Connecticut's 1819 grant "the first state money ever given to a school in America"** (outline). The American School for the Deaf's own history is the only source (§6j), and it is the school describing itself. No independent source was checked in this pass. Write it as the school's claim ("the school's own history calls it the first state aid of its kind"), or drop it. The outline now attributes it.
- **Laura Bridgman, "first deaf and blind person in the United States to be formally educated"** (Mass Moments). Sourced. Keep "formally."
- **Mary Elizabeth Miles, first Black graduate of any public teachers college** (Breitborde and Kolodny, 2015). Sourced.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): Webster's speller, the "60 million" figure, sourced

Carried from era 5 (§5f and the T-261a unit-2 check): the 60 million figure was unsourced. It now has two sources that open:

- "by 1837 it had sold 15 million copies, and some 60 million by 1890" (Jeremy Norman, HistoryofInformation.com, "Noah Webster Reforms the Teaching of English in the United States," https://www.historyofinformation.com/detail.php?id=3228 , read by curl).
- "over time sold some sixty million copies" (Connecticut Academy of Arts and Sciences, "Our Founders: Noah Webster," https://caas.yale.edu/node/90 ).
- The National Museum of Language gives "By 1861, it was selling a million copies per year" and "up to a total of 100 million copies" (https://languagemuseum.org/exhibits/emerging-american-language-in-1812/blue-backed-speller/ ).
- **So the range stands and both ends are now sourced:** about 15 million by 1837, about 60 million by 1890, and estimates up to about 100 million in all. All are estimates. Nobody kept one count. The era-5 Webster block can keep its range. This pass did not edit era 5.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): era-6 bank check, what was checked and left alone

- **Who stoned Bishop Hughes's house (§6l):** not searched further. The sources say only that it was stoned. Prose: "Someone stoned his house". Do not name a crowd or a group.
- **Land:** no settlement or land-taking is told in era 6's outline apart from the Choctaw Academy, which sat on Johnson's own farm in Kentucky. The land point for these fifty years belongs to `native-nations` (removal). The Morrill land is era 7.
- **Perishable:** none in era 6. The era-7 line "about 180 today" days of school is re-checked by the era-10 calendar entry (§10h). It is not restated here.
