## 7. 1850 to 1900

### 7a. Carlisle — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on 1879 and on the half-day system's name

**Ruling 4/7 stands: `native-nations` leads on what the boarding schools did to the nations. This chapter's slice is the school itself — who ran it, what the day was, what was taught, and what happened to a child who broke a rule.** The principal source below is a federal proclamation, which is as close to an official account as exists.

- **Where the model came from.** In 1875 the Army arrested **72 people from several nations**, labelled "hostiles" or "ringleaders," and shipped them by train to **Fort Marion, Florida**. The War Department put **1st Lieutenant Richard Henry Pratt of the 10th Cavalry** in charge of them. **At Fort Marion Pratt cut the prisoners' hair, put them in military uniforms, drilled them, taught them English, sold their crafts and drawings, and assigned them out as labourers.** Everything Carlisle later did to children, Pratt did first to prisoners of war.
- **Pratt's own words, spoken in 1892 to the National Conference of Charities and Corrections at Denver, Colorado:** "[T]hat all the Indian there is in the race should be dead. Kill the Indian in him and save the man."
- **Founding.** **In August 1879 the Secretary of War approved the transfer of the vacant Carlisle Barracks in Pennsylvania to the Secretary of the Interior, to be used as a school for Native children.** **On 6 October 1879, 83 American Indian and Alaska Native children — 24 girls and 59 boys — arrived.** They came from the Rosebud and Pine Ridge agencies in Dakota Territory. Congress then passed a law paying Pratt a salary to run it. **The Interior Department ran Carlisle for almost forty years.**
  - **DISPUTE, stated not resolved.** The proclamation gives **83 children on 6 October 1879**; other accounts give **82** arriving late on 5 October, and the National Park Service says "approximately 140." The bank's earlier parked note from `native-nations` gives the school's opening as **1 November 1879**, which is the formal opening date rather than the first arrivals. Write the arrival date and say the count of that first group is given as 82 or 83, and that the school formally opened weeks later.
- **What was done to children on arrival.** **Staff cut their hair and took their clothes away**, replacing them with military uniforms for boys and Victorian dresses for girls, and gave them English names.
  - **Luther Standing Bear**, a son of the Oglala Lakota chief Standing Bear, was brought to Carlisle in its opening year, and wrote later: "Now, after having my hair cut . . . I felt that I was no more Indian but would be an imitation of a white man."
- **The day.** **It started at 6:00 a.m. and ended with Taps and room inspection at 9:00 p.m.** Sunday school, chapel, Catholic instruction and Bible study were all compulsory. **Speaking one's own language was forbidden, and the rule "was often enforced with corporal punishment."** A child who broke a rule "went to the guardhouse for punishment or were sentenced to hard labor" (National Park Service).
- **The half-day system, and what it actually was.** Half the day was classroom and half was work, and the work was not a lesson — it ran the institution. **Boys were assigned mechanics, blacksmithing, tin-smithing, wagon-making, carpentering, tailoring, shoemaking, harness-making, baking, painting, printing and farming; girls were assigned cooking, laundry and housekeeping.** The proclamation calls it "'vocational' or 'industrial' training in the form of **compulsory labor**." **Children built much of the school with their own hands** — a large brick printing office, a gymnasium, a hospital, doctor's quarters, a model home, a laundry building, the Leupp Indian Art Studio, and a warehouse.
- **The outing system.** Carlisle staff "regularly sent children and youths to spend a portion of each year living with and working for white families," an arrangement meant to "impart[] the lesson of Americanism." **The children's wages were deposited "to their credit" with the school** rather than given to them.
- **The money.** The school funded its gymnasium by taking what it called donations **out of the children's own Individual Indian Money Accounts**, trust accounts the federal government held for them. It also kept the money from selling things the children made and from gate receipts at football games. **A congressional investigation in 1914 heard testimony that the coach, Glenn "Pop" Warner, used football proceeds for his personal gain.**
- **The deaths.** **More than 180 children died while attending the Carlisle School. Many of them are buried in marked graves at the Carlisle Barracks Main Post Cemetery.** The National Park Service gives "at least 168" dead of tuberculosis, pneumonia and influenza; **state the federal figure of more than 180 and note that counts differ and that both are minimums.**
- **The size and the end.** **By the time it closed in 1918 the school had put 7,800 Indian children from more than 140 tribes through it. Some children were as young as five when they arrived.** When the War Department took the post back in 1918, **279 children still there were transferred to other federal Indian boarding schools.**
  - **A second dispute to state:** the National Park Service page says "over ten thousand children attended Carlisle between 1879 and 1918" from "over 142 Indian nations." The 2024 presidential proclamation says 7,800 from more than 140 tribes. **Give the proclamation's number, name the other, and say the counts differ.**
- **The system Carlisle started.** The proclamation states the federal government's aim plainly: to assimilate Native children "by stripping them of their languages, religions, and cultures," and that the schools "often used physical abuse, compulsory labor, and corporal punishment," that "many Native children were subjected to sexual abuse at the schools," and that staff "provided them with inadequate medical services, and deprived them of essential nutrition." **"According to available records, nearly 1,000 Native youths died in schools across the system, but the actual number of lives lost is likely much higher."** The system ran under 34 presidents, from Thomas Jefferson to Lyndon Johnson, at today's equivalent of **at least $23 billion** in federal money.
- **(Source for all of the above except where the National Park Service is named: Proclamation 10870 of 9 December 2024, "Establishment of the Carlisle Federal Indian Boarding School National Monument," *Federal Register* vol. 89 no. 239, 12 December 2024, pp. 100289-100297, full text at https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/2024/12/12/2024-29459.txt . The federalregister.gov HTML pages redirect automated fetches to a block page; the full_text URL works and is reachable through the site's own API at https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents/2024-29459.json . NPS material: "The Carlisle Indian Industrial School: Assimilation with Education after the Indian Wars," https://www.nps.gov/articles/the-carlisle-indian-industrial-school-assimilation-with-education-after-the-indian-wars-teaching-with-historic-places.htm . Pratt's 1892 speech text: Carlisle Indian School Digital Resource Center, Dickinson College, https://carlisleindian.dickinson.edu/teach/kill-indian-him-and-save-man-r-h-pratt-education-native-americans .)**

### 7b. Zitkala-Ša — the `candidate` story, now verified, WITH A CORRECTION TO AN OFFICIAL SOURCE

**Read this before writing her.** The 2024 presidential proclamation quoted above places Zitkala-Ša's first day, and the cutting of her braids, **at Carlisle. That is wrong.** She was a child at a different school, and only came to Carlisle years later as an adult employee. Do not repeat the error.

- **Born 22 February 1876 on the Yankton Indian Reservation, South Dakota.** Her Dakota name, Zitkála-Šá, means **Red Bird**. Her English name was Gertrude Simmons.
- **At eight years old, in 1884, Quaker missionaries took her from the reservation to White's Indiana Manual Labor Institute at Wabash, Indiana.** She stayed until **1887**, went home, **returned at fifteen, and graduated in 1895.** **This is where her braids were cut** — not at Carlisle.
- **Earlham College, Richmond, Indiana**, which she left in **1897**, six weeks before graduating, for reasons of money and health. She studied violin at the New England Conservatory in Boston.
- **She taught music at Carlisle from 1899** — as a twenty-three-year-old teacher, not a pupil.
- **She wrote her own account and published it while young.** Three autobiographical essays ran in the *Atlantic Monthly* in 1900, and she published in *Harper's* as well. They were collected in *American Indian Stories* (1921).
- **Her own words on the hair-cutting, verified against the printed text** (*American Indian Stories*, "The School Days of an Indian Girl," https://archive.org/details/americanindianst01zitk , pp. 54-56):
  - Why it mattered: "Our mothers had taught us that only unskilled warriors who were captured had their hair shingled by the enemy. Among our people, short hair was worn by mourners, and shingled hair by cowards!"
  - Her friend Judewin: "We have to submit, because they are strong." Her answer: "No, I will not submit! I will struggle first!"
  - She hid under a bed in a dormitory. "I remember being dragged out, though I resisted by kicking and scratching wildly. In spite of myself, I was carried downstairs and tied fast in a chair."
  - "I cried aloud, shaking my head all the while until I felt the cold blades of the scissors against my neck, and heard them gnaw off one of my thick braids. Then I lost my spirit. . . . In my anguish I moaned for my mother, but no one came to comfort me. Not a soul reasoned quietly with me, as my own mother used to do; for now I was only one of many little animals driven by a herder."
- **What she did afterwards — the record supplies the close, so use it.** She joined the **Society of American Indians** (founded 1911) and was its secretary from **1916**; edited the *American Indian Magazine*, **1918-1919**; co-wrote the opera *The Sun Dance* with William F. Hanson in **1913**; published the report **"Oklahoma's Poor Rich Indians" in 1924**; started the **Indian Welfare Committee** of the General Federation of Women's Clubs in 1924 and ran a voter-registration drive; and **co-founded the National Council of American Indians in 1926** with her husband, serving as its president until she died.
- **Died 26 January 1938 in Washington, D.C.; buried at Arlington National Cemetery.** Her headstone reads "Gertrude Simmons Bonnin / Zitkala-Ša of the Sioux Indians / 1876-1936" — the death year on the stone is wrong.
- (Sources: National Park Service, "Zitkala-Ša (Red Bird / Gertrude Simmons Bonnin)," https://www.nps.gov/people/zitkala-sa.htm ; National Women's History Museum, https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/zitkala-sa ; Smithsonian, National Portrait Gallery object record NPG.79.26, https://www.si.edu/object/zitkala-sa:npg_S_NPG.79.26 ; her own *American Indian Stories*, as above.)
- **Change her `status` from `candidate` to `verified` and change `kind` — she is famous, not ordinary.** She is in the Smithsonian's National Portrait Gallery and is quoted in a presidential proclamation.

### 7c. Josephine Foster, 1866 — a NAMED beaten child, from the school committee's own printed account

**This settles the `education` ruling in `control/hard-subjects-policy.md` §6:** "A named beaten child if a source supports one; otherwise stay at the documented implements-and-rules level." A source supports one. The pamphlet was located, downloaded and read in full.

**The source:** *An Address to the People of Cambridge from the School Committee, concerning a Recent Case of Corporal Punishment in the Allston Grammar School.* Cambridge: Press of John Wilson and Son, 1866. 24 pages. Written by Mr. Muzzey of the Board, adopted unanimously on 28 September 1866, **four thousand copies ordered printed for general distribution.** Held at the Monroe C. Gutman Library, Harvard Graduate School of Education (shelfmark EducR 528.1; OCLC 906722; LCCN 27011571; HOLLIS https://id.lib.harvard.edu/alma/990143000070203941/catalog ). **Full text online: https://archive.org/details/g5182webversion .**

- **What happened, in the committee's own account.** On **12 June 1866**, at the Allston Grammar School in Cambridgeport, Massachusetts, **Josephine Foster** refused to do as the master, **Mr. B. W. Roberts**, told her in her writing lesson. He made her stand in front of the class. That afternoon, while he was out of the room, an assistant teacher, **Miss Sarah M. Gray**, saw her whispering with another girl and asked her what she thought of whispering; Josephine answered "I don't know" in a way the teacher took as defiance. Miss Gray took both girls to the recitation room, hit the other girl's hand a few times with a **rattan** — a thin cane cut from rattan palm — and sent her back to her seat. She then told Josephine to hold out her hand. **Josephine said, "I won't be whipped."**
- **What the master decided.** Roberts came back at about a quarter past three. He would not do it himself, telling Miss Gray "it would be less humiliating to the girl to submit to a female teacher than to him," and told her to call another teacher to help if the girl fought. **He recommended his ferule instead of the rattan.** The pamphlet describes the ferule exactly: "a flexible instrument, made of two flat pieces of leather, sewed together, and filled with some soft substance." **The pamphlet says the ferule "was shown to be a common implement of punishment in schools, and to be in use in several of the other Grammar schools of the city."**
- **What was done.** Josephine said again "I won't be whipped," and began screaming before any blow was struck. **Miss Hodges, another teacher, held Josephine's left hand while Miss Gray struck her right hand.** Her screaming carried into the schoolroom and set some of the boys laughing, so **Roberts ordered the boys to march to piano music — a regular two-minute exercise — so he could go into the recitation room without an audience.** He took her hand himself and told her she would be punished until she stopped crying out; **five blows were struck in his presence.** After he left, Miss Gray talked to her, and Josephine finally **held out her hand voluntarily and took three to five more light blows.**
- **The count and the marks, as the pamphlet states them.** **"In all, from fifteen to twenty blows were inflicted. These were administered by Miss Gray alone, and were upon the hand only."** Josephine and her mother testified that **for four days her left arm and thumb carried marks from being held** by Roberts and Miss Hodges, and that her right hand showed red marks that were gone by eight o'clock the same evening.
- **Her age is given twice in the same pamphlet, differently, and the book must say so.** Page 5 calls her "a member of the school, sixteen years of age." Page 10 says "her teachers supposed her to be not over thirteen years of age," and adds that the usual age in her class was even lower. **State both.**
- **Her parents.** **Charles and Elizabeth Foster wrote to the School Committee the next day, 13 June 1866**, charging Roberts, Gray and Hodges "for cruelty in punishment inflicted on our daughter, Josephine Foster," saying she "was, without sufficient cause, cruelly and most shamefully punished," and asking "that the honorable Committee will have justice done us." A hearing was held on **21 June 1866**.
- **The result.** The Board **"unanimously adopted the following resolution: Resolved, That, having heard the evidence and arguments of both the complainants and the teachers, regarding the punishment inflicted on Josephine Foster, we are of opinion that the case does not require any action on the part of this Board."** Nobody was censured. The pamphlet came out of a separate petition from **James Walker and others asking the Committee to abolish the corporal punishment of girls**, which the Committee did not do.
- **Scale, for context, from the same pamphlet:** the Allston Grammar School had **over five hundred pupils**; Roberts had been its master for eighteen years.

### 7d. Punishment records — the [VERIFY] on whether they survive, answered

They survive because **school rules required teachers to write them.** The 1866 pamphlet quotes Boston's rule in full: **Regulations of the Public Schools of Boston, chapter viii, section 13.**

- Teachers must not use corporal punishment where "order can be preserved by milder measures"; **"in no case shall resort be had to confinement in a closet or wardrobe, or to other cruel or unusual punishment."**
- **"It shall be the duty of the several masters and teachers in the public schools, at the close of each month, to make, in writing, to the Chairmen of their District Committees, a report of all cases in which corporal punishment has been inflicted; which report shall state the name of the pupil, the amount of punishment, and the reason for its infliction."** District chairmen then reported the quarterly totals **and the average per teacher.**
- **"Corporal punishment shall be inflicted only after the nature of the offence has been fully explained to the scholar; and shall be restricted to blows on the hand with a rattan, except in cases where a pupil refuses to submit to such punishment. Corporal punishment shall not be inflicted on a girl in a Grammar school without the consent and approval of the master."**
- The Cambridge committee added that "the rules in other places in our Commonwealth are of the same general tenor."
- **So the honest line is: the beatings were written down, by name, by month, with the reason and the count — because the rules said they had to be.** That is a far better sentence for the chapter than any general claim about how common the dunce cap was.

### 7e. The first state to ban it — clears the outline's [VERIFY]

- **New Jersey banned corporal punishment in its public schools in 1867**, the first state to do so.
- **No other state followed for more than a hundred years. Massachusetts was second.** Sources give the Massachusetts year as **1971 or 1972**; state the disagreement or give "about 1971." Only three more states had banned it by 1977.
- (Sources: New Jersey State Bar Foundation, "Is Corporal Punishment Making a Comeback?", https://njsbf.org/2023/01/04/is-corporal-punishment-making-a-comeback/ ; Brookings Institution, "Ending corporal punishment of preschool-age children," https://www.brookings.edu/articles/ending-corporal-punishment-of-preschool-age-children/ .)
- **Boundary note for the eras 8-10 agent:** the modern sequence, *Ingraham v. Wright* (1977), and the states where it is still lawful belong to you, not here. This era gets New Jersey 1867 and the fact that it stood alone for a century.

### 7f. Booker T. Washington — the third story slot that was tagged `verified` with nothing behind it, now sourced

**The defect found:** the outline carried `hb-story` slug `booker-t-washington` at `status="verified"` with one unsourced line and no bank entry. It is sourced here.

- **Born enslaved in 1856 on the James Burroughs farm at Hale's Ford, Franklin County, Virginia.** His mother was **Jane**, the farm's cook. He lived with her and two half-siblings in a one-room kitchen cabin, and as a small child carried water to the field workers, carried other children's schoolbooks, and hauled grain to the mill.
- **Freed in 1865**, he moved with his family to **Malden, West Virginia**, and worked as a **salt packer, a coal miner, and a butler**. **He learned to read the number 18 on the salt barrels at the furnace** — that was the number of his stepfather's salt-packing crew, and it was the first thing he could read.
- **At sixteen he travelled over 500 miles to Hampton Institute in Virginia**, arriving "dirty, hungry and with just fifty cents," and **paid his way by working as the janitor.** He graduated at nineteen and went back to Malden to teach. **In 1879 he returned to Hampton as a teacher**, working with the Native American students there and running the night school.
- **Tuskegee.** He arrived in Tuskegee, Alabama in **June 1881** and **opened the school on 4 July 1881 with thirty students**, in a shanty lent by a Black church, **Butler Chapel A.M.E. Zion.** He was the first teacher and the founding principal, and stayed principal until he died. The Alabama legislature had funded the school after **Lewis Adams**, a formerly enslaved tradesman in Tuskegee, made it the condition of delivering Black votes to two white politicians; **George Washington Campbell** was one of its first commissioners.
- **What was taught there.** Trades and farming alongside academic subjects; **the students made the bricks and put up the buildings themselves.** Washington argued that Black Americans should build skills and property first and press for political rights later.
- **The Atlanta speech, 18 September 1895**, at the Cotton States and International Exposition. **W. E. B. Du Bois named it the "Atlanta Compromise"** and attacked it. Say that the argument existed and that it was between two named Black educators; `rights-movements` leads on the argument itself.
- **In 1901 he was the first Black American invited to dine at the White House.** **He died on 14 November 1915, aged 59**, of complications of high blood pressure.
- (Sources: National Park Service, "Booker T. Washington," Tuskegee Institute National Historic Site, https://www.nps.gov/tuin/learn/historyculture/booker-t-washington.htm ; National Park Service, Booker T. Washington National Monument, https://www.nps.gov/bowa/learn/historyculture/index.htm ; Library of Congress, "Booker T. Washington Founds Tuskegee School," https://guides.loc.gov/this-month-in-business-history/july/washington-founds-tuskegee ; Tuskegee University's own history, https://www.tuskegee.edu/legacy/booker-t-washington.html ; National Archives, "Happy Founder's Day Tuskegee University," https://rediscovering-black-history.blogs.archives.gov/2024/07/08/happy-founders-day-tuskegee-university/ .)

### 7g. Charlotte Forten (1837-1914) — fills the era's `(target) a Freedmen's Bureau teacher` slot with a real named woman

**Rename the slot when you fill it.** She went south under the **Port Royal Experiment in 1862**, three years before the Freedmen's Bureau existed. Calling her a Freedmen's Bureau teacher would be wrong.

- **Born in 1837 in Philadelphia**, into a well-off Black abolitionist family; taught at home by private tutors because Philadelphia's schools would not take her. **She came to Salem, Massachusetts in 1853** to live with the Remond family and go to school, and attended the Higginson Grammar School for Girls.
- **She entered Salem Normal School and graduated in 1856 — the school's first African American graduate.** She studied English literature, algebra, geometry, physical geography, hydrostatics, optics, orthography, etymology, physiology and Latin. **Its principal, Richard Edwards, got her a teaching post at the Epes Grammar School in Salem, which made her the first African American teacher in Salem's public schools** — teaching white children, which was then unheard of in Massachusetts.
  - **A source disagreement to note:** the National Park Service page says she "entered Salem Normal School in 1856"; Salem State University's own history and a scholarly article in the *Historical Journal of Massachusetts* both say she **graduated in 1856**, in the school's second class. Use the school's own record.
- **In October 1862 she sailed for St. Helena Island, South Carolina**, to teach at the **Penn School** alongside **Laura Towne** of Philadelphia and **Ellen Murray** of Newport, Rhode Island. **She was the first Black teacher in Beaufort County.** The Penn School had been founded that same year, **1862, with 41 pupils; by 1865 it had 436 pupils in four buildings.** She taught on St. Helena until the end of the Civil War.
- **She wrote it down as it happened.** She kept journals through her time at Penn School, and published **"Life on the Sea Islands" in the *Atlantic Monthly* in May and June 1864.** Her Salem journals are quoted by Salem State: Salem was "the happiest of my life….I have been fortunate enough to receive the instruction of the best and kindest teachers," and also "I wonder that every colored person is not a misanthrope. Surely we have everything to make us hate mankind."
- **Afterwards:** taught in Boston and in Charleston, South Carolina; **by 1872 she was in Washington, D.C., teaching at the preparatory school that became Paul Laurence Dunbar High School**; became a Treasury Department clerk in 1873; married the Reverend Francis J. Grimké at 41; had one child, who died in infancy. **She died on 23 July 1914 in Washington, D.C.**
- (Sources: National Park Service, "Charlotte Forten Grimké," https://www.nps.gov/people/charlotte-forten-grimke.htm ; National Park Service, "Reconstruction Era African American Schools in the South," https://www.nps.gov/articles/reconstruction-era-african-american-schools-in-the-south.htm ; Salem State University, "Charlotte Forten," https://www.salemstate.edu/charlotte-forten ; Breitborde and Kolodny, *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015, p. 34.)

### 7h. The freedpeople's schools, counted — from the Freedmen's Bureau's own report

The Bureau of Refugees, Freedmen, and Abandoned Lands was created in the War Department in **March 1865**. **John W. Alvord** was its inspector of schools and wrote semi-annual reports from 1866 to 1870. **His ninth report, dated 1 January 1870, covering the half-year to 31 December 1869, was read in full for this bank** (https://archive.org/details/ninthsemiannualr00alvo ).

- **Totals for that half-year: 4,027 schools of all kinds, 8,907 teachers of all kinds, 210,018 pupils of all kinds.** Of those, **122,317 pupils were in day and night schools**, with 3,262 teachers.
- **Alvord says plainly what his own numbers mean, and the book should copy that honesty:** people who went to more than one kind of school are counted more than once, so the total "give[s] the amount of tuition furnished, rather than the exact number of persons taught."
- **The freedpeople paid for their own schools.** **They "sustained wholly or in part 1,094 of the above day and night schools, and own 554 of the school buildings."** The Bureau furnished 635 buildings.
- **They went in the growing season anyway.** Alvord notes that because children were needed to gather the cotton crop, many schools did not open until December — and then adds: "It is noteworthy, however, that the desire to learn carried many schools through all the vacation months." **In July 1869 there were 1,020 schools running; August, 1,048; September, 1,482.** Alabama kept 309 going through the summer, North Carolina 276, Georgia 92, Mississippi 59.
- **What they were studying, from the same report:** 34,320 advanced readers, 27,471 studying geography, 30,749 in arithmetic, 39,437 writing, 5,973 in the higher branches; **3,834 pupils in high and normal schools, training for professions.** Average attendance was **78 percent** of those enrolled.
- **Alvord's own five-year estimate:** because most pupils stayed about a year, "in the five years of this work we have given an elementary education to about one million of pupils," not counting "the tens of thousands of parents and other adults who have learned to read in various ways, especially from these children at home."
- **The Bureau stopped supporting schools in 1870.**
- **Shared with:** `slavery-freedom` leads on what schooling meant to people just freed. This chapter's slice is the schools as institutions — who built them, who paid, who taught, how many, and what was studied in them.

### 7i. The Morrill Acts — the outline's land-grant line, sourced

- **The Morrill Act became law on 2 July 1862.** It gave each state **30,000 acres of public land for each of its senators and representatives in Congress**, issued as land-scrip certificates. States sold the land and used the money to found colleges teaching **agriculture and the mechanic arts**, which opened higher education to farmers' and working people's children. (Source: National Archives, "Morrill Act (1862)," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/morrill-act ; Library of Congress research guide, https://guides.loc.gov/morrill-act .)
- **Say where the land came from.** The bank's era-5 section already makes the point about the Land Ordinance of 1785; the same point applies here and harder. This was land taken from Native nations, sold, and the proceeds used to build colleges for settlers' children.
- **The Second Morrill Act, 1890,** gave money rather than land, and **required a state either to admit Black students to its 1862 land-grant college or to set up a separate land-grant college for them.** Most Southern states set up separate ones. That is where a set of the historically Black colleges and universities come from. (Source: USDA National Institute of Food and Agriculture, "Celebrating the Second Morrill Act of 1890," https://www.nifa.usda.gov/about-nifa/blogs/celebrating-second-morrill-act-1890 ; the text of both acts, https://www.nifa.usda.gov/sites/default/files/2025-04/First%20and%20Second%20Morrill%20Act.pdf .)
- **Alcorn State University in Mississippi, established 1871, is the oldest public historically Black land-grant college.** (Source: NPS, "Reconstruction Era African American Schools in the South.")

### 7j. Compulsory attendance — clears the outline's [VERIFY] on both ends

Already verified and parked in PART B by the `home-family` agent; restated here so the era-7 writer does not have to hunt for it.

- **Massachusetts first, 1852: ages 8 to 14, a minimum of 12 weeks a year, 6 of them consecutive.**
- **32 states by 1900.**
- **All states by 1918, Mississippi last.** The outline's seed guess of "the last state in the 1910s" is right; the year is 1918.
- How hard the early laws were actually enforced was **not** verified in that pass and is still not. Do not assert it.
- (Sources as recorded in PART B: Sage *Encyclopedia of Education Law*, "Compulsory Attendance"; *Boston Magazine*; usedulaw.com.)

### 7k. Teaching becomes women's work — clears the [VERIFY] on the share, and corrects the question

**There is no national "year it crossed."** The first federal count already shows women in the majority, so the crossing happened before anybody was counting nationally, and it happened at different times in different regions. Write it that way.

- **Federal figures, U.S. Department of Education, table 14 of *120 Years of American Education: A Statistical Portrait*, NCES, 1993** (https://nces.ed.gov/pubs93/93442.pdf ), public elementary and secondary classroom teachers, in thousands:
  - **1869-70: 201 total — 78 men, 123 women (61 percent women).**
  - **1879-80: 287 total — 123 men, 164 women (57 percent).**
  - **1889-90: 364 total — 126 men, 238 women (65 percent).**
  - **1899-1900: 423 total — 127 men, 296 women (70 percent).**
  - The report's own text: the proportion of women teachers rose "from 57 percent in 1879-80 to 86 percent in 1919-20," and over that stretch **the number of male teachers fell by 22 percent.**
- **Region by region in 1850, which is where the real story is:** **60 percent of teachers in New England were women; 24 percent in the South; 81 percent in the Midwest.** Between 1870 and 1900 the number of teachers in the country more than tripled. (Source: Breitborde and Kolodny, *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015, p. 33.)
- **Why superintendents liked it, said in the period's own words:** they were "confident that they were hiring knowledgeable, nurturing, young women skilled at managing children, **at half the price of men.**" (Same source, p. 33.) The dollar figures behind that are in §6e.

### 7l. The marriage bar — clears the [VERIFY] by moving it out of this era

**The measured spread of the marriage bar is a twentieth-century fact, not an 1850-1900 one.** Claudia Goldin's study of marriage bars (NBER working paper 2747, "Marriage Bars: Discrimination Against Married Women Workers, 1920's to 1950's," https://www.nber.org/papers/w2747 ) dates the policies "from about the early 1900's to 1950." The figures usually quoted — school boards refusing to keep married teachers rising **from 52 percent in 1928 to 70 percent by 1942**, then falling to a 10 percent retain-bar and an 18 percent hire-bar by 1951 — all belong to eras 8 and 9.
- **So: take the marriage bar out of the 1850-1900 cell.** What is true of this era is that teaching was a job most women left when they married, and that districts expected it; the counted bans come later.
- **Parked forward for the eras 8-10 agent** — see §8-FWD below.

### 7m. The school year and the summer break — clears the [VERIFY], and the disagreement is the point

- **The usual explanation is wrong, and a named historian says so.** **Kenneth Gold**, historian at the College of Staten Island and author of *School's In: The History of Summer Education in American Public Schools* (2002), argues the agrarian story is backwards: **a farming district's calendar was a short winter term and a short summer term**, because children were needed most in **spring planting and autumn harvest** — the very months the modern school year covers. "Summer was the logical time to take off," Gold says, but "the cycles of farming had nothing to do with it." (Source: PBS NewsHour, "Agrarian roots? Think again. Debunking the myth of summer vacation's origins," https://www.pbs.org/newshour/education/debunking-myth-summer-vacation .)
- **City schools ran far more days.** **New York City's schools were open 248 days a year in 1842** — against about 180 today. Attendance was not compulsory; children came when they could. (Same source.)
- **The federal averages for the country as a whole, from NCES table 14:** the **average length of the school term was 132.2 days in 1869-70 and 144.3 days in 1899-1900**, while **the average number of days a pupil actually attended was 78.4 in 1869-70 and 99.0 in 1899-1900.** So the average American child in 1870 was in school for under 80 days a year while New York City's schools had been open 248.
- **What made the modern calendar:** late in the 1800s reformers pushed to make the city and country calendars the same, and the compromise was the long summer break — partly so teachers could be trained in the gap. (Gold, via PBS.)
- **Write it as a disagreement, per `AGENT-BRIEF.md` §3.** The chapter should say the harvest explanation is the one everybody gives, that historians who have looked at the records say it does not fit, and give the two calendars that actually existed.

### 7n. Catholic schools become a system — clears the [VERIFY] on the 1884 council

- **The Third Plenary Council of Baltimore met from 9 November to 7 December 1884.** Present were fourteen archbishops, sixty-one bishops or their representatives, six abbots, and one head of a religious congregation.
- **What it decreed about schools:** it declared the "absolute necessity" of Catholic schools, said that **pastors are obliged to establish them**, said it was desirable that the schools be free, and ordered that **"Parents must send their children to such schools unless the bishop should judge the reason for sending them elsewhere to be sufficient."**
- **A widely repeated claim this pass could NOT confirm from the decree itself:** that every parish was required to build a school **within two years**. The Catholic Encyclopedia's summary of the council's decrees does not carry a time limit. **State the obligation and the date; do not print the two-year clause unless someone reads it in the decree.**
- (Source: *Catholic Encyclopedia*, "Plenary Councils of Baltimore," https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/02235a.htm ; Archdiocese of Baltimore, "The Synods and Councils of Baltimore (1791-1884)," https://www.archbalt.org/the-synods-and-councils-of-baltimore-1791-1884/ .)
- **Shared with:** `religion` leads on the faith argument. This chapter's slice is that a second national school system was built alongside the public one, and why.

### 7o. Two loose ends this pass did not close, stated plainly

- **The boarding-round teacher's diary.** The seed outline asked for a teacher who boarded round and left a diary. **One genuine candidate exists and was not used:** **Arozina Perkins**, sent west by Catharine Beecher's Board of National Popular Education, who kept a diary from **November 1848 to June 1851** covering her training in Hartford and her teaching at Fort Des Moines, Iowa, from **13 November 1850 to 21 March 1851**. It is published in **Polly Welts Kaufman, ed., *Women Teachers on the Frontier*, Yale University Press, New Haven, 1984.** Two reasons she is not the story here: her Iowa teaching falls in **1850-51**, which the project's boundary rule puts in era 7, not era 6; and **this pass did not confirm from the diary itself that she boarded round.** If a later agent wants her, read Kaufman first. **Mary Swift (§6g) fills the era-6 ordinary slot instead, with a published journal that was verified.**
- **The Bray School's 86 named pupils** (the follow-up the eras 3-5 agent flagged as the highest-value one left in eras 1-5) was **not attempted in this pass**, because it belongs to era 5. It is still open.

---

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): Margaret Douglass, jailed in Norfolk in 1854 for teaching free Black children to read

**A named teacher, the named officials who arrested, indicted and sentenced her, and the sentence, all from her own published account, which prints the court documents.** Source: Margaret Douglass, *Educational Laws of Virginia: The Personal Narrative of Mrs. Margaret Douglass, a Southern Woman, Who Was Imprisoned for One Month in the Common Jail of Norfolk, under the Laws of Virginia, for the Crime of Teaching Free Colored Children to Read*, Boston: John P. Jewett, 1854. Read in full at Project Gutenberg, ebook 70331, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/70331/pg70331.txt (also Internet Archive, https://archive.org/details/personalnarrativ1854doug ). Not told in any outline in the book (grep 2026-09-27). It is her own account, written to defend herself, so its judgements are hers; its dates, names and the court papers it reprints are the facts used here.

- **The school.** Margaret Douglass, a white woman in Norfolk, Virginia, and her daughter **Rosa** opened a school for free Black children in **June 1852**, at the request of a Mr. Robinson whose two daughters she had begun to teach. Tuition was **three dollars a quarter**. It had **twenty-five pupils**, boys and girls, and ran about eleven months, openly.
- **The arrest.** **On 9 May 1853, between eight and nine in the morning**, the city constable, **Mr. Cherry**, sent by the mayor, came to the house with a second officer (named later in the narrative as **officer Cox**). They wrote down the names of every child and their parents and marched teacher, daughter and children to **Mayor Simon S. Stubbs**. The mayor read out the Virginia statute, whose maximum penalty he gave as **a fine of one hundred dollars and imprisonment for six months**, and dismissed the children. (A Norfolk *Daily News* report reprinted in the narrative says the officers found "some eighteen or twenty" children there.)
- **The charge.** The grand jury's presentment of **2 June 1853** charged that Margaret and Rosa Douglass "did, on the 9th day of May, 1853, at the City of Norfolk, unlawfully assemble with divers negroes, for the purpose of instructing them to read and to write, and did instruct them to read and to write." The summons is dated **13 July 1853**. Rosa left Norfolk for New York on **29 June** and was not tried.
- **The verdict and sentence.** The jury found her guilty in November 1853 and **fixed the fine at one dollar**. On **10 January 1854 Judge Richard H. Baker** of the Norfolk Circuit Court added jail: "as an example to all others in like cases disposed to offend ... the judgment of the Court is, in addition to the proper fine and costs, that you be imprisoned for the period of one month in the jail of this city." In the same sentence he called her opinions "manifestly mischievous." **She served the month.** (Judge's first name: Zinn Education Project, https://www.zinnedproject.org/news/tdih/woman-jailed-teaching-black-children-to-read/ , search summary; the narrative calls him "Judge Baker." *(first name unconfirmed: search summary only)*)
- **What she says about the double standard:** the Sunday school of Christ Church in Norfolk had been giving Black children primers and teaching them to read for years, and the mayor said such facts "had never been reported to him."
- **Why it belongs in 1850-1900:** it shows the 1831 Virginia law (§6o and the T-261b era-6 patch) enforced on a named white teacher twenty-two years later, and it names every official in the chain.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): attacks on the freedpeople's schools and their teachers, from the Bureau's own reports

**Gap found:** §7h counts the schools and never says that people burned them and whipped their teachers. The outline's freedpeople's span did not either. The Bureau's inspector, John W. Alvord, recorded it himself. Both reports were read as OCR text from the Internet Archive: *Fifth Semi-Annual Report on Schools for Freedmen, January 1, 1868* (https://archive.org/details/fifthsemiannualr00alvo ) and *Ninth Semi-Annual Report, January 1, 1870* (https://archive.org/details/ninthsemiannualr00alvo ).

- **Alvord's own summary (Fifth Report, 1868):** "Teachers were proscribed and ill-treated, school-houses burned, and threats so strong that many schools could not be opened; and others, after a brief struggle, had to be closed." He adds that Southern men who thought the attacks inhuman did not "openly ... defend the schools," and that "only military force for some time to come could prevent the frequent outbreak of every form of violence."
- **Named places and people, Fifth Report:** in the Oxford sub-district of Mississippi, "A Mr. Borum started a school about twelve miles from this place and it was immediately burned." In West Tennessee "a teacher lately disappeared under circumstances exciting the suspicion that he was murdered. He had previously been assaulted in the street." In another district a Welsh clergyman sent to teach at a schoolhouse freedpeople had built was driven from his hotel, then a mob surrounded the Black man's house where he was staying and demanded him, until soldiers arrived; he then lived in a tent inside the army camp under guard.
- **Georgia, Ninth Report (superintendent Brevet Colonel J. R. Lewis), late 1869:** "bands of K. K. K., armed and disguised men, have committed most atrocious outrages." About the end of November **R. H. Gladding**, teaching at Greensboro for the American Missionary Association, was driven away. The white man he boarded with was "taken out of his own house in the night and unmercifully whipped." **Abram Colby**, a Black member-elect of the legislature and one of Gladding's strongest supporters in the school work, "was also taken out of his house and beaten nearly to death." The mayor told Gladding the civil authorities could not protect him. **P. H. Gillen**, a white man at Maxey's Station, Oglethorpe County, was "whipped nearly to death, and forced to leave." Lewis names Warren, Greene, Taliaferro, Wilkes and Elbert counties and says the attacks were "committed by organized gangs, generally in the night."
- **Tennessee, Ninth Report (superintendent Brevet Lieutenant Colonel C. E. Compton):** two Fisk University students ran a school of about fifty pupils at **Dresden, Weakley County**. **On the night of 2 September 1869** "men masked and armed" took them from their boarding house to the woods, "severely whipped" them, and ordered them on pain of death to stop teaching and leave. "Although the attention of the civil authorities was called to this case ... nothing was done in the matter."
- **Delaware, Ninth Report:** the school at **Slaughter Neck** "was burned by some drunken white men"; evidence was given to the state's attorney and the governor was told, "yet nothing was done to bring the parties to justice." Black residents, with the Delaware Association for the Moral Improvement of Colored People and some Bureau money, rebuilt it.
- **South Carolina, Ninth Report:** at **Newberry**, on **24 December 1869**, a man building a large school for freed children "was driven from the hotel at which he was boarding by armed men, for no other reason than that he was building a school house."
- **Alabama, 1870: a teacher killed.** "In July 1870, for example, Klansmen killed six people, including a schoolteacher, at Cross Plains, Calhoun County" (Keith S. Hébert, "Ku Klux Klan in Alabama during the Reconstruction Era," *Encyclopedia of Alabama*, https://encyclopediaofalabama.org/article/h-2934/h-1561 ). The same article: "Klansmen routinely forced out white northern schoolteachers ... They also burned numerous black schools and churches." **The teacher was William Luke**, a Wesleyan preacher from Flesherton, Ontario, who taught freedpeople for Talladega College. He was lynched on **11 July 1870**, "He and seven Black Alabamans were lynched by the Ku Klux Klan," and he left a widow, Fanny Ann (Irwin) Luke, and six children (Owen Sound Hub, reporting the Grey Highlands Peace Committee's 150th-anniversary commemoration, https://owensoundhub.org/news/9773-150th-anniversary-of-lynching-of-william-luke-of-flesherton.html ). **Counts differ: six dead (Encyclopedia of Alabama) or eight (Owen Sound Hub). Give both.** Names of the Black men killed with him appear in search summaries only *(unconfirmed: search summary only)*, and are not recorded here. A book-length account exists: *Death at Cross Plains: An Alabama Reconstruction Tragedy*, University of Alabama Press, 1984 (search summary).
- **Memphis, 1-3 May 1866:** the `slavery-freedom` bank (its era 7, line 'Schools burned') has it and leads on it: "white mobs and police killed 46 Black residents and burned 91 homes, 4 churches, and 8 schools, every Black church and school in the city (the counts are from the congressional investigating committee)," sourced there via Britannica, the Tennessee Encyclopedia and the Zinn Education Project. Not re-checked in this pass. This chapter can say the city's Black schools were burned and point there.
- **The pattern, from the government's own reports:** the attackers are named as armed, disguised or masked men and as "K. K. K."; the victims include white Northern teachers, Black students from Fisk, a Black legislator and the people who boarded teachers; and the reports say three times that the civil authorities did nothing or could not act.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): segregated schools after 1865, and who made and upheld the rule

**Gap found:** the outline's only line is "Schools were separated by race in the South by law and in much of the North by custom or by school-board rule." It names no lawmaker, no court and no year.

- **Tennessee, as an example of the statutes.** The U.S. Commission on Civil Rights, *School Desegregation in Tennessee* (April 2008), pp. 4-5 (https://www.usccr.gov/files/pubs/docs/TNDESEGFULL.pdf ): "The first school segregation act was passed in 1866, one year following the end of the Civil War." The state constitution then barred mixed schools (the report says "in 1869" under article XI; the constitution was ratified in March 1870). **In 1873 the legislature wrote: "white and colored persons shall not be taught in the same school, but in separate schools under the same general regulations as to management, usefulness, and efficiency."** In **1901** it made teaching Black and white students together an offence "punishable by a fine of $50 or imprisonment from 30 to 60 days or both." (The 1866 date comes from the report's citation of a Tennessee State Library timeline, and a search summary gives 1867 for the first public-school law with separation. State "just after the war" or give the report's year with its source.)
- **Georgia's rule appears in the 1899 Supreme Court case** below: the state's 1872 school act told local trustees to give each race the same facilities, in separate schools.
- ***Cumming v. Richmond County Board of Education*, 175 U.S. 528, argued 30 October 1899, decided 18 December 1899.** Read in full at LII, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/175/528 .
  - **Who:** J. W. Cumming, James S. Harper and John C. Ladeveze, Black parents in Augusta, Georgia, sued the **Board of Education of Richmond County** and its tax collector, Charles S. Bohler.
  - **What the board did:** it had run the **Ware High School** for Black students since **1880**. **On 10 July 1897 it closed it**, "for 'purely economic reasons in the education of the negro race,'" and kept supporting high schools for white students (the Tubman High School for white girls, and help to the Hephzibah High School). Its stated reason: "400 or more of negro children were being turned away from the primary grades," and the building that taught 60 high-school pupils would seat 200 younger ones.
  - **What the Court held:** **Justice John Marshall Harlan**, writing for the Court, refused to stop the white high schools' funding. Stopping it "would only be to take from white children educational privileges enjoyed by them, without giving to colored children additional opportunities." He wrote that school funding "is a matter belonging to the respective states," and federal courts could interfere only on "a clear and unmistakable disregard of rights." He declined to rule on separate schools themselves because the parents had not challenged them.
  - `government-politics` tells the case in one paragraph and says "`education` leads on the schools."
- ***Plessy v. Ferguson*, 163 U.S. 537 (1896), on schools** (LII, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/163/537 ): the Court called separate schools "the most common instance" of lawful racial separation, "held to be a valid exercise of the legislative power even by courts of states where the political rights of the colored race have been longest and most earnestly enforced," and cited ***Roberts v. City of Boston*** first, quoting Chief Justice Shaw. It listed state cases upholding separate schools in **Ohio, Missouri, California, Louisiana, New York, Indiana and Kentucky**, and pointed to Acts of Congress requiring separate schools in the District of Columbia. **This confirms the Sarah Roberts story's last line and the outline's "much of the North" line, and names Congress as one of the lawmakers.**
- **San Francisco** (parked by `rights-movements`, PART B): the school district itself ran segregated schools from 1854 and closed its Chinese school in 1870.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): the boarding schools, the two federal reports' figures as they touch this era

- **2022 (Vol. I, Department of the Interior, May 2022, https://www.bia.gov/sites/default/files/dup/inline-files/bsi_investigative_report_may_2022_508.pdf ):** "between 1819 and 1969, the United States operated or supported **408 boarding schools** across 37 states (or then-territories)"; burial sites at "approximately **53** different schools"; "approximately 19 Federal Indian boarding schools accounted for **over 500**" child deaths; and the Department "expects that continued investigation will reveal the approximate number of Indian children who died at Federal Indian boarding schools to be **in the thousands or tens of thousands**." Rules "were often enforced through punishment, including corporal punishment such as solitary confinement; flogging; withholding food; whipping; slapping; and cuffing," and the schools "at times made older Indian children punish younger Indian children."
- **2024 (Vol. II, July 2024, https://www.bia.gov/sites/default/files/media_document/doi_federal_indian_boarding_school_initiative_investigative_report_vii_final_508_compliant.pdf ):** **417** federal schools in 37 states or territories; **1,025** other institutions; "at least **973** documented Indian child deaths occurred across the Federal Indian boarding school system between 1819 and 1969," and "the actual number of children who died ... is greater"; **18,624** children identified by name, a number the Department says is incomplete; **74** marked or unmarked burial sites (53 marked, 21 unmarked) at **65** schools; more than **$23.3 billion** (FY2023 dollars) appropriated 1871-1969. **Read these as system totals for 1819-1969, not as counts for 1850-1900.** Neither report's text gives a total for this era alone. (Its Appendix H is a graph of deaths by year and was not read.)
- **Who made parents send children, in law:** the **Act of 3 March 1893** (27 Stat. 628, 635) let the Secretary of the Interior withhold rations, "including those guaranteed by treaties," from any Indian family whose children aged eight to twenty-one had not attended school the year before (2022 report, pp. 35-36). **The 1891 compulsory-attendance act in PART B (sourced there to a timeline and a legal-history site) was not found in either federal report in this pass.** Use 1893, which the government's report quotes.
- **Carlisle was the first *off-reservation* federal boarding school, not the first federal school for Native children.** Proclamation 10870 (9 December 2024): "the first Federal off-reservation boarding school for Native children." See the era-6 patch for the Civilization Fund (1819) and the Choctaw Academy (1825).
- **Named children who died at Carlisle.** "On May 23, 1881, Chief Spotted Tail and parents from the Rosebud Sioux Tribe requested that the Federal Government return the human remains of Rosebud Sioux Tribe children buried at the Carlisle School." **On 14 July 2021 the Army returned the remains of nine children to the Rosebud Sioux Tribe: Dennis Strikes First (Blue Tomahawk); Rose Long Face (Little Hawk); Lucy Take The Tail (Pretty Eagle); Warren Painter (Bear Paints Dirt); Ernest Knocks Off (White Thunder); Maud Little Girl (Swift Bear); Alvan, aka Roaster, Kills Seven Horses, One That Kills Seven Horses; Friend Hollow Horn Bear; and Dora Her Pipe (Brave Bull).** (Proclamation 10870, Federal Register 89:239, full text https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/full_text/text/2024/12/12/2024-29459.txt ; the same names in the 2024 report, Vol. II.) Not in any outline in the book (grep 2026-09-27). The ages and causes of death of these nine are not given in either source. **SEARCHED, NOT FOUND in this pass (two federal sources only): the nine children's ages and causes of death.** How the prose can say it: "The records printed with their names do not give their ages or what they died of."
- **The outing system, as a federal survey described it in 1928** (Meriam Report, p. 627, quoted in the 2024 report): it "suggests the parole system of a correctional institution," and a Native man who had seen it called it "a kind of peonage which the children must undergo." Peonage means forced labour to work off a debt. Belongs mainly to era 8, but it describes the system Carlisle started.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-261b): the Morrill land, counted

§7i says the land was taken from Native nations but gives no count or names. Robert Lee and Tristan Ahtone, "Land-Grab Universities," *High Country News*, 30 March 2020 (https://www.hcn.org/issues/52-4/indigenous-affairs-education-land-grab-universities/ ):

- "approximately **10.7 million acres** taken from nearly **250 tribes, bands and communities** through over **160 violence-backed land cessions**."
- "To extinguish Indigenous title to land siphoned through the Morrill Act, the United States paid **less than $400,000**."
- The grants "raised **$17.7 million** for university endowments, with unsold lands valued at an additional $5.1 million," for **52 institutions**.
- This is a journalists' reconstruction from land records, not a government count. Say whose count it is.

- Every era-7 story block (Booker T. Washington §7f, Charlotte Forten §7g, Zitkala-Ša §7b, Josephine Foster §7c) and every era-6 block (Horace Mann §6a, Catharine Beecher §6d, Mary Swift §6g, Sarah Roberts §6b) was compared line by line with its bank section. Each key fact is in the bank with a source. One wording fix: the Zitkala-Ša block is correct that her braids were cut at White's Institute, not Carlisle (§7b).
- **The era summary's "More than 180 children died at the first of those schools"** needs "off-reservation": the federal proclamation calls Carlisle the first *off-reservation* federal boarding school. Fixed in the outline.

Left in place so the trail is readable.]** **JOHN DEWEY HAS THE SAME DEFECT MANN, BEECHER AND WASHINGTON HAD.** In `outlines/education.md`, era `1900-1950`, the `hb-story` slug `john-dewey` carries `status="verified"` with a one-line unsourced note and **nothing in the research bank behind it.** The eras 6-7 agent fixed the three in its own eras (§6a, §6d, §7f) and left this one because it is out of scope. **Either source it in the bank or demote it to `target`.** Note that `tools/project_state.py`'s `suspect_verified` check does not catch this: it only flags a verified story whose own text still contains `[VERIFY]`, so an unsourced verified story passes silently.
- **[RESOLVED 2026-09-09 — checked against Goldin directly; see PART D §8m.]** **The marriage bar belongs to you, with numbers.** Claudia Goldin, "Marriage Bars: Discrimination Against Married Women Workers, 1920's to 1950's," NBER working paper 2747 (https://www.nber.org/papers/w2747 ), dates the policies to about 1900-1950. Commonly quoted figures: school boards refusing to keep a woman teacher who married rose **from 52 percent in 1928 to 70 percent by 1942**; by **1951** only **18 percent** had a hire bar and **10 percent** a retain bar. Massachusetts: about **30 percent** of school committees banned married women teachers in **1926**, rising to **42 percent by 1952**. Verify the 1928/1942/1951 figures against Goldin directly before printing.
- **[RESOLVED 2026-09-09 by the era-9 agent — Massachusetts is 1971; see PART E §9l, sourced to Gershoff and Font's dated table. Drop the hedge.]** **Corporal punishment, the modern sequence.** **New Jersey banned it in its public schools in 1867 and stood alone for over a century** (era 7, §7e). **Massachusetts was second — sources give 1971 or 1972, state the disagreement.** Only three more states had banned it by **1977**. *Ingraham v. Wright* (1977) is yours.
- **A school-prayer case fifty years before *Engel v. Vitale*, from a National Park Service source.** **Daniel Freeman — the first person to file a claim under the Homestead Act — sued in 1898 over religious instruction at the Freeman School in Nebraska, and the Nebraska Supreme Court ruled in his favour on 9 October 1902**, forty-five years before the federal courts applied the Establishment Clause to the states. (Source: National Park Service, "An 1872 One-Room Schoolhouse Reveals Secrets at Homestead National Historical Park," https://home.nps.gov/articles/000/an-1872-one-room-schoolhouse-reveals-secrets-at-homestead-national-historical-park.htm .) This is era 8 and it is a good, concrete, small-place opening for the school-prayer thread.
- **Zitkala-Ša's adult life is mostly era 8, and the record is rich:** Society of American Indians secretary from 1916; edited the *American Indian Magazine* 1918-19; the opera *The Sun Dance*, 1913; the report "Oklahoma's Poor Rich Indians," 1924; the Indian Welfare Committee of the General Federation of Women's Clubs, 1924; co-founded the National Council of American Indians in 1926; died 26 January 1938; buried at Arlington. Her story block is in era 7 for her school days. See §7b, and **note the correction there to the 2024 presidential proclamation, which puts her hair-cutting at Carlisle when it happened at White's Indiana Manual Labor Institute in Wabash, Indiana.**
- **Carlisle closed in 1918 and 279 children still there were transferred to other federal boarding schools** (Proclamation 10870, §7a). **The Carlisle Federal Indian Boarding School National Monument was established by proclamation on 9 December 2024** — a 2000-today item.
- **The federal boarding-school system's own numbers, from Proclamation 10870:** the system ran under 34 presidents from Thomas Jefferson to Lyndon Johnson, at today's equivalent of at least **$23 billion**; "nearly 1,000 Native youths died in schools across the system, but the actual number of lives lost is likely much higher." Cross-check against the 2022 and 2024 DOI reports already parked in PART B.
- **The normal schools' later life:** Massachusetts added Salem 1854, the Normal Art School in Boston 1873, Worcester 1874, and Fitchburg, North Adams, Hyannis and Lowell in 1894; **by 1870 there were 39 normal schools in 18 states, and by 1900 there were 180.** They became teachers' colleges and then state universities in the 1900s. (Breitborde and Kolodny, *Historical Journal of Massachusetts*, Winter 2015, pp. 29-30.)

*Written by the era-8 research agent, 2026-09-08. Every fact below carries its source. Where sources disagree, the disagreement is stated and NOT resolved silently. Nine `[VERIFY]` tags stood in this era at the start of this pass; each is answered below, including the two that are answered with a negative.*

**Access note added by this pass (see PART C-ACCESS for the running list):** `si.edu` and `americanhistory.si.edu` now sit behind a "Smithsonian request verification" JavaScript challenge and return **HTTP 403** to WebFetch and to curl. `guides.loc.gov` — which the eras 6-7 agent recorded as working — **now 403s as well.** `supreme.justia.com` 403s; **`law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/<vol>/<page>` works and returns the opinion text**, and that is how the four Supreme Court cases below were read. `oregonencyclopedia.org` 403s. `courtlistener.com` HTML returns an empty 202 and its API needs a key.

## 8. 1900 to 1950

### 8a. John Dewey — the fourth story slot that was tagged `verified` with nothing behind it, now sourced

This is the defect the eras 6-7 agent flagged in PART C-FWD and could not fix, because it sat in era 8. It is fixed here. Every date below is from the **Center for Dewey Studies chronology, Southern Illinois University Carbondale, compiled by Barbara Levine, updated 30 November 2016** (https://deweycenter.siu.edu/_common/documents/chrono.pdf), which cites its own source for each entry.

- **Born 20 October 1859**, at 186 South Willard Street, **Burlington, Vermont**, to Lucina Rich and Archibald Sprague Dewey.
- **A.B., University of Vermont, 25 June 1879.**
- **1879 to June 1881: assistant principal at Oil City High School, Oil City, Pennsylvania**, teaching classics, sciences and algebra. He was nineteen. **This matters for the chapter: the man who argued that children learn by doing started as a high school teacher, and did that before he did any philosophy.**
- **Ph.D., Johns Hopkins University, 5 June 1884.** Then the University of Michigan.
- **Accepted a position at the University of Chicago on 19 March 1894**; announced 2 April 1894.
- **January 1896: the University Elementary School opens.** Dewey was **Director of the Laboratory School from January 1896 to 1904.**
- **5 April 1904: Alice Chipman Dewey resigns as principal of the University Elementary School, effective 1 October 1904 — and Dewey resigns from the University of Chicago the same day**, effective 1 July 1904. Both resignations were submitted to and accepted by the Board of Trustees on 2 May 1904 (Chicago Daily Tribune, 3 May 1904, p. 7). **The school and the Deweys parted from Chicago together, in one afternoon.** He unofficially accepted Columbia's offer on 28 April 1904 and was formally offered a Columbia professorship on 2 May 1904 at **$5,000 a year, starting 1 February 1905.**
- **Professor Emeritus at Columbia from 30 June 1930.**
- **Died of pneumonia, 1 June 1952**; cremated 2 June at Fresh Pond Crematory, Queens, New York.
- Books that matter here: ***The School and Society*, University of Chicago Press, 1899**, and ***Democracy and Education*, 1916.**

**"Learning by doing" is Dewey's own phrase, and it is verified in his own book — do not hedge it, and do not put it in quotation marks as a slogan invented by other people.** In *Democracy and Education* (1916) he writes that recognising how children actually develop "always sets out with situations which involve learning by doing. Arts and occupations form the initial stage of the curriculum." (Verified word for word in the Project Gutenberg plain text, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/852/pg852.txt .)

**His sentence about what schools are for, verified word for word** in *The School and Society* (Cornell University Library copy of the University of Chicago Press printing, https://archive.org/details/cu31924013061050 , OCR text `cu31924013061050_djvu.txt`):

> "What the best and wisest parent wants for his own child, that must the community want for all of its children. Any other ideal for our schools is narrow and unlovely; acted upon, it destroys our democracy."

That is the line to use for the outline's "the argument over what school is for." It is Dewey's answer to it, in his words, and it is short enough to quote whole.

### 8b. The Laboratory School, counted — from the teachers' own account

Source: **Katherine Camp Mayhew and Anna Camp Edwards, *The Dewey School: The Laboratory School of the University of Chicago, 1896-1903*, Appleton-Century, 1936**, pp. 7-8. Both authors taught in the school. Full text: https://archive.org/details/deweyschoolthela008095mbp (OCR text `deweyschoolthela008095mbp_djvu.txt`).

- **Opened January 1896 in a private house, with sixteen pupils and two people in charge.** The first six months were, in their words, "a 'trial-and-error' period and was chiefly indicative of what not to do."
- **Reopened October 1896** at 5718 Kimbark Avenue with **32 children aged six to eleven** and three regular teachers.
- **January 1897:** moved to the old South Park Club House; enrolment **45**.
- **December 1897:** sixteen teachers, **60 children**.
- **October 1898:** moved to 5412 Ellis Avenue; **82 children**; a sub-primary department added for four- and five-year-olds; gymnasium and manual training rooms in a barn joined to the house by a covered way; two science laboratories.
- **Maximum reached: 140 children, 23 teachers and instructors, and about ten graduate assistants.**

Confirming institutional source for the founding and the address: **University of Chicago Library exhibition, "Education for Life: 100 Years of the University of Chicago Laboratory Schools"** (https://www.lib.uchicago.edu/media/documents/exefl-text.pdf ). It gives: Dewey arrived in Chicago **aged 34**; founded the elementary school in **January 1896 in a house on 57th Street**, now 1328 East 57th Street; the student paper *The Maroon* carried news of the opening of the "Dewey School" on **13 January 1896**, reporting that on the first day "each student had completed a paper box for pencils and other materials"; and *The School and Society* "became a best seller in its first year."

**Scale note for whoever writes the prose: this was a school of 140 children at its largest, running for eight and a half years.** Do not let the outline imply that American schools were remade in its image. Say what it was and what it argued, and let the size stand.

### 8c. The classroom made "for listening" — and the one claim in this era this pass could NOT verify

**The outline seed said: "desks bolted to the floor giving way to movable ones [VERIFY]."**

**The negative half, stated plainly: this pass did not find a period source that says school desks were bolted or screwed to the floor, and the claim is NOT printed in the outline.** What was tried: Dewey's *The School and Society* (the words "bolted", "screwed", "fastened" and "nailed" do not occur in it); the Smithsonian's National Museum of American History school-desk objects, including the Moulthrop movable desk — **si.edu and americanhistory.si.edu both 403 behind a verification wall now**; *Modern Schoolhouses* (1919), which discusses fastening gymnasium apparatus to the floor but not desks; and two 1930 school-furniture catalogues on archive.org that have no OCR text. **A later agent with working Smithsonian access should check the Moulthrop Movable Langslow Fowler Desk record, NMAH object nmah_1692390, which is the movable desk itself.**

**The positive half, and it is better than what it replaces, because it is primary.** Dewey opens the second chapter of *The School and Society* by describing the classroom he was arguing against, and he does it through a shop conversation. Verified word for word from the Cornell copy, pp. 47-48:

> "Some few years ago I was looking about the school supply stores in the city, trying to find desks and chairs which seemed thoroughly suitable from all points of view — artistic, hygienic, and educational — to the needs of the children. We had a good deal of difficulty in finding what we needed, and finally one dealer, more intelligent than the rest, made this remark: 'I am afraid we have not what you want. You want something at which the children may work; these are all for listening.'"

And his own description of the room, same pages:

> "the ordinary schoolroom, with its rows of ugly desks placed in geometrical order, crowded together so that there shall be as little moving room as possible, desks almost all of the same size, with just space enough to hold books, pencils and paper"

> "It is all made 'for listening'"

**Use the dealer's line.** It is a real quotation from a real book, it is one sentence, it is funny in a dry way, and it teaches the whole point of the span without a single adjective. It also does not require the unverified bolting claim.

### 8d. The high school becomes ordinary — clears the [VERIFY] on the figures and the years

All figures from **U.S. Department of Education, National Center for Education Statistics, *120 Years of American Education: A Statistical Portrait*, NCES 93-442, January 1993** (https://nces.ed.gov/pubs93/93442.pdf ), read with `pdftotext -layout` and checked line by line. **Table 9** is enrolment; **Table 19** is graduates. These are the federal government's own long series and they settle the span.

**Enrolled in grades 9 to 12, all public and private schools (Table 9, columns 4 and 13; enrolment in thousands; the last column is enrolment as a share of 14- to 17-year-olds):**

- **1900-01: 650,000 — 10.6 per 100 fourteen-to-seventeen-year-olds**
- 1909-10: 1,032,000 — 14.5
- **1919-20: 2,414,000 — 31.2**
- **1929-30: 4,741,000 — 51.1**
- **1939-40: 7,059,000 — 71.3**
- 1943-44: 5,974,000 — 63.0 (the wartime dip; children left school for war work)
- **1949-50: 6,397,000 — 74.5**

**High school graduates (Table 19; thousands; the last column is graduates per 100 seventeen-year-olds):**

- **1899-1900: 95,000 graduates — 6.4 per 100 seventeen-year-olds**
- 1909-10: 156,000 — 8.8
- 1919-20: 311,000 — 16.8
- 1929-30: 667,000 — 29.0
- **1939-40: 1,221,000 — 50.8**
- 1949-50: 1,200,000 — 59.0

**The two sentences the chapter needs, and both are the government's own arithmetic: in 1900 about six children in a hundred finished high school; by 1940 it was about half. Enrolment in the high school grades went from about one in ten of the right-aged children to about seven in ten in forty years.** Note the shape: graduates rose every single decade including through the Depression — 1929-30 to 1939-40 is the single largest jump in the series, from 29.0 to 50.8 per 100. Children stayed in school partly because there were no jobs.

**One more from Table 19 that is worth a clause: girls finished more often than boys, every decade.** 1899-1900: 38,000 boys and 57,000 girls. 1929-30: 300,000 boys, 367,000 girls. 1939-40: 579,000 boys, 643,000 girls.

**Table 14 gives the school year itself**, and it changes in this era too: in **1899-1900** the average term was **144.3 days and the average enrolled pupil actually attended 99 of them**; by **1949-50** the term was **177.9 days and the average pupil attended 157.9.** That is the real change — not the length of the term but how much of it a child was there for.

### 8e. The one-room school ends — clears the [VERIFY] on the count and its source, with the source problem stated

**The federal series does not start early enough to give a 1900 count, and that has to be said rather than papered over.** NCES *120 Years*, **Table 20**, "Public school districts and public and private elementary and secondary schools," begins at **1929-30**. Its one-teacher-school column:

- **1929-30: 149,282 one-teacher public schools**
- 1937-38: 121,178
- 1939-40: 113,600
- 1945-46: 86,563
- 1947-48: 75,096
- **1949-50: 59,652**
- (for the era-9 agent: 20,213 in 1959-60; 1,815 in 1970-71; 617 in 1990-91)

**So within era 8 the count is federal, exact, and falls by about 60 percent: roughly 149,000 in 1929-30 to roughly 60,000 in 1949-50.**

**For 1900 itself:** the figure in general circulation is **about 200,000 one-room schools at the peak, and 212,000 in 1913, with roughly half of American schoolchildren in them.** This pass could not trace that number to a federal or archival source; it appears in local-history and popular accounts, including the Morning Ag Clips piece already cited in PART B of this bank for the two-term calendar. **Handling: give the 1929-30 to 1949-50 federal numbers as the hard figures, and if the ~200,000 peak is used at all, attribute it as a commonly given estimate rather than a count.** Do not print "212,000 in 1913" as a fact with no source behind it. The Clarke Historical Library at Central Michigan University, checked directly, carries no national count.

### 8f. What actually closed them — the school bus, counted

Same NCES report, **Table 20** for districts and **Table 13** for transport.

**School districts (Table 20):** **119,001 in 1937-38** → **101,382 in 1945-46** → **83,718 in 1949-50**. (For later agents: 40,520 by 1959-60 and 17,995 by 1970-71.) **About 35,000 school districts disappeared inside era 8.**

**Pupils carried to school at public expense (Table 13):**

- **1929-30: 1,902,826 pupils — 8.9 percent of average daily attendance**
- 1939-40: 4,144,161 — 18.8 percent
- 1945-46: 5,056,966 — 25.5 percent
- **1949-50: 6,947,384 — 31.2 percent**

**That is the mechanism, and the two tables prove it together: you cannot close a one-room school unless you can carry the children past it.** By 1950 nearly a third of American schoolchildren were riding to school. Average spending per pupil transported was about $31 in 1949-50 (about $166 in 1989-90 dollars, the report's own conversion).

### 8g. Testing arrives in schools — clears three [VERIFY] tags at once, and the chain is documented end to end

The outline seed carried "[VERIFY]" on intelligence testing after the army's wartime tests and "[VERIFY name and date]" on the college entrance test first given in 1926. Both are answered, and the link between them is documented rather than assumed.

**The army tests.** In May 1917 the psychologist **Robert Yerkes** assembled a committee — himself, W. V. Bingham, Henry H. Goddard, T. H. Haines, **Lewis Terman**, Guy Whipple and F. L. Wells — at the Vineland Training School in New Jersey to build a test the Army could give to whole rooms of men at once. **Army Alpha** was for men who could read English; **Army Beta** was the picture version for men who could not. **About 1.75 million soldiers were tested in 1917-18.** (This pass could not reach an institutional page giving the 1.75 million figure directly; it is the standard number and appears in the peer-reviewed literature, including the article on the Army Beta at the NIH's PubMed Central, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6526409/ . **Attribute it as "about 1.75 million" and do not sharpen it.**)

**The school link is not a guess — Terman wrote it down himself, in 1916, before the army tests, in the book that put the intelligence quotient into American schools.** *The Measurement of Intelligence: An Explanation of and a Complete Guide for the Use of the Stanford Revision and Extension of the Binet-Simon Intelligence Scale*, Houghton Mifflin, 1916. Verified word for word from the full text at https://archive.org/details/measurementofint00term (OCR text `measurementofint00term_djvu.txt`), pp. 91-92, writing about two boys he had tested:

> "It is interesting to note that M. P. and C. P. represent the level of intelligence which is very, very common among Spanish-Indian and Mexican families of the Southwest and also among negroes. Their dullness seems to be racial, or at least inherent in the family stocks from which they come."

> "Children of this group should be segregated in special classes and be given instruction which is concrete and practical. They cannot master abstractions, but they can often be made efficient workers, able to look out for themselves. There is no possibility at present of convincing society that they should not be allowed to reproduce, although from a eugenic point of view they constitute a grave problem because of their unusually prolific breeding."

**That is the whole argument of this span in the test-maker's own printed words, and it is why testing and §8h belong in the same cell of the chapter and not in separate ones.** The man whose test American schools adopted wrote, in the manual that told teachers how to give it, that the children it sorted lowest should be put in separate classes and should not be allowed to have children. Note also that Terman is the same man who sat on Yerkes's 1917 committee. The chain runs schoolroom → army → schoolroom.

**Rules for using this quotation, and the decision this pass actually took.** The first paragraph contains a slur that was ordinary printed usage in 1916 and is not now. **What `outlines/education.md` does: it quotes the second paragraph whole — the one about special classes and not being allowed to reproduce, which carries the argument and contains no slur — and states the first paragraph's claim in full, in the book's own voice, naming the groups Terman named and using his word "racial" in quotation marks.** Nothing is softened: the reader is told exactly what he claimed and about whom. The only thing not reprinted is the 1916 word for Black people. **Do not "restore" it without a ruling from Jon, and do not weaken the surrounding sentences instead — the claim itself must stay explicit.** Always say who wrote it, when, and what the book was for. Never use any of its language in the book's own voice.

**The SAT.** The test first given in 1926 was the **Scholastic Aptitude Test**, owned by the **College Entrance Examination Board**, built by a committee headed by **Carl Campbell Brigham** of Princeton. **It was first given on 23 June 1926.** Source: **Rodney Hubin, "Configuring and Administering the First SAT," chapter VI of his dissertation** (https://darkwing.uoregon.edu/~hubin/6_FINAL.pdf ), which opens: "Slightly over eight thousand candidates took the first Scholastic Aptitude Test on June 23, 1926. Although twenty three different colleges received the scores of these candidates, the June date and the experimental nature of the examination meant that 'in most cases decisions regarding admission or rejection were made without reference to the test scores.'" His source for that is **William E.
