## 9a. Brown, and what desegregating a school actually looked like

**The two rulings.** *Brown v. Board of Education of Topeka*, 347 U.S. 483, decided **17 May
1954**, unanimous, opinion by Chief Justice Earl Warren. *Brown II*, 349 U.S. 294, **31 May
1955**, ordered desegregation "with all deliberate speed." (Cornell LII,
https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/347/483 and https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/349/294 .)
**The Warren sequence the era-8 agent parked in PART D-FWD holds**: Warren signed California's
school desegregation bill as governor in 1947, and wrote *Brown* as Chief Justice in 1954.
*Mendez* (1946-47) is era 8 and is already in the outline there.

**How slowly it went, in figures.** A web search offered "1 percent of Black students in the South
in school with whites in 1964, 32 percent by 1968." **This pass could not find those two numbers in
any source it could read, and they are NOT used.** What follows is what was read directly.

- **David J. Armor and Christine H. Rossell, "Desegregation and Resegregation in the Public
  Schools," in Abigail and Stephan Thernstrom, eds., *Beyond the Color Line* (Hoover Institution
  Press, 2002), p. 222** (https://www.hoover.org/sites/default/files/uploads/documents/0817998721_219.pdf ,
  which 301-redirects to https://hoover-s3-website.s3.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/uploads/documents/0817998721_219.pdf ;
  read with `pdftotext -layout`). Their sentence, word for word: **"Ten years after Brown, one study
  estimated that 99 percent of black children in the South were in one-race schools."** Note they
  attribute it to "one study" without naming it in that sentence; print it as an estimate.
- **The Coleman report**, ordered by the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and carried out by James Coleman
  and colleagues in the **1965-66** school year (*Equality of Educational Opportunity*, 1966), is
  summarised by Armor and Rossell on the same page: **nationally 65 percent of all Black students
  were in schools that were over 90 percent Black, and 80 percent of all white students were in
  schools that were over 90 percent white.**
- **Armor and Rossell, Table 1, "Percentage of Black and White Elementary Students in Schools over
  90 Percent Black or White, Southern Cities in 1965-1966"** — percentage of Black elementary
  pupils in schools over 90 percent Black, then white pupils in schools over 90 percent white:
  Mobile, Alabama **100 / 100**; Birmingham **99 / 99**; Jackson, Mississippi **99 / 100**;
  Charleston County, South Carolina **99 / 99**; Atlanta **97 / 95**; **New Orleans Parish 96 /
  84**; Little Rock **96 / 97**; Charlotte-Mecklenburg **96 / 95**; Memphis **95 / 94**; East
  Baton Rouge Parish **95 / 95**; Nashville **82 / 91**; Kansas City, Missouri **69 / 65**. The
  authors call the integration in these cities ten years after *Brown* "clearly nominal for black
  students."
  **The New Orleans line is the one to use next to Ruby Bridges:** five years after she walked into
  Frantz, 96 of every 100 Black elementary pupils in the parish were still in a school that was
  over 90 percent Black.
- **UCLA Civil Rights Project, *Southern Schools More Than a Half-Century After the Civil Rights
  Revolution*, May 2017** (https://civilrightsproject.ucla.edu/wp-content/uploads/2017/05/Brown63_South_052317-RELEASE-VERSION.pdf ,
  read with `pdftotext -layout`): the share of Black students in the South attending schools that
  were 90-100 percent students of color **"dropped sharply, from nearly 80% to 23%, during the
  desegregation era"**, then rose again — 35.8 percent in 2014. Their South = Alabama, Arkansas,
  Florida, Georgia, Louisiana, Mississippi, North Carolina, South Carolina, Tennessee, Texas,
  Virginia. Pre-1991 data is Office for Civil Rights data as analysed in Orfield, *Public School
  Desegregation in the United States, 1968-1980* (1983). **The 2014 figure belongs to era 10.**

**Prince Edward County, Virginia — the county that shut its schools rather than desegregate.**
Sources: Encyclopedia Virginia, "Moton School Strike and Prince Edward County School Closings"
(https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/moton-school-strike-and-prince-edward-county-school-closings/ )
and Virginia Museum of History & Culture, "The Closing of Prince Edward County's Schools"
(https://virginiahistory.org/learn/civil-rights-movement-virginia/closing-prince-edward-countys-schools ).
- **23 April 1951**: Barbara Johns, aged 16, led a walkout of the students at Robert Russa Moton
  High School in Farmville over the condition of their school. That strike became one of the five
  cases decided together as *Brown*.
- Ordered by two courts on **1 May 1959** to integrate, the county did not. On **26 June 1959**
  the Board of Supervisors voted to cut off the money for the public schools. The schools did not
  open in September 1959 and stayed shut for five years.
- **Roughly 1,700 to 2,000 Black children had no school at all.** (Virginia Museum of History &
  Culture gives "approximately 1,750 Black children" locked out in June 1959; Encyclopedia
  Virginia gives "nearly 2,000" denied formal education. **Accounts differ; state the range.**)
  Some went to relatives in other counties or states; some were taught in church basements.
- White children moved into **Prince Edward Academy**, a new private school paid for partly by
  **state-approved tuition grants** and by donations. Same mechanism as the segregation academies
  in §9j.
- The privately organised **Prince Edward Free School Association** ran classes for one year,
  **1963-64**, with Kennedy administration backing and private money: about **1,500 pupils**,
  including four white children, returning to formal classes from **16 September 1963**.
- ***Griffin v. County School Board of Prince Edward County*, 25 May 1964**: the Supreme Court
  ruled the county could not stay closed and ordered the schools reopened. About 1,500 pupils
  came back on **8 September 1964**.
- In **June 2003** Prince Edward County held a symbolic graduation for the people denied school
  between 1959 and 1964, and Virginia apologised and set up a scholarship fund.

**What desegregation cost Black teachers.** Source: **Owen Thompson, "School Desegregation and
Black Teacher Employment," NBER Working Paper 25990**
(https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w25990/w25990.pdf ), summarised in the NBER
Digest, September 2019 (https://www.nber.org/digest/sep19/southern-schools-desegregated-share-black-teachers-declined ).
In **781 Southern districts** across the eleven former Confederate states, the Black share of
teachers fell from **30.6 percent in 1964 to 24.2 percent in 1972** — a drop of 31.8 percent in
the number of Black teachers. The Digest gives the mechanism in the researchers' words: **"As
districts consolidated schools, they disproportionately dismissed African American teachers,"**
while districts **"stepped up recruitment of white teachers, who in many cases were less
experienced."** The districts with the fewest Black pupils cut Black teachers hardest. Of the
teachers who lost their jobs, Thompson estimates **about half moved into less-skilled work and
the rest left the South to teach elsewhere.**

**Busing.** ***Swann v. Charlotte-Mecklenburg Board of Education*, 402 U.S. 1, decided 20 April
1971**, unanimous, opinion by Chief Justice Warren E. Burger (Cornell LII,
https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/402/1 ). The opinion's own numbers: the district had
**more than 84,000 pupils, about 24,000 of them Black (29 percent), in 107 schools**. It was
already running buses on a large scale — in the year before the order, **26,600 pupils were
carried an average of 15 miles one way, an average trip of over an hour**. Under the desegregation
plan elementary pupils would travel **about seven miles, "not over 35 minutes at the most."** The
Court held that ordering buses was within a district judge's power, and that an objection to
busing has force only "when the time or distance of travel is so great as to either risk the
health of the children or significantly impinge on the educational process."

`rights-movements` leads on the campaign that produced *Brown*. **This chapter's angle is what
happened to schooling**: schools closed, Black teachers put out of work, and the bus.

---

## 9b. Ruby Bridges [V — closes the story-block VERIFY]

Jon ruled that Ruby Bridges belongs to `education` rather than to `rights-movements`.

Sources: **National Park Service, "Ruby Bridges"** (https://www.nps.gov/people/rubybridges.htm );
**64 Parishes**, the Louisiana Endowment for the Humanities encyclopedia, "Ruby Bridges"
(https://64parishes.org/entry/ruby-bridges-adaptation ); **National Women's History Museum**
(https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/ruby-bridges ); **NPS, "Louisiana:
Leona Tate Foundation for Change"** (https://www.nps.gov/articles/louisiana-leona-tate-foundation-for-change.htm );
**New Orleans Historical**, a public-history project of the University of New Orleans
(https://neworleanshistorical.org/items/show/1627 ).

- Born **8 September 1954 in Tylertown, Mississippi**. NPS says the family moved to New Orleans
  when she was two; the National Women's History Museum biography says four. **Accounts differ; do
  not print an age for the move.** NPS calls her the eldest of five children; the Women's History
  Museum says eldest of eight. **Accounts differ — say "the eldest child" and stop.**
- **1960**: federal judge **J. Skelly Wright** ordered New Orleans public schools to desegregate a
  grade at a time, starting with first grade (64 Parishes; National Women's History Museum). The
  day usually given is **16 May 1960**, but this pass found it only in journalism (Mississippi
  Today, Verite News), not in an archival source. **Print the year, not the day.**
- The Orleans Parish School Board made Black applicants sit an entrance test and pass background
  checks; white children transferring the other way were asked for neither (64 Parishes). Six
  children passed; two of them stayed at their own school (NPS).
- **14 November 1960.** Ruby Bridges, aged six, went into **William Frantz Elementary School**
  escorted by **four federal marshals**, with her mother Lucille. The same morning **Leona Tate,
  Gail Etienne and Tessie Prevost**, also six, went into **McDonogh No. 19** with marshals. Those
  four were the first Black children in white public schools in Louisiana. (NPS, both pages.)
- A crowd gathered outside and screamed at her. Women who called themselves **the Cheerleaders**
  stood outside both schools day after day, insulted the families, damaged property and tried to
  get sympathetic parents sacked (64 Parishes).
- White parents pulled their children out. **From January to May 1961 Ruby Bridges was the only
  pupil attending Frantz** (64 Parishes). Every teacher but one refused to teach her. **Barbara
  Henry**, a white teacher from Boston, taught her alone for the year (National Women's History
  Museum; New Orleans Historical). **Bridges did not miss a day** (NPS).
- The cost to her family (NPS): her father lost his job; grocery stores refused to serve her
  mother; her grandparents were **evicted from the farm they had sharecropped for 25 years**. The
  Women's History Museum adds that her parents later separated.
- The child psychiatrist **Robert Coles** met her through that year, after a protester threatened
  to poison her lunch, and had her draw and talk about it (64 Parishes).
- **Norman Rockwell** painted the walk to school as *The Problem We All Live With*, 1964
  (64 Parishes). She saw it hanging in the White House on **15 July 2011** (New Orleans
  Historical).
- She finished elementary school at Frantz, worked in travel for about fifteen years, and started
  the **Ruby Bridges Foundation in 1999**. A bronze statue of her was unveiled at the school on
  **14 November 2015** (New Orleans Historical).
- **Movie:** the `movie=` attribute is left **empty**. A 1998 Disney television film called *Ruby
  Bridges* exists, but this pass did not verify it against an institutional source, and
  `AGENT-BRIEF.md` §5 says verify or omit.

**McDonogh No. 19 reopened on 4 May 2022 as the Tate, Etienne, and Prevost Center** (NPS). That is
era 10's if anyone wants it.

---

## 9c. The National Defense Education Act [V — closes "the 1958 act's name"]

The act the outline asks about is the **National Defense Education Act**. **Public Law 85-864, 72
Stat. 1580, signed by President Eisenhower on 2 September 1958.** (National Archives catalogue
description 299869, https://research.archives.gov/description/299869 .)

Eisenhower's signing statement, released at the US Naval Base, Newport, Rhode Island, 2 September
1958 (American Presidency Project,
https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/statement-the-president-upon-signing-the-national-defense-education-act ):
he called it **"an emergency undertaking to be terminated after four years"** and said it would
**"strengthen our American system of education so that it can meet the broad and increasing
demands imposed upon it by considerations of basic national security."** He also noted that
Congress had left out the National Defense scholarships he had asked for. **The statement gives no
cost figure — do not print one from memory.** The act put federal money into teaching science,
mathematics and modern foreign languages, and into loans for college students.

---

## 9d. Prayer, Bible reading, and the Amish case [V — closes three tags]

All three read directly from Cornell LII, which returns full opinion text.

**[V] The 1962 case is *Engel v. Vitale*, 370 U.S. 421** (https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/370/421 ).
Decided **25 June 1962**. Opinion by Justice **Hugo Black**. **Vote 6-1**; Justice Stewart
dissented, and Justices Frankfurter and White took no part. The school board was **Union Free
School District No. 9, New Hyde Park, New York**, and it had directed that each class say a prayer
written by the **New York State Board of Regents** at the start of every day. The prayer, in full,
is 22 words: **"Almighty God, we acknowledge our dependence upon Thee, and we beg Thy blessings
upon us, our parents, our teachers and our Country."** The holding, in the Court's words: **"it is
no part of the business of government to compose official prayers for any group of the American
people to recite as a part of a religious program carried on by government."**

**[V] The 1963 case is *School District of Abington Township v. Schempp*, 374 U.S. 203**
(https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/374/203 ). Decided **17 June 1963**. Opinion by
Justice **Tom C. Clark**. **Vote 8-1**; Justice Stewart alone dissented. Pennsylvania law said:
**"At least ten verses from the Holy Bible shall be read, without comment, at the opening of each
public school on each school day."** In Abington the verses and the Lord's Prayer went out over
the school's intercom. The **Baltimore case *Murray v. Curlett*** was decided with it; a Maryland
rule adopted in **1905** required a King James Bible reading and the Lord's Prayer. The Court held
both unconstitutional, and required of government **"strict neutrality, neither aiding nor opposing
religion,"** whether or not a child could be excused.

**[V] The 1972 Amish case is *Wisconsin v. Yoder*, 406 U.S. 205**
(https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/406/205 ). Decided **15 May 1972**. Opinion by Chief
Justice **Warren E. Burger**; Justice Douglas dissented in part; Justices Powell and Rehnquist took
no part. The parents prosecuted were **Jonas Yoder, Wallace Miller and Adin Yutzy**, of the Old
Order Amish religion and the Conservative Amish Mennonite Church. Their children were **Frieda
Yoder, 15, Barbara Miller, 15, and Vernon Yutzy, 14**. Wisconsin required school to age **16**; the
parents had kept the children out after eighth grade. Each parent was **fined $5**. The holding:
**"The First and Fourteenth Amendments prevent the State from compelling respondents to cause
their children to attend formal high school to age 16."**

`religion` leads on the faith argument in all three. **This chapter's angle is the school day** —
what a child did at 9 a.m. before and after 1962, and how long a child had to stay in school.

**Note for whoever writes the prose:** the era-8 agent left an unused Nebraska school-prayer item
in PART C-FWD (Daniel Freeman sued in 1898 over religious instruction at the Freeman School; the
Nebraska Supreme Court ruled for him on 9 October 1902, source NPS) and asked the era-9 agent to
decide where it goes. **Decision: leave it in era 8's bank and do not open the thread here.** Era 9
already has three sourced cases, and adding a fourth from a different era would make the span a
list of cases rather than an account of the school day.

---

## 9a-2. Little Rock, 1957-1959

Sources: **NPS, "Crisis Timeline," Little Rock Central High School National Historic Site**
(https://www.nps.gov/chsc/learn/historyculture/timeline.htm ) and **NPS, "The Little Rock Nine"**
(https://www.nps.gov/people/the-little-rock-nine.htm ).

**Warning about the names.** The NPS timeline page, read through a summarising fetch, returned
**"Jane Lee Hill"** in the list of nine. **That is wrong and must not be printed.** The NPS
biography page gives the nine as **Elizabeth Eckford, Minnijean Brown, Ernest Green, Thelma
Mothershed, Gloria Ray, Jefferson Thomas, Carlotta Walls, Terrence Roberts and Melba Pattillo.**
Use that list.

- **2 September 1957**: Governor **Orval Faubus** said on television that he had called out the
  Arkansas National Guard to "preserve order" at Central High.
- **3 September**: the school year began with Guard soldiers around the building. No Black student
  tried to go in; Superintendent Virgil Blossom had asked them to stay away.
- **4 September**: the nine tried to enter. **Elizabeth Eckford arrived alone**, not knowing the
  others were being escorted, and met a crowd of **more than 300 people**. Guard soldiers refused
  to let her in. She walked to a bus stop through the crowd. The other eight came with ministers
  arranged by **Daisy Bates** and were turned away by Lt. Col. Marion Johnson on Faubus's orders.
- **20 September**: federal judge **Ronald Davies** ruled that Faubus had used soldiers to stop
  integration rather than to prevent violence, and ordered him to stop. Faubus pulled the Guard
  out that evening.
- **23 September**: a crowd of **more than 1,000** white people surrounded the school while city
  police took the nine in. Four Black journalists — **Alex Wilson, James Hicks, Moses J. Newsom
  and Earl Davy** — were attacked by the crowd. After three and a half hours school staff took the
  nine out through a side door.
- **24 September**: Mayor **Woodrow Mann** telegraphed President Eisenhower: "mob is armed",
  "situation is out of control."
- **25 September**: Eisenhower federalised the Arkansas National Guard and sent **1,000 soldiers of
  the 101st Airborne Division**. At **9:22 a.m.** the nine went in the front doors with **more than
  20 paratroopers**, over 350 soldiers around the building and an Army helicopter overhead.
- **3 October**: vice principal Elizabeth Huckaby stopped white students harassing the nine; about
  **150 students walked out**, 70 names were taken and all 70 were suspended.
- **24 October**: the nine went in the front door without a federal escort for the first time.
  **18 November**: the last 101st Airborne soldiers left.
- **17 December**: Minnijean Brown dropped chili on two white boys who had blocked her way in the
  cafeteria and was suspended until 13 January 1958. **15 January**: white students poured chili on
  her and were expelled. **17 February 1958**: the school board expelled Minnijean Brown for the
  rest of the year after she called a student "white trash", and suspended three white students,
  including boys wearing badges reading **"One Down and Eight to Go."** She finished school in New
  York.
- **27 May 1958**: **Ernest Green** became the first African American to graduate from Central High.
  (The NPS people page says "May 1958" without a day; the timeline page gives 27 May. Use 27 May
  and attribute it to the timeline.)
- **21 June 1958**: judge Harry Lemley granted a delay of integration until January 1961, saying it
  was too early for Black students to "enjoy" their constitutional rights. **12 September 1958**:
  the Supreme Court reversed him.
- **15 September 1958**: Faubus ordered **all four Little Rock high schools closed** to avoid
  integration. Arkansas legislators had given him laws to do it. On **27 September** the city voted
  **19,470 to 7,561** against integration and the schools stayed shut for the school year. This is
  called **the Lost Year**. **NPS does not give the number of children shut out — do not print
  one.**
- **May 1959**: segregationist board members voted not to renew the contracts of **44 teachers and
  administrators** thought to favour integration. **25 May 1959**: the Women's Emergency Committee
  to Open Our Schools won a recall election and removed three segregationists from the board.
- **12 August 1959**: the high schools reopened nearly a month early.

`rights-movements` leads on the campaign and on Daisy Bates. **This chapter's angle: what a school
year was like inside the building, and the year with no school at all.**

---

## 9a-3. Massive Resistance in Virginia, and what the state did about it afterwards

**Prince Edward was not the only Virginia locality that closed schools.** The Commonwealth's own
scholarship programme names **five**: **Arlington, Charlottesville, Norfolk, Prince Edward County
and Warren County**, "where the public schools were closed to avoid desegregation" between
**1954 and 1964**. (Commonwealth of Virginia, Brown v. Board of Education Scholarship Program,
official fact sheet, https://brownscholarship.virginia.gov/pdf/meetings/brown-fact-sheet.pdf ,
read with `pdftotext -layout`.) The other four closed for months, not years; **Prince Edward is the
one that stayed shut for five years.**

**Correction to what a first pass would print.** Encyclopedia Virginia's summary says "the state of
Virginia apologized." **The state did not apologise.** In **2003** the General Assembly passed a
resolution expressing **"profound regret"**, and *Education Week*'s report of it says the wording
stopped short of an apology because some legislators thought an apology could lead to demands for
payment. (*Education Week*, "Va. Expresses 'Regret' for Closures Aimed at Resisting
Desegregation," February 2003,
https://www.edweek.org/leadership/va-expresses-regret-for-closures-aimed-at-resisting-desegregation/2003/02 .)
**Use "regret", not "apology", and say why the word was chosen.**

**The Brown v. Board of Education Scholarship Program and Fund**, from the state fact sheet: created
by the Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr. Memorial Commission and passed by the **2004** General Assembly,
re-passed in **2005** to fix defects. It pays tuition for people who were in, or were due to start,
Virginia public schools in those five places between 1954 and 1964 and could not finish — for a
GED, an adult high school diploma, technical training, or a two- or four-year degree. **Funding:
$50,000 from the 2004 General Assembly; then the philanthropist John Kluge gave $1 million on
condition the General Assembly matched it, which it did, bringing the fund to $2,050,000.**

The fact sheet also uses the state's own phrase for the schools white families moved to: **"a
private academy or foundation, in- or out-of-state, established to circumvent desegregation."**
That is the segregation-academy item (§9j) in the Commonwealth's own words.

---

## 9e. Federal money: ESEA and Head Start, 1965 [V — closes "federal school aid and Head Start, 1965"]

**The Elementary and Secondary Education Act of 1965.** H.R. 2362, **Public Law 89-10, 79 Stat.
27**. President Johnson signed it on **11 April 1965 at 4:20 p.m. on the front lawn of the former
Junction Elementary School, Johnson City, Texas**, the one-room school he had started at aged four.
His first teacher, **Kate Deadrich Loney**, sat beside him. (American Presidency Project, "Remarks
in Johnson City, Tex., Upon Signing the Elementary and Secondary Education Bill,"
https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/remarks-johnson-city-tex-upon-signing-the-elementary-and-secondary-education-bill ;
LBJ Library, https://www.lbjlibrary.org/object/text/remarks-signing-elementary-education-bill-04-11-1965 .)

From the remarks, in Johnson's words:
- **"I started school when I was 4 years old, and they tell me, Miss Kate, that I recited my first
  lessons while sitting on your lap."**
- **"By passing this bill, we bridge the gap between helplessness and hope for more than 5 million
  educationally deprived children."**
- **"As a son of a tenant farmer, I know that education is the only valid passport from poverty."**
- **"I believe deeply no law I have signed or will ever sign means more to the future of America."**
- He said the bill would put **"more than 30 million new books"** into children's hands.
- The votes: **263-153 in the House, 73-18 in the Senate.**

**Title I** is the part that sends money to schools with poor children. An interim report made
public on **30 December 1965** by Commissioner Francis Keppel covered the first 100 Title I
projects and said they were reaching **347,047 children in 12 states**. (Same source set.)

**Project Head Start.** Sargent Shriver, director of the **Office of Economic Opportunity**, put
together the group that designed it. Johnson announced it on **18 May 1965** (American Presidency
Project, "Remarks on Project Head Start,"
https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/remarks-project-head-start ). His figures that day:
about **530,000 children**, **2,500 projects**, **11,000 child development centres**, **$112
million**.

On **31 August 1965** he announced plans to extend it (American Presidency Project,
https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/remarks-announcing-plans-extend-project-head-start ).
The figures that day: **more than 500,000 children that summer, nearly 560,000 in total, 13,400
centres, 2,500 communities**, with **nearly a million parents** and **half a million volunteers**,
including 100,000 teachers, doctors and dentists. He announced year-round centres expected to take
**350,000 children** at first, and "follow-through" classes and home visits.

**Do not print "eight weeks."** That length is in general circulation but `acf.gov` (the Office of
Head Start's own history page) returns **HTTP 403**, and neither presidential document states a
number of weeks. Say "a summer programme."

Head Start was not only lessons. Johnson's 18 May remarks describe **"preschool training to prepare
them for regular school in September"** plus **"medical and dental attention that they badly need"**
and counselling for parents.

---

## 9f. Language in the classroom [V — closes the *Lau v. Nichols* tag]

**The Bilingual Education Act, 1968.** Introduced by **Senator Ralph Yarborough of Texas** in 1967;
signed by President Johnson on **2 January 1968** as **Title VII of the Elementary and Secondary
Education Act** (Public Law 90-247). It was the first federal law that put money behind teaching
children who did not speak English. Yarborough's stated reason was that Spanish-speaking children
in Texas finished about **four years less schooling** than Anglo children.
**Sourcing warning: this pass could only reach secondary summaries for these facts.** The federal
NCELA paper *The Bilingual Education Act: Twenty Years Later* (Gloria Stewner-Manzanares, Fall 1988,
https://ncela.ed.gov/sites/default/files/legacy/files/rcd/BE021037/Fall88_6.pdf ) is the right
primary-ish source and **the PDF would not convert on this pass**. **Print only the signing date and
the fact that it was Title VII of ESEA; leave the four-years figure out unless a later agent reads
the NCELA paper.**

**[V] *Lau v. Nichols*, 414 U.S. 563, decided 21 January 1974** (Cornell LII,
https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/414/563 ). Opinion by **Justice William O. Douglas**;
every justice agreed on the result, several concurring separately. The district was the **San
Francisco Unified School District**. The Court's own numbers: about **2,856 pupils of Chinese
ancestry did not speak English**; about **1,000 were getting extra English lessons**; that left
about **1,800 with none**. The holding, in the Court's words: the failure to provide English
instruction to those roughly 1,800 pupils **"denies them a meaningful opportunity to participate in
the public educational program and thus violates § 601 of the Civil Rights Act of 1964."** The named
plaintiff, **Kinney Kinmon Lau**, was a schoolchild.

**Note on what this case is and is not.** *Lau* did not order bilingual teaching. It said a school
may not put a child in a class conducted only in a language the child does not understand and call
that an equal education.

---

## 9g. A Nation at Risk, 1983 [V — closes the "1983 report" tag]

Full title: ***A Nation At Risk: The Imperative For Educational Reform. An Open Letter to the
American People. A Report to the Nation and the Secretary of Education.*** By the **National
Commission on Excellence in Education**, chaired by **David P. Gardner**. Published **April 1983**.
Read from the ERIC full text, **ED226006** (https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED226006.pdf ), with
`pdftotext -layout`; the scan's OCR mangles some letters, and the quotations below are given in
their published spelling.

- **Secretary of Education T. H. Bell created the commission on 26 August 1981.** The report was
  "the result of 18 months of study."
- Its two most-quoted sentences: **"the educational foundations of our society are presently being
  eroded by a rising tide of mediocrity that threatens our very future as a Nation and a people"**;
  and **"If an unfriendly foreign power had attempted to impose on America the mediocre educational
  performance that exists today, we might well have viewed it as an act of war. As it stands, we
  have allowed this to happen to ourselves."** It goes on: **"We have, in effect, been committing an
  act of unthinking, unilateral educational disarmament."**
- Its "Indicators of the risk," from the report itself:
  - On 19 international academic tests American students **"were never first or second and, in
    comparison with other industrialized nations, were last seven times."**
  - **"Some 23 million American adults are functionally illiterate by the simplest tests of everyday
    reading, writing, and comprehension."**
  - **"About 13 percent of all 17-year-olds in the United States can be considered functionally
    illiterate. Functional illiteracy among minority youth may run as high as 40 percent."**
  - Average achievement on most standardised tests was **lower than 26 years earlier, when Sputnik
    was launched.**
  - **SAT scores fell in an almost unbroken line from 1963 to 1980: average verbal down over 50
    points, average mathematics down nearly 40 points.**
  - Of 17-year-olds, **nearly 40 percent could not draw inferences from written material, only about
    one-fifth could write a persuasive essay, and only about one-third could solve a mathematics
    problem needing several steps.**
  - **Between 1975 and 1980 remedial mathematics courses in public four-year colleges rose 72
    percent**, and made up a quarter of the mathematics taught in them.

**Handling note.** The report is an argument, not a neutral measurement, and several of its claims
were disputed afterwards. **Attribute the numbers to the commission rather than stating them as
findings of fact**, and say plainly that this report is what started the modern argument about
school standards.

---

## 9h. College, and the start of student debt

**Enrollment.** NCES *Digest of Education Statistics* **Table 303.10** (total fall enrollment in
degree-granting postsecondary institutions,
https://nces.ed.gov/programs/digest/d20/tables/dt20_303.10.asp ):
**1950: 2,281,298. 1960: 3,639,847. 1970: 8,580,887. 1980: 12,096,895. 1990: 13,818,637. 2000:
15,312,289.** So college enrollment rose about **6.7 times over the era**, and nearly all of the
steepest rise is in the 1960s.

**Cost.** NCES *Digest* **Table 330.10** (average undergraduate tuition, fees, room and board for
full-time students, https://nces.ed.gov/programs/digest/d20/tables/dt20_330.10.asp ). Public
four-year institutions, **current dollars then constant 2019-20 dollars**: 1963-64 **$929 /
$7,745**; 1970-71 **$1,326 / $8,586**; 1980-81 **$2,550 / $7,573**; 1990-91 **$5,243 / $10,070**;
1999-2000 **$8,274 / $12,572**. Private four-year: 1963-64 **$1,810 / $15,093**; 1980-81 **$5,594 /
$16,610**; 1999-2000 **$20,737 / $31,509**. **Use the constant-dollar column** — it shows the real
rise, about **62 percent at public four-year colleges** and **more than double at private ones**,
and it shows the dip in the 1970s that the current-dollar column hides.

**The loans.** Federal student lending starts in this era. The National Defense Education Act of
1958 (§9c) created student loans. **The Higher Education Act of 1965**, signed at **Strahan
Gymnasium, Southwest Texas State College, San Marcos, Texas, on 8 November 1965** (American
Presidency Project,
https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/remarks-southwest-texas-state-college-upon-signing-the-higher-education-act-1965 ),
added need-based scholarships of up to **$1,000 a year**, part-time campus jobs worth about **$400
a year**, and government-guaranteed loans with nothing to repay until after graduation. Johnson said
it would reach **1 million of the 1.3 million high school graduates who had not gone to college**,
and that **140,000 young people** would be in college the next year who otherwise would not.
In the same speech he described teaching at the Welhausen Mexican School in Cotulla, Texas for
**$125 a month** after his second year of college, and said: **"I shall never forget the faces of
the boys and the girls in that little Welhausen Mexican School, and I remember even yet the pain of
realizing and knowing then that college was closed to practically every one of those children
because they were too poor."**

**What borrowing looked like by the end of the era.** NCES 2005-170, **Susan P. Choy and Xiaojie
Li, *Debt Burden: A Comparison of 1992-93 and 1999-2000 Bachelor's Degree Recipients a Year After
Graduating*, March 2005** (https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2005/2005170.pdf , read with `pdftotext
-layout`), Executive Summary: the share of bachelor's degree recipients who had borrowed from any
source rose **from 49 percent in 1992-93 to 65 percent in 1999-2000**, and among borrowers the
average amount borrowed rose **from $12,100 to $19,300 in constant 1999 dollars**. Among students
from the highest-income families the share who borrowed **roughly doubled, from 24 to 46 percent**.

## 9i. Title IX, 1972

**Public Law 92-318, signed 23 June 1972**, part of the Education Amendments of 1972; renamed the
**Patsy Takemoto Mink Equal Opportunity in Education Act** in 2002. Text, from the Department of
Justice's Civil Rights Division page
(https://www.justice.gov/crt/title-ix-education-amendments-1972 ) and 20 U.S.C. § 1681
(https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/20/1681 ): **"No person in the United States shall, on the
basis of sex, be excluded from participation in, be denied the benefits of, or be subjected to
discrimination under any education program or activity receiving Federal financial assistance."**

**`rights-movements` leads on Title IX.** The outline says so. **This chapter should give it one
sentence and move on** — the school-sports story is not this chapter's, and this pass did not
research it.

---

## 9f-2. The "No Spanish" rule — a federal report, and the children's own words

**This is the strongest single source found in this pass, and it is a federal document.**

**U.S. Commission on Civil Rights, *The Excluded Student: Educational Practices Affecting Mexican
Americans in the Southwest*, Mexican American Education Study, Report III, May 1972.** Read in full
from the Thurgood Marshall Law Library's scan of the Commission's historical publications,
https://www2.law.umaryland.edu/marshall/usccr/documents/cr12m573rp3.pdf , with `pdftotext -layout`.
Also ERIC ED062069. The study surveyed school districts in **Arizona, California, Colorado, New
Mexico and Texas** with enrollments more than 10 percent Spanish-surnamed.

**How common the rule was.** Of a projected total of **5,800 schools** in the survey area, the
policies of **about one-third discouraged the use of Spanish in the classroom**. **About 15 percent
of the total** discouraged it on the schoolgrounds as well.

**Texas was the worst.** **Two-thirds of all surveyed Texas schools discouraged Spanish in the
classroom**, and slightly more than one-third on the schoolgrounds — at least twice the rate of
most other states. **California was the least: fewer than one-fifth of schools in the classroom**,
and the rule was rare on California playgrounds. The other three states ran about one-third in the
classroom and one-tenth on the grounds.

**Who got it.** "Five in every 10 schools serving poor barrios responded that they have a 'No
Spanish' rule in the classroom." In schools with high-income families and few Mexican American
pupils, **about 15 percent** did.

**It was a criminal offence in Texas.** The report: "Texas continues to go so far as to make it a
crime to speak Spanish in ordinary school activities. As recently as October 1970 a Mexican American
teacher in Crystal City, Texas was indicted for conducting a high school history class in Spanish,
although this case was subsequently dismissed." (Report's footnote 17: interview with Jesse Gamez,
San Antonio attorney for the defendant.)

**How schools enforced it** — the Commission's own table, "Methods of Correction / Percent of
Schools" (schools could give more than one, so the figures do not add to 100):
- Suggesting that staff correct those who speak Spanish — **48**
- Requiring staff to correct those who speak Spanish — **12**
- Encouraging English — **10**
- Advising students of the advantages of speaking English — **9**
- Encouraging other students to correct Spanish speakers — **7**
- **Punishing persistent Spanish speakers — 3**
- Miscellaneous means of correction — **11**

**"None of the school principals or staff who responded to the survey admitted to using corporal
punishment as a means of dealing with children who spoke Spanish in school. However, at least 3
percent of the schools did admit to actual discipline of the pupils involved."** Allegations of
physical punishment **were** heard by the Commission at its **December 1968 hearing in San
Antonio**. **State it exactly that way: the schools did not admit hitting children for it, and
witnesses at the hearing said they did.**

**El Paso.** At the San Antonio hearing a principal of "a highly segregated Mexican American school
in El Paso, Texas" testified that children caught speaking Spanish during school hours were sent to
a **Spanish detention class for an hour after school**. The report reproduces the printed
**violation slip** used to send a child there (Figure 4, "Texas, 1968").

**The children's own words.** The report prints extracts from essays by **one class of seventh-grade
Mexican American students in Texas, written in October 1964**, describing their elementary schools.
(Report's footnote 20: communication to the Commission from **Alonzo Perales, Texas teacher, 1965**.)
The OCR of the scan garbles one figure — "pay 54" is "pay 5¢" — so print it as five cents.
- "If we speak Spanish we had to pay 5¢ to the teacher or we had to stay after school . . ."
- "In the first through the fourth grade, if the teacher caught us talking Spanish we would have to
  stand on the 'black square' for an hour or so. . . ."
- "When I was in elementary they had a rule not to speak Spanish but we all did. If you got caught
  speaking Spanish you were to write three pages saying, 'I must not speak Spanish in school'. . . ."
- "In the sixth grade, they kept a record of which if we spoke Spanish they would take it down and
  charge us a penny for every Spanish word. If we spoke more than one thousand words our parents
  would have to come to school and talk with the principal. . . ."
- "If you'd been caught speaking Spanish you would be sent to the principal's office or given extra
  assignments to do as homework or probably made to stand by the wall during recess and after
  school. . . ."

**These are unnamed children.** Under `hard-subjects-policy.md` §4 they belong in `hb-zoom` prose,
not in an `hb-story` block. Do not attach a name to any of these quotations.

**Texas law.** The Texas State Historical Association's *Handbook of Texas*, "Bilingual Education"
(https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/bilingual-education ): an English-only teaching
requirement dating back to **1918**; in **1969** the 61st Texas Legislature made bilingual teaching
permitted where it helped, but left it to districts; **on 3 June 1973 Governor Dolph Briscoe signed
S.B. 121**, requiring bilingual teaching in elementary schools with 20 or more limited-English
children in a grade, which "abolished the English-only teaching requirement." **So the no-Spanish
rule was lawful in Texas for 55 years, and the Commission's survey caught it in its last years.**

**The Blackwell School, Marfa, Texas — a National Park Service site.** NPS, *Blackwell School
National Historic Site Foundation Document*, May 2025
(https://www.nps.gov/blsc/learn/management/upload/BLSC_FD_508_2025-English1.pdf , read with
`pdftotext -layout`). Marfa opened its first public school in 1885 for all children; in 1892 the
district built a new school and put the white children in it, leaving the Mexican American children
in the old 1885 building — "School officials claimed Marfa students of Mexican descent had
English-language deficiencies that necessitated separate, specialized instruction." The **Marfa
Mexican School opened in September 1909**; it later took the name Blackwell. NPS says such Mexican
schools "eventually operated in over 120 school districts in 59 counties across Texas," and that
their curriculum aimed "to assimilate students into the Anglo-dominant American culture and promote
the English language through a 'no Spanish in the classroom' rule." **Blackwell closed in 1965**,
the year Marfa built an integrated elementary school and moved the children into it. Listed on the
National Register in **2019**; **designated a national historic site in October 2022**; **established
in July 2024** when the property was transferred to the federal government.

**Not used, and why.** A StoryCorps oral history broadcast by NPR in October 2017 describes a
"burial of Mr. Spanish" ceremony at Blackwell, in which children wrote Spanish words on slips of
paper and buried them in a cigar box. **NPR timed out twice on this pass and the NPS foundation
document does not mention the ceremony.** Do not print it until someone reads the NPR transcript
(https://www.npr.org/transcripts/558739863 ) or the Blackwell School Alliance's own account.

---

## 9f-3. Hawaiian, and Native languages, in this era

**ʻAha Pūnana Leo** official history (https://www.ahapunanaleo.org/history-hl-1 ):
- An **1896** law required English instruction in Hawaii's schools, and in practice children were
  stopped from speaking ʻōlelo Hawaiʻi at school.
- **January 1983**: Hawaiian-language educators met to plan how to keep the language alive.
- **August 1984**: the first Pūnana Leo preschool opened at **Kekaha, Kauaʻi**. *Pūnana leo* means
  "nest of voices."
- **1986**: they worked with the Hawaii State Legislature to pass a bill **removing the 90-year ban
  on teaching in Hawaiian** in public and private schools.
- **1987**: the first elementary indigenous-language immersion classes in the United States, at
  **Keaukaha Elementary in Hilo** and **Waiau Elementary in Pearl City**.

**Already in this bank from `native-nations` (PART B) and usable here:** the **Kennedy Report**,
Senate Report 91-501, *Indian Education: A National Tragedy — A National Challenge*, **November
1969**, which called the policy "coercive assimilation" and asked for Native control of Native
schools; **Navajo Community College, 1968**, the first tribally controlled college, now Diné
College; the **Indian Self-Determination and Education Assistance Act, P.L. 93-638, January 1975**,
which let nations contract to run their own schools; and **boarding-school enrollment peaking at
about 60,000 in 1973** and falling as nations took the schools over. The **Wôpanâak Language
Reclamation Project** began in **1993**.

**The boarding schools do not end in era 8.** The era-8 agent's close was that the government read
the Meriam Report in 1928 and the schools went on. **Era 9's fact is that enrollment peaked in 1973,
45 years after that report** — the system was at its largest inside living memory of today's
readers' grandparents.

---

## 9k. Teaching as a job [V — closes both Teaching tags]

**[V] Teacher unions and the first large strikes.**

- **7 November 1960, New York City.** The first teachers' strike in the city's history, a one-day
  walkout held on Election Day, called by the newly formed **United Federation of Teachers**. *Time*
  reported at the time that the UFT "claims one-quarter of the 39,000 teachers in the nation's
  biggest urban public school system" (*Time* archive,
  https://time.com/archive/6811844/education-teachers-strike/ ). New York teachers voted for
  collective bargaining in **June 1961** and chose the UFT as their bargaining agent in **December
  1961** — the first big-city teachers' union in the country to win collective bargaining.
  **Sourcing note: `uft.org` returns HTTP 403 to WebFetch**, so the union's own account could not be
  read. Figures above are journalism of the period plus secondary summaries; **label them as such
  and do not add detail this pass did not read.**
- **19 February 1968, Florida — the first statewide teachers' strike in the United States.** More
  than **27,000 teachers and administrators** in the Florida Education Association handed in
  resignation letters and walked out, after Governor **Claude Kirk** refused the association's
  demands on salaries and the school budget. It ended on **8 March 1968** when the State Board of
  Education and the FEA agreed on more money for schools. **Thousands of the teachers were then
  refused rehire because they had struck.** (Zinn Education Project,
  https://www.zinnedproject.org/news/tdih/florida-teacher-strike/ , which cites **Jody Noll, "'We
  Are Not Hired Help': The 1968 Florida Statewide Teacher Strike and the Formation of Modern
  Florida," *Florida Historical Quarterly* 95, no. 3 (2017)**. **`floridamemory.com` returns HTTP
  403**, so the State Archives of Florida's own page could not be read; a later agent should try it
  by another route.)

**[V] Teacher pay against other jobs needing a degree.**

- **The government's own salary series.** NCES *Digest* **Table 211.60**, estimated average annual
  salary of public elementary and secondary teachers
  (https://nces.ed.gov/programs/digest/d20/tables/dt20_211.60.asp ). Current dollars, then constant
  2019-20 dollars: **1969-70 $8,626 / $58,739; 1979-80 $15,970 / $52,915; 1989-90 $31,367 /
  $63,544; 1999-2000 $41,807 / $63,523.** **Read the constant-dollar column:** real teacher pay
  *fell* about 10 percent across the 1970s, recovered through the 1980s, and was **flat through the
  1990s** — $63,544 in 1989-90 against $63,523 in 1999-2000. The table starts at 1969-70; **it has
  no 1959-60 row.**
- **The comparison the outline asks for.** **Economic Policy Institute, Sylvia Allegretto, *The
  teacher pay penalty reached a record high in 2024*, 24 September 2025**
  (https://files.epi.org/uploads/The-teacher-pay-penalty.pdf , read with `pdftotext -layout`;
  `epi.org` HTML pages return HTTP 403 but the `files.epi.org` PDF works). Method, in her words: she
  compares the weekly wages of public school teachers with those of **other college graduates**,
  using regression "to control for systematic differences in age, education, state of residence, and
  other factors known to affect wage rates," on Current Population Survey data.
  Her Table 1, teacher **weekly wage penalty**: **1979 −7.3 percent; 1993 −5.1 percent; 2004 −12.8
  percent.** Her text gives **1996 at −6.1 percent**, which she also states as teachers earning
  **93.9 cents on the dollar** compared with similar college graduates.
  **For this era, the honest statement is: through the 1980s and mid-1990s a teacher earned about
  five to seven percent less a week than other college graduates, and the gap began to widen
  sharply after 1996.** The record-high figures (−26.9 percent in 2024) belong to era 10.

## 9l. Hitting children at school — the Supreme Court says yes, and then the states say no one at a time [V — closes four tags: the case name and year, the first state ban in the 1800s, the first modern ban 1971, and the sequence]

**Written 2026-09-09 by the era-9 finishing agent.** This closes the outline's `Discipline ends, slowly`
span, which carried four `[VERIFY]` tags.

**What corporal punishment in school was, in plain words.** An adult who worked at the school hit a
child on purpose, to hurt the child, as a punishment for something the child had done. In American
schools the usual method was a wooden board — a paddle — swung against the child's buttocks. Gershoff
and Font's definition (below): "the use of physical force with the intention of causing a child to
experience pain so as to correct their misbehavior," and in schools "a teacher or administrator
typically administers corporal punishment by using a large wooden board or 'paddle' to strike the
buttocks of a child."

### The case: *Ingraham v. Wright*, 430 U.S. 651 (1977) — the outline's guess was right

Source: **Cornell Legal Information Institute, full opinion text, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/430/651 .**

- Full name in the reports: ***James Ingraham, by his mother and next friend, Eloise Ingraham, et al.
  v. Willie J. Wright, I, et al.*** Citation **430 U.S. 651** (also 97 S. Ct. 1401; 51 L. Ed. 2d 711).
- **Argued 2-3 November 1976. Decided 19 April 1977. The vote was 5 to 4.** **Justice Lewis Powell
  wrote the majority.** **Justice Byron White wrote the dissent, joined by Brennan, Marshall and
  Stevens.**
- **The children were real and they are named in the case.** **James Ingraham was in the eighth grade
  at Charles R. Drew Junior High School in Dade County, Florida, in October 1970.** The opinion's own
  words: **"Because he was slow to respond to his teacher's instructions, Ingraham was subjected to
  more than 20 licks with a paddle while being held over a table in the principal's office."** He
  **"suffered a hematoma requiring medical attention and keeping him out of school for several
  days."** A hematoma is a pool of blood collected under the skin from a blow.
- **Roosevelt Andrews was in the ninth grade at the same school.** He was paddled several times for
  small things, and was hit on the arms twice; one blow left him **"depriving him of the full use of
  his arm for a week."**
- **What the rules already said.** Florida's statute allowed punishment that was not "degrading or
  unduly severe" and required the teacher to consult the principal first. Dade County's own Policy
  5144 described the paddle — **"less than two feet long, three to four inches wide, and about
  one-half inch thick"** — and set the normal punishment at **"one to five 'licks' or blows with the
  paddle."** James Ingraham was hit more than twenty times.
- **The district court's finding, quoted in the opinion:** the severe instances **"took place in one
  junior high school,"** and **"the regime at Drew was exceptionally harsh."**
- **The holding, in the Court's own words.** On the Eighth Amendment: **"the Eighth Amendment does not
  apply to the paddling of children as a means of maintaining discipline in public schools."** On the
  Fourteenth: **"the Due Process Clause does not require notice and a hearing prior to the imposition
  of corporal punishment in the public schools."** So a school could hit a child without telling the
  child why first and without any hearing.
- **Justice White's dissent** said the record **"reveals beatings so severe that if they were
  inflicted on a hardened criminal for the commission of a serious crime, they might not pass
  constitutional muster."**

### The state sequence — one authoritative table, and it corrects a note parked earlier in this bank

Source: **Elizabeth T. Gershoff and Sarah A. Font, "Corporal Punishment in U.S. Public Schools:
Prevalence, Disparities in Use, and Status in State and Federal Policy," *Social Policy Report* 30
(2016), read in full at PubMed Central,
https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5766273/ .** Their **Table 1** is titled **"List of the
states that have banned corporal punishment in public schools in chronological order."**

**CAUTION FOR ANY LATER AGENT — a real trap.** On a first fetch of this page an automated reader
returned Massachusetts as **1871**, Hawaii as **1873** and Maine as **1875**. That is wrong. Asked to
copy the digits verbatim and not normalise them, the same page returned **Massachusetts 1971, Hawaii
1973, Maine 1975**. **Always ask for the digits verbatim when a century looks surprising.**

The full table, in the order the table gives:

**New Jersey 1867** · **Massachusetts 1971** · **Hawaii 1973** · **Maine 1975** · District of Columbia
1977 · Rhode Island 1977 · New Hampshire 1983 · New York 1985 · Vermont 1985 · California 1986 ·
Nebraska 1988 · Wisconsin 1988 · **Alaska, Connecticut, Iowa, Michigan, Minnesota, North Dakota,
Oregon and Virginia all in 1989** · South Dakota 1990 · Montana 1991 · Utah 1992 · **Illinois,
Maryland, Nevada and Washington all in 1993** · West Virginia 1994 · Delaware 2003 · Pennsylvania
2005 · Ohio 2009 · New Mexico 2011.

- **So: the first ban was New Jersey in 1867, and New Jersey stood alone for 104 years.** (The 1867
  date is also in this bank at §7e, sourced there to the New Jersey State Bar Foundation.)
- **The first modern ban was Massachusetts in 1971** — that answers the outline's `[VERIFY the state]`.
- **Gershoff and Font's own summary sentence:** **"This decline occurred in large part because 25
  states banned corporal punishment from public schools between 1974 and 1994."**
- **Counting their table at the end of this era: 27 states and the District of Columbia had banned it
  by 1994, and then no state banned it again until Delaware in 2003.** So **the twentieth century
  closed with 23 states where an adult at school could still lawfully hit a child.** State that
  plainly. It did not end everywhere.
- **This supersedes the "Massachusetts 1971 or 1972 — sources disagree" note parked in PART C-FWD and
  PART D-FWD.** Gershoff and Font, a peer-reviewed policy report with a dated table, give 1971. The
  earlier note came from an advocacy page and a think-tank article that did not give a table. **Use
  1971 and drop the hedge.** (The earlier note also said "only three more states by 1977" — the table
  shows Hawaii 1973, Maine 1975, and then D.C. and Rhode Island in 1977, which is consistent.)

### How many children this was

Same source.

- **"The prevalence of school corporal punishment has been on a steady decline since the late 1970s,
  decreasing from 4% of all schoolchildren in 1978 to less than 0.5% today."** Four in every hundred
  American schoolchildren were hit at school in 1978.
- For scale at the far end, and it belongs to era 10, not here: **"A total of 163,333 children were
  subject to corporal punishment in these states' public schools during the 2011-2012 school year."**
  **The outline forbids printing a current state count from a stale figure. The era-10 agent should
  re-source the present-day number rather than reuse 19 states from a 2016 report.**


## 9m. Kinds of school — magnet, segregation academy, parochial, charter [V — closes four tags in the "Kinds of school" span]

### The first magnet school: McCarver Elementary, Tacoma, Washington, autumn 1968

Source: **Christine H. Rossell, "Magnet Schools: No Longer Famous, But Still Intact," *Education Next*
(Program on Education Policy and Governance, Harvard Kennedy School / Hoover Institution), page last
updated 9 November 2009, https://www.educationnext.org/magnetschools/ .** Rossell is a political
scientist at Boston University who has studied school desegregation plans for decades.

- **McCarver Elementary School in Tacoma, Washington opened as the country's first magnet school in
  the autumn of 1968.** Before it did, **91 percent of its pupils were African American.**
- **The idea was to desegregate by attracting families instead of assigning them.** The district got a
  **$200,000 federal Title III grant** in the summer of 1968, recruited the district's best teachers,
  and sent counsellors from the summer programme on **home visits** to persuade families to choose the
  school. The teaching itself was the draw: team teaching, and children moving through the work at
  their own pace ("continuous progress").
- **It worked at once.** On opening day in September 1968 minority enrolment had fallen from 91 to
  **64 percent** — about **200 white children enrolled where there had been about 50**. Rossell:
  **"By 1970, African-American enrollment at McCarver was less than 50 percent, and we had a waiting
  list."**
- **The word "magnet."** Rossell traces it to an article about someone in Pittsburgh arguing for a
  school that would do something so well that children would want to enrol.
- **The second was the William Monroe Trotter School in Boston, 1969.**
- **Growth: "By 1981, there were some 1,000 such magnet schools in the United States; by 1991, there
  were over 2,400."**

### Segregation academies — the term, and named schools

**The term.** Historians and researchers call them **segregation academies**; the term is used
throughout the **Southern Education Foundation's "A History of Private Schools and Race in the
American South"** (https://southerneducation.org/publications/history-of-private-schools-and-race-in-the-american-south/ ).
In Mississippi the ones the Citizens' Council founded were called **council schools**. The sociologist
**Kenneth T. Andrews** calls them **"white flight" schools** (*Social Forces* 80:3, March 2002, cited
below). **Use "segregation academies" and say in plain words what that means: private schools started
so that white children would not have to sit next to Black children.**

**A note on what could not be settled.** The claim often made online that the phrase was coined in a
**1969 Southern Regional Council report** could not be verified from a primary source in this pass.
**It is left out rather than hedged.**

**Named schools, with dates.**

1. **Prince Edward Academy, Farmville, Virginia, 1959** — already in this chapter's era-9 outline in
   the "Schools shut rather than mixed" span. The county stopped paying for public schools and white
   children went to this new private school, partly on state tuition grants.
2. **Citizens' Council School No. 1, Jackson, Mississippi, 1964.** Source: **Ted Ownby, University of
   Mississippi, "Private Schools Since the 1950s," *Mississippi Encyclopedia*, Center for Study of
   Southern Culture, published 11 July 2017, updated 2 February 2018**
   (https://mississippiencyclopedia.org/entries/private-schools-since-the-1950s/ ). Ownby: three new
   private schools opened in Mississippi in 1964, and **"One of the new schools was Citizens' Council
   School No. 1 in Jackson."** The Citizens' Council was founded in **Indianola, Mississippi in 1954**,
   months after *Brown* (Laura R. Walton, Mississippi State University, "Citizens' Council,"
   *Mississippi Encyclopedia*, https://mississippiencyclopedia.org/entries/citizens-council/ ), and
   **"By the mid-1960s the Citizens' Councils and their publications began to promote the idea of
   private school education as an alternative to integrated public school systems."**

**The Mississippi numbers, all from Ownby unless noted** — this is the clearest documented case in the
country and the chapter should use it:

- **Before *Brown* in 1954, Mississippi had only three private schools that were not run by a church.**
- **The Mississippi legislature passed a law in 1964 creating tuition grants of $185 a child** to
  attend non-religious private schools. **In 1964-65 more than five hundred children received more
  than $80,000 in grants**, and **at most schools the grants paid more than half the tuition.**
- **The number of private schools in Mississippi rose from 121 in 1966 to 236 in 1970.** The driver was
  the Supreme Court's **1969 decision in *Alexander v. Holmes County Board of Education*, which
  required Mississippi districts to desegregate by 1970.** (Also in **"The Last Stand of Massive
  Resistance: Mississippi Public School Integration, 1970," *Mississippi History Now*, Mississippi
  Department of Archives and History, February 2009**,
  https://mshistorynow.mdah.ms.gov/issue/the-last-stand-of-massive-resistance-1970 , which puts it the
  same way: **"Between 1966 and 1970, however, the number of private schools in the state rose from
  121 to 236, and the number of students attending these schools tripled; most of this growth occurred
  in Black-majority districts."**)
- **By the 1971-72 school year more than a third of all white children in the Mississippi Delta, in
  southwestern Mississippi and around Jackson were in private schools.** **"In 1970, for example,
  virtually every white student in Canton left public schools for a private academy, while more than
  40 percent of Jackson's white students left public schools."** In north-east and south-east
  Mississippi, where few Black people lived, **fewer than 10 percent** of white children went.
- **The new private schools used public school property.** Ownby quotes the historian **Michael
  Fuquay**: Mississippi's **"academies received books, supplies, sports equipment, organizational
  resources, facilities, and funds directly from the public school system. In Tunica and Clay counties,
  private school teachers were kept on the public school payrolls. In Forrest County, private school
  students were transported on public school buses."**
- **The association that ran them said so in writing.** Source: **Ernest Gray Flora, University of
  Mississippi, "Mississippi Private School Association," *Mississippi Encyclopedia*, 29 November 2023**
  (https://mississippiencyclopedia.org/entries/mississippi-private-school-association/ ). The MPSA was
  **formed in 1968**, led by **William J. Simmons**, editor of the Citizens' Council's newspaper. **In
  autumn 1964 Mississippi had nine private day schools that were not military or Catholic; by 1972 the
  MPSA had 30,515 pupils in more than 110 schools across four states.** The minutes of the MPSA
  executive board meeting of **23 January 1971**, taken by Simmons himself, read: **"[I]t was decided
  that these schools remain members of the MPSA until such a time as they accept a Negro student or
  faculty member. At that time, they will be asked to resign from the MPSA if they not done so
  voluntarily."** (The garbled last clause is in the original.)
- **The tax fight.** In **May 1969** a group of African Americans in **Holmes County** sued to stop
  private schools that excluded Black children from holding tax-exempt status, and in ***Green v.
  Kennedy* (1970) the federal district court granted it.** (Ownby.)

### Parochial school enrolment: the peak is 1964-65, and it more than halves by 1990

Source: **NCES, *120 Years of American Education: A Statistical Portrait* (1993), Table 15, "Catholic
elementary and secondary enrollment, teachers, and schools, by level: 1919-20 to 1990-91"**
(https://nces.ed.gov/pubs93/93442.pdf , read with pdftotext -layout). NCES's own source for the
table is the **National Catholic Educational Association** and the *Official Catholic Directory*.

- **Enrolment peaked in 1964-65 at 5,601,000 children in 13,249 Catholic schools.** The number of
  schools peaked a year later, at **13,292 in 1965-66**.
- The climb before it: **1,925,521 in 1919-20; 2,396,305 in 1939-40; 3,066,387 in 1949-50; 4,276,000
  in 1955-56; 5,253,791 in 1960-61.**
- The fall after it: **5,199,000 in 1967-68; 4,363,566 in 1970-71; 3,504,000 in 1974-75; 3,106,000 in
  1980-81; 2,821,000 in 1985-86; 2,475,439 in 1990-91.**
- **So Catholic school enrolment fell by more than half — from 5.6 million to under 2.5 million — in
  the twenty-six years after 1964-65, and the number of schools fell from 13,292 to 8,587.**
- The class sizes changed with it. **Children per member of teaching staff: 32.8 in 1964-65, 26.3 in
  1970-71, 21.3 in 1980-81, 18.9 in 1990-91.** Total teaching staff went **up** from 171,000 in
  1964-65 to about 179,000 in 1967-68 and then down to 131,198 by 1990-91.
- **Not settled here:** a figure for 2000 was not sourced in this pass, because *120 Years* stops at
  1990-91. **The honest statement for era 9 is the 1964-65 peak and the 1990-91 figure.** An era-10
  agent wanting a 2000s number should use the NCES Digest of Education Statistics private-school
  tables, not this one.

### The first charter school law, and the first charter school

Sources: **Minnesota Legislative Reference Library, "Charter Schools — Minnesota Issues Resources
Guides,"** https://www.lrl.mn.gov/guides/charter.htm ; and **Jon Schroeder, *Ripples of Innovation:
Charter Schooling in Minnesota, the Nation's First Charter School State*, Progressive Policy
Institute, ERIC ED491210**, https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED491210.pdf (read with
pdftotext -layout).

- **The law is Laws of Minnesota 1991, chapter 265, article 9, section 3** (bill HF700/SF467), passed
  as part of the omnibus K-12 education finance bill. **Both dates in the outline are right: the law is
  1991 and the first school opened in 1992.**
- **It was cut down badly to pass.** Schroeder: the bill had failed in the House in 1989, 1990 and
  1991; a conference committee made three compromises — **only local school districts could grant a
  charter, and only eight charter schools were allowed in the whole state** — and it **survived a
  House floor fight by one vote.** **Governor Arne Carlson signed it.** The senators who had pushed it
  since 1989 were **Ember Reichgott Junge and Nelson.**
- **The first charter approved was Bluffview Montessori in Winona, in December 1991.**
- **The first charter school in the United States to open was City Academy in St. Paul, in the autumn
  of 1992.** Its founder was **Milo Cutter** (Schroeder names her). **A specific opening date of 7
  September 1992 is widely reported but was not verified from a primary source in this pass — print
  "the autumn of 1992" unless someone sources the day.**
- **The 1993 Minnesota legislature raised the cap from eight to twenty** and allowed appeals to the
  State Board of Education when a district said no.
- Scale, for the later eras: Schroeder, writing in 2004, gives **88 charter schools in Minnesota** and
  charter schools nationally **serving some 750,000 students**. **These are 2004 figures and belong to
  era 10, not here.**


## 9n. Homeschooling — the two roots, the prosecutions, and the claim about "the last state" that the record does not support [V — closes three tags in the "Homeschooling" span, and corrects the outline's premise]

### First: the outline's premise was too strong, and the correction matters

The seed outline said teaching your own children at home was **"illegal or effectively illegal in most
states into the 1980s."** **That is not what the historians say, and it should not be printed that
way.**

- **Milton Gaither** wrote the only book-length scholarly history of American homeschooling
  (*Homeschool: An American History*, 2008). In his article **"Why Homeschooling Happened,"
  *Educational Horizons*, Summer 2008, ERIC EJ799390**
  (https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/EJ799390.pdf , read with pdftotext -layout), he puts it
  exactly this way: **"In many states extant laws made the practice either illegal or dubious. The
  conflicts between families and government were sometimes ugly."** **Illegal or dubious** — not
  illegal everywhere.
- The **Coalition for Responsible Home Education**, whose history brief footnotes Gaither, Mitchell
  Stevens's *Kingdom of Children* (2001), Knowles/Marlow/Muchmore, and Jennifer Lois
  (https://crhe.org/research/histories/ ... brief at
  https://crhe.org/research/summaries/a-brief-history-of-homeschooling/ ), is blunter: **"When Holt
  and Moore first began advocating homeschooling, educating children at home was legal in every
  state, but subject to varying regulations, which were sometimes quite stringent (for example, six
  states required parents to have teaching licenses)."**
- **What was true, and is the honest version for the book:** compulsory attendance laws in many states
  required a child to be taught **in a school** or **by a certified teacher**, and parents who taught
  their own children were prosecuted for truancy under those laws. **The prosecutions are real and
  documented. The blanket word "illegal" is not.**

### The two roots — one secular, one religious

Sources: the CRHE brief above (footnoted to Gaither, Stevens and Knowles et al.), and the material
already parked in this bank's **PART B by the `home-family` agent** (johnholtgws.com; Sutherland
Institute).

- **The secular root is John Holt.** He was a school-reform writer who argued that ordinary schooling,
  built on learning by rote, made an oppressive classroom whose real purpose was to produce obedient
  employees. He told parents to take their children out of it. **He founded the newsletter *Growing
  Without Schooling* in 1977 — the first issue is August 1977, and it is reported to be the first
  homeschooling publication in the United States.** **He coined the word "unschooling."** **He died in
  1985.**
- **They were not strangers to one another.** CRHE describes Moore as **"Holt's friend"** who **"added his voice"** soon after Holt's writing had produced the first homeschoolers. **So do not write that the two roots grew up independently — the two men knew each other; their reasons differed, not their acquaintance.**
- **The religious root is Raymond Moore**, an educational researcher and a **Seventh-day Adventist**,
  and his wife **Dorothy Moore**. They argued that formal schooling before about eight or nine years
  old harmed children, and that children should be taught at home until then. **Their 1975 book
  *Better Late Than Early* set out the argument; their 1981 *Home Grown Kids* was, per CRHE, "often
  the first book homeschoolers read."**
- **The alliance broke.** Through most of the 1980s religious and secular homeschoolers built
  organisations and fought court cases together. **Michael Farris, a homeschooling father and a
  lawyer, founded the Home School Legal Defense Association in 1983.** By 1990, per CRHE and Gaither,
  the movement's leadership had passed to the Christian right; **Moore was pushed aside by newer
  leaders who did not count him, as a Seventh-day Adventist, as Christian enough**, and in 1990 he
  appealed without success for the movement to stay united.

### Named prosecutions, with the court records — three families, three states

**1. *State v. Riddle*, 168 W. Va. 429, 285 S.E.2d 359, decided 11 December 1981.** Opinion read at
https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/state-v-riddle-no-888283524 (law.justia.com and leagle.com both
return HTTP 403 to this environment; casemine.com also carries it).

- **Bobby E. Riddle and Esther Riddle of Harrison County, West Virginia** taught their two children,
  **Tim and Jill**, at home. Jill was ten.
- **They were convicted of failing to comply with the Compulsory School Attendance Law (W. Va. Code
  18-8-1) and fined ten dollars each.**
- **The court said the teaching itself was good:** **"By all accounts in the record below, the Riddles
  did an excellent job — possibly better than the public schools could do."** It affirmed the
  convictions anyway. **Justice Neely wrote the opinion.** The Riddles were "Biblical Christians" of a
  Methodist sect, the Wesley.

**2. *Delconte v. State*, 313 N.C. 384, 329 S.E.2d 636, decided 7 May 1985.** Opinion at
https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/delconte-v-state-no-886191860 ; the **North Carolina Department of
Administration hosts the judgment as a PDF** at
https://www.doa.nc.gov/documents/files/dnpe-delconte-v-state-nc-pdf/open .

- **Larry Delconte moved to North Carolina in March 1981 with his wife Michelle and their four
  children** and taught them at home.
- **The North Carolina Court of Appeals held that the state's compulsory attendance law forbade home
  instruction.** **The North Carolina Supreme Court reversed on 7 May 1985**, holding that his home
  school counted as a nonpublic school and that teaching a child at home did not break the attendance
  law. **This is the case that made homeschooling lawful in North Carolina.**

**3. *People v. DeJonge*, 442 Mich. 266, 501 N.W.2d 127, decided 25 May 1993** — the best-documented
family, and the one used for this era's story block. See §9o below.

### "The last state to legalise homeschooling" — NOT SETTLED, and left out of the outline

- **The claim usually made is that homeschooling was legal in all fifty states by 1993.** It is
  repeated constantly and is usually traced to the Home School Legal Defense Association, an advocacy
  organisation that fought the last cases and has an interest in the story.
- **The historians decline to name a last state.** CRHE, summarising Gaither and Stevens: **"The story
  of the legalization of homeschooling is really fifty different stories: Some states saw education
  policy change without the need for legislative action, some added only a few words or a sentence to
  their statutes, and others composed and passed detailed homeschool statutes."** And: **"By 1989 the
  vast majority [of] states had made peace with homeschoolers, with only a few states holding out into
  the early 1990s."** CRHE says HSLDA **"made a name for itself by bringing about the end of the last
  remaining holdouts"** in the early 1990s but does not say which state was last.
- **Because different states changed by legislation, by regulation and by court decision, there is no
  single event that can be called "the last state legalising homeschooling," and no source consulted
  in this pass names one.**
- **Do NOT write that Michigan's was "the strictest rule left in the country."** The *DeJonge* opinion
  was checked directly for that: **it names no other state, gives no count of states with a
  certification requirement, and makes no comparison.** The superlative was drafted and then removed
  for lack of a source.
- **Ruling applied (per the era-9 agent's instructions): the unsettled claim is kept out of the
  outline rather than hedged in prose.** What the outline prints instead is what is documented: most
  states had settled with homeschoolers by 1989, a few held out into the early 1990s, and **Michigan's
  certified-teacher requirement — the strictest rule left anywhere — fell in court on 25 May 1993.**
- **For a later agent who wants to settle it:** the route is Gaither's book pp. 175-200 and CRHE's
  fifty individual state histories at https://crhe.org/research/histories/ . Neither was worked
  through here.

## 9o. The DeJonge family — the story block for `target-homeschooling-family-prosecuted` [V — closes the story-block VERIFY]

**This replaces a `target` slot with a named family and a real court record**, which is what the slot
required. Two sources: the opinion, and a 2008 newspaper-style interview with the parents.

**Source 1, the court record: *People v. DeJonge*, 442 Mich. 266, 501 N.W.2d 127 (1993)**, read at
https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/people-v-dejonge-docket-890609850 (law.justia.com and leagle.com
return HTTP 403 here).

- **Mark DeJonge and Chris DeJonge** began teaching their children at home in **August 1984**.
- **Michigan law (M.C.L. 380.1561) required a child aged 6 to 16 to be taught in a public school or in
  a state-approved nonpublic school by a state-certified teacher.** The DeJonges used a programme run
  by the **Church of Christian Liberty and Academy of Arlington Heights, Illinois**. Neither parent
  held a teaching certificate.
- **They were charged and convicted of violating the compulsory education law.** **The sentence was
  two years' probation each, a $200 fine each, an order to have the children tested, and an order to
  arrange certified instruction.**
- **The trial judge was, in the opinion's account, "very impressed" with the support the family had
  and had a "very, very favorable report on the education of the children."** He convicted them
  anyway, because the law was about who taught, not about how well.
- **Their religious argument, in the opinion's words:** they wanted a **"Christ centered education,"**
  because **"the major purpose of education is to show a student how to face God, not just show him
  how to face the world."**
- **The appeals took years.** The Michigan Court of Appeals affirmed on **8 August 1989** (the case was
  consolidated there with ***People v. Bennett***). The Michigan Supreme Court sent it back on **17
  October 1990**. The Court of Appeals affirmed again in **1991**. **On 25 May 1993 the Michigan
  Supreme Court reversed, holding the teacher-certification requirement an unconstitutional violation
  of the First Amendment's Free Exercise Clause as applied to families with a sincere religious
  objection. Justice Riley wrote the opinion.** The court accepted that the state has a compelling
  interest in children being educated, and held that Michigan had not shown that requiring a certified
  teacher was necessary to achieve it.

**Source 2, the family's own account: Lorie A. Shane, "Fifteen years later, home-school parents say
legal battle was worth it," Mackinac Center for Public Policy, 27 May 2008**
(https://www.mackinac.org/9483 ). **This is a think-tank publication based on an interview with the
DeJonges — label it as their own recollection, not as court record.**

- They lived at **Shelbyville, in rural western Michigan**. They began homeschooling in the **autumn
  of 1984**.
- **Chris DeJonge was in her second week of teaching at home when officials from the Michigan
  Department of Social Services came to the house and told the family the children were truants.**
- **They were formally charged and convicted in Ottawa County District Court in 1985**, and given a
  **stay of sentence** while they appealed.
- **The fight lasted about ten years and took nine court appearances.**
- **The Michigan Supreme Court ruled 4 to 3 in their favour in the spring of 1993.**
- Mark DeJonge, afterwards: **"We're glad we went through it. We grew as a family and we grew as a
  couple."** By 2008 the couple had ten children and sixteen grandchildren.

**One discrepancy, stated rather than resolved:** the Mackinac interview places the family at
**Shelbyville**, which is not in Ottawa County, while the court record has them tried in **Ottawa
County**. Both are printed here. **The outline says only "rural western Michigan" and "Ottawa County
District Court," which both sources support.** **No source consulted names the DeJonge children or
