gives their ages, so the outline does not name them.**


## 9p. The children who were kept out of school altogether, and the law that ended it [V — closes the story-block VERIFY on `target-special-education-student-or-parent`]

**Why this section exists.** Era 8 established (§8s, *State ex rel. Beattie v. Board of Education*,
1919) that a disabled child could be shut out of an American public school and had no right to be let
in. **PART D-FWD parked the rest of the curve for this era and flagged that the 1975 Act is the era-9
agent's.** The era-9 outline had a `target` story slot for a special-education child or parent and no
span to put it in. **Both are now filled.** The named children come from a federal court record.

### *Mills v. Board of Education of the District of Columbia*, 348 F. Supp. 866 (D.D.C.), decided 1 August 1972

Opinion read at https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/mills-v-board-of-886070158 (law.justia.com and
leagle.com return HTTP 403 here; the same opinion is on Google Scholar and in the Civil Rights
Litigation Clearinghouse, https://clearinghouse.net/case/11084/ , docket 1:71-cv-01939).
**Judge Joseph C. Waddy** decided it.

**The seven named children, with the school system's own words for them.** *(The quoted labels —
"behavior problem," "retarded," "brain-damaged" — are the record's period words. Name them as the
record's words and do not use them in the book's own voice. Same rule as §8s.)*

- **Peter Mills, 12.** **Excluded from Brent Elementary School on 23 March 1971**, out of the fourth
  grade. The school called him **a "behavior problem."** He was a committed dependent ward of the
  District of Columbia. He stayed **"excluded from any publicly-supported education."**
- **Duane Blacksheare, 13.** **Excluded from Giddings Elementary School in October 1967**, out of the
  third grade, also as a **"behavior problem."** Later given two hours a day. **He was out of school
  for about four years.**
- **George Liddell, Jr., 8.** **His application to Maury Elementary School was refused** on the ground
  that he needed a special class. **He had never been to school at all.**
- **Steven Gaston, 8.** **Out of Taylor Elementary School since September 1969**, first grade,
  described as slightly brain-damaged and hyperactive; the record says he wandered around the
  classroom. He was waiting for a tuition grant that had not come.
- **Michael Williams, 16.** **Out of the Sharpe Health School since October 1969**, excluded after a
  spell in hospital because of his health and his absences. He has epilepsy. He was on a waiting list
  for a tuition grant.
- **Janice King, 13.** Refused a place from the age at which the law required her to be at school,
  because there was no programme for her. She has right hemiplegia — weakness down one side of the
  body. **She had never been to school.**
- **Jerome James, 12.** **"Totally excluded from public school,"** in the opinion's words, and on a
  waiting list for a tuition grant.

**How many children this was.** The opinion sets out the competing counts and they do not agree, so
print the disagreement:

- **The families' estimate:** about **22,000** children in the District who were "retarded, emotionally
  disturbed, blind, deaf, and speech or learning disabled," of whom **perhaps 18,000 were not being
  given any specialised education.**
- **The Board of Education's own data:** it was providing publicly supported special education to **at
  least 3,880 school-age children.**
- **The District's own 1971 report admitted that an estimated 12,340 disabled children would not be
  served in the 1971-72 school year.** **That is the figure the District itself published, so it is
  the safest one to print.**

**The ruling.** The court held that the District had to provide a publicly supported education to
every child of school age, whatever the child's disability, and that **the extra cost of doing it was
not a defence.** It also required due process — notice and a hearing — before a child could be
excluded or moved.

**The companion case, named by the U.S. Department of Education alongside Mills:** ***Pennsylvania
Association for Retarded Citizens v. Commonwealth* (1971)**.

### The Education for All Handicapped Children Act, 1975

Source: **U.S. Department of Education, "IDEA History," https://sites.ed.gov/idea/IDEA-History .**

- **Public Law 94-142, signed by President Gerald Ford on 29 November 1975.**
- The Department's own framing: **"In 1970, U.S. schools educated only one in five children with
  disabilities."** The law answered a Congressional concern about two groups, the first being **"the
  more than 1 million children with disabilities excluded entirely from the education system."**
- **It guaranteed a free, appropriate public education — FAPE — to every child with a disability in
  every state.**
- The Department names ***PARC* (1971)** and ***Mills* (1972)** as the cases that established that
  states and localities had to educate disabled children.

### The count, before and after — NCES *120 Years*, Table 12, checked line by line in this pass

Source: **NCES, *120 Years of American Education: A Statistical Portrait* (1993), Table 12, "Children
served in special education programs, by type of disability: 1921-22 to 1989-90"**
(https://nces.ed.gov/pubs93/93442.pdf , read with pdftotext -layout). Figures in thousands in the
table; written out here.

- **1931-32: 161,000 children, 0.6 percent of public school enrolment**
- **1947-48: 356,000, 1.5 percent**
- **1957-58: 838,000, 2.5 percent**
- **1965-66: 1,794,000, 4.3 percent**
- **1969-70: 2,677,000, 5.9 percent** — *before the Act*
- **1976-77: 3,692,000, 8.3 percent** — the first year counted under P.L. 94-142
- **1980-81: 4,142,000, 10.1 percent**
- **1989-90: 4,641,000, 11.4 percent**

**Two things in this table that the chapter should say and that PART D-FWD flagged:**

1. **The rise starts before the law.** From 1.5 percent in 1947-48 to 5.9 percent in 1969-70 is a
   quadrupling, and all of it happens before 1975. **The law did not begin the change; it made it a
   right and stopped it being reversible.** Do not write "the law fixed it."
2. **A whole category appears out of nowhere.** The table has **no "learning disabled" column at all
   until 1976-77**, when it shows **796,000 children.** By **1989-90 it is 2,050,000 — the largest
   single group, and nearly half of all children in special education.** Meanwhile the number counted
   as "mentally retarded" (the table's word) **falls from 830,000 in 1969-70 to 548,000 in 1989-90.**
   **Children were being sorted differently, not only in greater numbers.**

**Boundary note for the era-10 agent:** the Act's later names — the Education of the Handicapped Act
amendments, and **IDEA**, the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act — and everything after 1990
are yours. **The 1990 renaming to IDEA sits on your boundary; era 9 stops at the 1989-90 count.**


---

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): Virginia, the named men who closed the schools, and a date disagreement

The outline's Prince Edward span names no one who decided to shut the schools. These are the people the sources name.

- **Harry F. Byrd**, United States senator, publicly called for "massive resistance" against *Brown* in **late February 1956** (James H. Hershman, "Massive Resistance," *Encyclopedia Virginia*, 7 December 2020, https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/massive-resistance/ , read through WebFetch because curl gets 403).
- **The Stanley Plan (1956)**, named for **Governor Thomas B. Stanley**, set up a state **Pupil Placement Board** to block Black pupils from white schools, and provided that **the governor would close any school facing a federal desegregation order** (Hershman, same entry).
- **Governor J. Lindsay Almond Jr.** used that law in **September 1958**. He "closed all nine schools, locking out nearly 13,000 students": Warren County High School in Front Royal, Lane High School and Venable Elementary in Charlottesville, and six white schools in Norfolk. On **19 January 1959** a state court and a federal court both ruled the closings unconstitutional (Hershman, same entry). *Southern Exposure* (below) gives "12,000 children in Norfolk, Front Royal and Charlottesville." **Two counts: about 12,000 and nearly 13,000. State both or say "about 12,000 to 13,000."**
- **Prince Edward County's own organisers.** *Southern Exposure* vol. 7 no. 2 (1979), "Prince Edward, 1954-1959: 'Our White Case is Lost'," reprinted by the Institute for Southern Studies at https://www.facingsouth.org/prince-edward-1954-1959-our-white-case-lost (curl works):
  - **J. Barrye Wall**, editor of the *Farmville Herald*, and **Robert (Bob) Crawford**, a laundry owner and sometime school board chairman, built the **Defenders of State Sovereignty and Individual Liberties** from 1954. By October 1954 it had a state charter and 2,000 members. In Prince Edward its directors included the mayor, a former school board chairman, a newspaper publisher and a tobacco manufacturer.
  - **31 May 1955**, hours after *Brown II*: the Board of Supervisors passed **Supervisor John Bruce's** motion to appropriate only the legal minimum of **$150,000**, a fraction of what the schools needed.
  - **7 June 1955**, a mass meeting at Longwood College voted **1,250 to 25** for **Mayor W. C. Fitzpatrick's** motion to set up a private corporation to pay white teachers if the schools closed. By mid-July the **Prince Edward Educational Foundation** had **$180,000** in pledges.
  - 1956: **4,184 people** signed a petition against desegregation (the county had about 8,460 white residents, children included).
  - 1959: the Board of Supervisors voted not to fund the public schools. The white private schools were organised under **Roy Pearson** of Farmville, an official of Standard Oil.
- **Date disagreement.** *Southern Exposure* dates the supervisors' vote **2 June 1959**. Encyclopedia Virginia and the Virginia Museum of History & Culture (§9a above) give **26 June 1959**. **Both are printed here. The outline's "26 June" follows two institutional sources; a writer can keep it.**
- **What the prose can say:** the county's supervisors voted to stop paying for the schools, and the plan behind that vote was drawn up by named local men (Wall, Crawford, Fitzpatrick, Bruce) and backed by a state law written under Governor Stanley and used by Governor Almond.

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): Little Rock's Lost Year, how many children and where they went

- **Who closed the schools:** Governor **Orval Faubus**, under laws the Arkansas legislature passed for him (NPS timeline, §9a-2; Equal Justice Initiative, *A History of Racial Injustice* calendar, 27 September, https://calendar.eji.org/racial-injustice/sep/27 , curl works). The four schools were **Central High, Hall High, Little Rock Technical High (white) and Horace Mann High (Black)** (unconfirmed: search summary only, Encyclopedia of Arkansas "Lost Year," which is behind a Cloudflare block).
- **How many children:** **3,665 Black and white students** were left without a public high school (unconfirmed: search summary only, Encyclopedia of Arkansas). NPS gives no number (§9a-2). **Print it only with the tag cleared, or say "thousands of students."**
- **Where they went** (John A. Kirk, historian, "Sixty years ago, Little Rock closed all its public high schools rather than desegregate them," *Arkansas Times*, 13 September 2018, https://arktimes.com/news/arkansas-reporter/2018/09/13/sixty-years-ago-little-rock-closed-all-its-public-high-schools-rather-than-desegregate-them ): white students went to private segregated schools, schools in other Arkansas districts, out-of-state schools, or took correspondence courses from the University of Arkansas. **"With access to fewer resources, black students were hit the hardest."** Most went to classes in other districts or out of state. Some took a correspondence course run by **L. M. Christophe, principal of the Black Horace Mann High School**. Faubus helped the **Little Rock Private School Corporation** buy private buildings with public money. The state kept white and Black teachers on full pay in empty classrooms. **Central High's football team kept playing all year.**
- Faubus won a third term in November 1958 (Kirk) and was re-elected until he served to 1967 (EJI).
- **Across the South** (EJI, same page): by the start of 1964-65, fewer than 3 percent of the South's Black children were in school with white children, and in Alabama, Arkansas, Georgia, Mississippi and South Carolina substantially below 1 percent. In 1967 the U.S. Commission on Civil Rights wrote that white violence and intimidation "continues to be a deterrent to school desegregation." **This is a second source for the outline's "99 of every 100" figure, from a different angle.**

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): New Orleans 1960, the people who fought Ruby Bridges's school

Source: Nikki Brown, "New Orleans School Crisis," *64 Parishes*, published 31 March 2011, updated 13 December 2024, https://64parishes.org/entry/new-orleans-school-crisis (curl 403, WebFetch works; quotations below were asked for verbatim).

- **Governor Jimmie Davis** and the **Louisiana legislature**. In the late summer of 1960 the legislature passed laws making anyone who "contested state control over public education in Louisiana" subject to fines and jail. Under **Act 496** Davis took over all decisions on desegregation in Orleans Parish. The school board had asked him to step in.
- **Leander Perez**, the political boss of Plaquemines Parish, told a rally: "Don't wait for your daughter to be raped by these Congolese." (The entry's next sentence contains a word that is garbled in the page text; do not quote past "Congolese." without checking it.)
- **The White Citizens' Council** held a rally of **5,000 people** and urged white parents to defy the court order. **A riot broke out on 16 November 1960** in front of the Orleans Parish School Board building.
- **The "Cheerleaders"**: "young, white, working-class mothers" who blocked the children and their parents at McDonogh and Frantz, "spat at the children, hurled racial invectives, slashed tires, and threatened much worse."
- **By the end of November fewer than ten white children attended McDonogh and Frantz combined.** The 1961-62 school year began without incident.
- **The doll in the coffin:** one protester carried a small coffin with a Black doll in it, and Ruby Bridges later said she had nightmares about it (unconfirmed: search summary only, attributed to an NPR interview with Bridges). Marshals let her eat only food brought from home (unconfirmed: search summary only).

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-28 (T-261d): who was the woman who threatened to poison Ruby Bridges, and who carried the coffin?
Sources checked: 64 Parishes "Ruby Bridges" and "New Orleans School Crisis" (the threat and the Cheerleaders, no names); NPS "Ruby Bridges" (no names); National Women's History Museum (no names); a web search across news and biography pages (the woman and the coffin carrier are described, never named); usmarshals.gov (403).
How the prose can say it: "No record names the woman who threatened her, or the woman with the coffin."

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): the University of Mississippi, 1962, and the two men killed

Owner note: `rights-movements` leads on James Meredith's campaign (its outline mentions him in the Medgar Evers story only). **Nothing in the book tells the riot or its dead.** This is a school being desegregated, so it fits this chapter's college span beside Little Rock and New Orleans. Parked as a pointer in `research/research-rights-movements.md` era 9.

- **James Meredith**, an Air Force veteran, applied on **20 January 1961** and was rejected after writing that he was Black. **Medgar Evers** helped him; NAACP lawyers **Constance Baker Motley** and **Jack Greenberg** took the case. The Fifth Circuit took it on 15 June 1962, and on **10 September 1962 Justice Hugo Black** ordered him admitted. Governor **Ross Barnett** worked to stop the admission and reached a deal with Attorney General **Robert F. Kennedy** on **30 September 1962**. (Candace Mckenzie, MDAH, "The Big Dreamer: James Meredith's Fight for Integration," *Mississippi History Now*, March 2023, https://mshistorynow.mdah.ms.gov/issue/jamesmeredith , curl works.)
- On **13 September 1962** Barnett said on television, "No school will be integrated in Mississippi while I'm governor," and on **20 September**, after the university's trustees made him registrar, he **personally blocked Meredith from registering** (unconfirmed: search summary only, Zinn Education Project and MDAH, behind Cloudflare).
- **The riot, 30 September to 1 October 1962.** Barnett had spoken at an Ole Miss football game in Jackson the day before and stirred the crowd (Megan Brodsky, "Ole Miss Riot (1962)," BlackPast, https://blackpast.org/african-american-history/ole-miss-riot-1962/ ; Chapter 16 / Humanities Tennessee, "Terror in Oxford," https://chapter16.org/terror-in-oxford/ ). About **500 federal marshals** guarded Meredith, who was moved into **Baxter Hall**. State Senator **George Yarbrough** dismissed the Mississippi Highway Patrol from the crowd at about 7:25 p.m., and a full riot began at 7:30 (BlackPast). The crowd reached **about 3,000** (BlackPast) or **more than 2,500** (MDAH), students and non-students, led in part by the former general **Edwin Walker** (BlackPast). Rioters threw rocks, bottles and Molotov cocktails and fired guns (BlackPast, MDAH).
- **The dead.** **Paul Guihard**, 30, New York correspondent of Agence France-Presse, was **shot in the back** within about ten minutes of reaching the campus, and found near a women's dormitory. **Ray Gunter**, 23, of Oxford, a jukebox repairman, was dead on arrival at the Oxford hospital. (Al Kuettner, UPI, 1 October 1962, https://www.upi.com/Archives/1962/10/01/Ole-Miss-enrolls-Meredith-after-riots-kill-2-injure-75/4258814340955/ ; BlackPast for Gunter's job.) **No one was ever charged with killing Guihard** (Chapter 16). No source read names who shot Gunter.
- **The injured, whose count:** UPI's first-day report, "at least 75"; MDAH, "more than 200"; BlackPast, **166 marshals** (a third of them) and **40 soldiers and Guardsmen**, **more than 300 in all**. Search summaries add "28 marshals with gunshot wounds" (unconfirmed: search summary only). **State the range and whose count each is.**
- **Arrests:** 108 by the first day, among them **Melvin Bruce**, 24, of Decatur, Georgia, charged as a sniper (UPI). Federal troops, about **3,000** by 1 October (BlackPast), under **Brig. Gen. Charles Billingslea**.
- **1 October 1962**: Meredith registered with Registrar **Robert B. Ellis** and walked to an American colonial history class to shouts of "Was it worth two deaths?" (UPI). Some students walked out of his classes; marshals escorted him everywhere. **He graduated in August 1963** (MDAH).

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-28 (T-261d): who killed Paul Guihard and Ray Gunter?
Sources checked: UPI 1 October 1962 (both dead, no killer named); BlackPast (no killer named); Chapter 16 (Guihard "shot in the back at close range," "No one has ever been charged"); MDAH *Mississippi History Now* (does not even name Gunter, "an unnamed young Oxford man"); Mississippi Encyclopedia (403).
How the prose can say it: "Nobody was ever charged with either killing."

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): Boston, 1974, inside South Boston High

`rights-movements` already tells the Boston ruling and the stoned buses (its bank era 9, NPS "Morgan v. Hennigan"; its outline span). **This chapter's part is the school year inside the building**, as with Little Rock.

- **11 December 1974**: during a shoving match outside a classroom, **Michael Faith**, 18 per *Time* (17 in later accounts, unconfirmed: search summary only), a white South Boston student, was stabbed. Police arrested **James White**, 18, a Black student from Roxbury. A crowd gathered at the school gates screaming racial abuse. Boston police and **125 state troopers** charged the crowd, which threw bricks and bottles and overturned a police car. **Four empty buses were sent to the front door as decoys while the Black students were put on buses at the back and driven home.** South Boston High and seven other Boston schools were then closed. (*Time*, "Education: Southie Boils Over," 23 December 1974, https://time.com/archive/6845469/education-southie-boils-over/ , curl works.)
- **A student's own account, named.** **Phyllis Ellison**, one of nearly five dozen Black students bused to South Boston High, who graduated in 1977 (PBS *American Experience*, "Being Bused," https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/americanexperience/features/eyesontheprize-being-bused/ ): on the first day crowds shouted "Niggers, go home," held signs, and held up bananas at the bus. Inside, there were "anywhere between ten and fifteen fights" on a normal day, Black and white students sat on opposite sides of classrooms, lunchrooms and washrooms, and on the day of the stabbing the Black students were locked in their rooms while the white students were let out. **She is a named person with a published first-person account, usable in `hb-zoom` prose or as a candidate story. `rights-movements` does not tell her (grep 2026-09-28).**
- **December 1975**: Judge **W. Arthur Garrity** put South Boston High under federal receivership, saying the School Committee was blocking his order (unconfirmed: search summary only; NPS gives only the 1982 hand-back and 1985 final order).

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): Mexican American children after Delgado, *Hernandez v. Driscoll* (1957)

Carry-forward from era 8 (Delgado 1948 allowed separate first-grade classes "for language-deficient" pupils). Source: V. Carl Allsup, "Hernandez v. Driscoll CISD," *Handbook of Texas Online*, TSHA, https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/hernandez-v-driscoll-cisd (WebFetch).

- The **American G.I. Forum** sued the **Driscoll Consolidated Independent School District** in Texas.
- Driscoll put Mexican American children through "beginners' classes," then "low first" and "high first," then a segregated second grade: **four years to get through two grades.**
- **Linda Pérez**, who spoke only English, was put in the "Mexican" first grade to learn English.
- In **1957** the federal court called the grouping "arbitrary and unreasonable" and ordered it stopped. Allsup: the system "did not change radically," and LULAC and the G.I. Forum kept suing through the 1960s.
- **The judge's name and the day of the ruling are not in this source.** Print the year only.

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): the Indian boarding schools, 1950-2000, and the Indian Child Welfare Act (1978)

`native-nations` leads on the schools and on ICWA. The era-9 outline has the 1973 peak but no harm, no named child, and nothing between 1950 and 1969. These are the facts in reach.

**1. How many children, and whose count.**
- **Kennedy Report**, U.S. Senate, Special Subcommittee on Indian Education, *Indian Education: A National Tragedy, A National Challenge*, Senate Report 91-501, November 1969 (ERIC ED034625, https://files.eric.ed.gov/fulltext/ED034625.pdf , read with pypdf): the BIA ran **226 schools in 17 states, 77 of them boarding schools**, with **34,605** Indian children in BIA boarding schools, 15,450 in BIA day schools and 3,854 in "peripheral dormitories" while attending public schools. An earlier page gives **35,309** in boarding schools and **"Nearly 9,000 of the boarding-school children are under 9 years old."** In 1968, **6,616** school-age Indian children were not in school at all, and the BIA could not say what schooling 2,842 others had. **Two boarding counts in one report; state both or say "about 35,000."**
- **7,476 Navajo children aged 9 and under were in 48 elementary boarding schools** on the Navajo Reservation, the subcommittee was told in December 1967, because no day school was near them (Kennedy Report, "Elementary boarding schools"). **Tuba City Boarding School** held **1,200** elementary pupils.
- **House Report 95-1386, 24 July 1978**, on the Indian Child Welfare Act (NARF National Indian Law Library, *ICWA Legislative History*, https://narf.org/nill/documents/icwa/print/app1.pdf , read with pypdf): "The Bureau of Indian Affairs (BIA), in its school census for 1971, indicates that **34,538** children live in its institutional facilities rather than at home. This represents more than **17 percent** of the Indian school age population of federally-recognized reservations and **60 percent** of the children enrolled in BIA schools." On the Navajo Reservation "about **29,000** children" in grades K-12 lived at boarding schools (the scan's percentage beside it is garbled, "9.0," and is not used).
- **The 1973 peak of about 60,000**: Heard Museum boarding-school exhibit site, "Reforms and Changes" (https://boardingschool.heard.org/reforms-and-changes/ , now redirected; read from the Internet Archive copy): "The rise of Native activism and involvement in education by tribal nations in the 1960s and 1970s resulted in a peak enrollment of 60,000 in 1973." Also Georgia State University Library exhibit *Health is a Human Right*, "American Indian Boarding Schools" (https://exhibits.library.gsu.edu/health-is-a-human-right/displacement/american-indian-boarding-schools/ ): "peaking in the 1970s, with an estimated enrollment of 60,000 in 1973." **Neither page says what the 60,000 counts (federal only, or federal plus mission and dormitory). The federal counts for 1969 and 1971 are about 35,000. Say "about 60,000 by one museum's count" or use the federal figures.** Heard adds: the 1975 Self-Determination Act moved students from distant boarding schools to local ones, **many federal boarding schools closed in the 1980s**, and four off-reservation boarding schools are still run by the Bureau of Indian Education (era 10 must re-check that number).

**2. What was done to the children, named where the record names them.**
- **Ronald Yazzie, 9, and Willie B. Yazzie, 13**, sons of Mr. and Mrs. Norman Yazzie of Chaco Canyon, ran away from the **Crownpoint Boarding School** in New Mexico with their brother **Ray Yazzie, 12**, trying to walk about 50 miles home just after the Christmas holiday. They wore jeans, shorts, shoes and light jackets. Ronald and Willie were **found frozen to death on 13 January**, about 30 miles northeast of Crownpoint, after a night about 10 degrees below zero. Ray survived with frostbite. Navajo Police superintendent **Al Adams** said this was "the first incident this winter of schoolboys being found frozen to death after running away from a boarding school." Officer **Stewart Silentman**: "They were lonesome for home and just took off." (Bill Donovan, "50 Years Ago: Boys freeze after running away from boarding school," *Navajo Times*, 11 January 2018, https://navajotimes.com/50years/50years-ago-boys-freeze-running-away-boarding-school/ , quoting a BIA press release.) **The year is January 1968, read from the column's "50 Years Ago" date; the Kennedy Report (1969) prints a Tuba City teacher's letter mentioning "the two young boys who froze to death while running away from a boarding school" trying to reach homes 50 miles away, which matches.** These are named children with a documented death. They qualify for an `hb-story` candidate or a named sentence in the span.
- **The Tuba City teacher's letter** (Kennedy Report): children from age 6 saw their parents only on occasional weekends; parents were "allowed to check out their children" only if the school judged the child's conduct good; a child who had run away could be kept from going home for up to a month; at **Toadlena Boarding School**, the teacher had been told, parents were not allowed to take children home on weekends at all. The teacher is unnamed in the report.
- **"Children are beaten"**: the Kennedy Report quotes an anthropologist's report of a Navajo Reservation boarding school where "Children are beaten, pervasive attacks are made against their cultural beliefs, classes start with the Lord's Prayer, and teachers advocate the free labor of Navajo girls in their homes." **The school and the anthropologist are not named in the text read** (footnote 28 cites the source; not followed). Print it as the Senate report's quotation, unnamed.
- The report found BIA schools had "a major emphasis on discipline and punishment which is deeply resented by the students."

**3. The Indian Child Welfare Act, 1978.**
- **Public Law 95-608, approved 8 November 1978**, 92 Stat. 3069 (govinfo, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-92/pdf/STATUTE-92-Pg3069.pdf ). Congress found "that an alarmingly high percentage of Indian families are broken up by the removal, often unwarranted, of their children from them by nontribal public and private agencies and that an alarmingly high percentage of such children are placed in non-Indian foster and adoptive homes and institutions."
- **House Report 95-1386**: surveys by the **Association on American Indian Affairs in 1969 and 1974** found "approximately **25-35 percent** of all Indian children are separated from their families and placed in foster homes, adoptive homes, or institutions." In 16 states surveyed in 1969 about **85 percent** of Indian children in foster care were in non-Indian homes. In South Dakota 40 percent of state adoptions since 1967-68 were of Indian children, who were 7 percent of the young population. The report: "The Federal boarding school and dormitory programs also contribute to the destruction of Indian family and community life," and "BIA boarding schools are full of children with such spurious 'behavioral problems.'" **Who removed the children: state welfare departments, courts and private agencies ("nontribal public and private agencies," in the statute's words), and the BIA through its schools.**
- The bill was introduced by Representative **Udall** and others (first name not given in the text read) (House report, footnote 1).

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): how many children were hit at school, 1976-2000

Source: Deana Pollard Sacks, "State Actors Beating Children: A Call for Judicial Relief," *UC Davis Law Review* 42 (2009), p. 1171 and note 11 (https://lawreview.law.ucdavis.edu/sites/g/files/dgvnsk15026/files/media/documents/42-4_Sacks.pdf , read with pypdf), citing the U.S. Department of Education Office for Civil Rights' Elementary and Secondary School Civil Rights Compliance Reports and their projected national values.

- **1976: about 1,521,896** public school students paddled.
- **1986: 1,099,731.**
- **1990: 613,514.**
- **2000: 342,038.** (Same figure for 2000-01 in Human Rights Watch, *A Violent Education*, August 2008, ch. V, https://www.hrw.org/reports/2008/us0808/5.htm , citing OCR.)
- Sacks, note 10: districts report the numbers themselves with no independent check, and "Some researchers have found that the Office of Civil Rights data severely underestimates the extent of school corporal punishment and that the true numbers may be twice as high as reported."
- **Whose count:** these count students, as reported by districts and projected nationally by OCR. Gershoff and Font's "4% of all schoolchildren in 1978" (§9l) is a share, from the same federal data. **The outline's 1978 share can now carry a number beside it: about one and a half million children in 1976, falling to about 340,000 by 2000.**
- DECISIONS #4: **James Ingraham and Roosevelt Andrews** (§9l) are the named beaten children for this era, from the Supreme Court record. No other named child was sought.

### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): outline claims and "firsts" checked for era 9

- **"Nobody ended it after four years" (NDEA).** Supported: the Federal Student Aid *Handbook* 2000-01, vol. 5 introduction (https://fsapartners.ed.gov/sites/default/files/2021-03/Vol5intro.pdf , pypdf) says National Defense Student Loans were made until **1 July 1972** and the program continued as National Direct Student Loans and then **Federal Perkins Loans**. The loans alone ran fourteen years under the first name.
- **Navajo Community College, 1968, first tribally controlled college.** Diné College's own history page (https://www.dinecollege.edu/about_dc/history/ , curl works): "established in 1968 as the first tribally-controlled community college in the United States." The outline's "first college run by a Native nation" is the college's own claim; say "the first college run by a Native nation" only with "community college" or attribute it.
- **"*Growing Without Schooling*, the first of its kind in the country."** The bank (§9n and PART B) has it only as "reported to be" and "likely" the first. **Unsupported as a flat first. The outline should drop "the first of its kind" or attribute it.**
- **"Milton Gaither, who wrote the only full history of American homeschooling."** §9n asserts "the only book-length scholarly history" with no source for "only." **Unsupported superlative. The outline should say "a historian who wrote a history of American homeschooling."**
- **"The first big-city teachers' union in the country to win" bargaining (UFT, 1961)** and **"the first statewide teachers' strike" (Florida 1968)**: §9k labels both as journalism and secondary summaries (Zinn Education Project citing Noll, *Florida Historical Quarterly* 2017). Keep, as §9k allows.
- **"The first magnet school" (McCarver, 1968)**, **"the first charter school law" (Minnesota 1991)**, **"the first Black student to finish Central High" (Ernest Green)**, **"first indigenous-language immersion classes in the United States" (1987)**: each sourced in §9m, §9a-2 and §9f-3; the last is ʻAha Pūnana Leo's own history, so attribute it if printed as a first.
- **Land (Find item 5):** no newcomers take or settle land in the era-9 events. Navajo Community College opened on the Navajo Nation's own land (26,000 square miles across Arizona, New Mexico and Utah, Diné College page). Nothing to record.
- **Perishable facts (Find item 4):** era 9 holds none. The Blackwell School dates (site established July 2024) and the UCLA 2014 figure are era 10's and were not re-checked here.

### Carry-forward decisions 2026-09-28 (T-261d)

- **Daniel Freeman's 1902 Nebraska case**: §9d already decided to leave it in era 8's bank. Unchanged.
- ***Buck v. Bell* and Virginia's sterilisation law to April 1974**: not a schooling fact. Nothing in the era-9 education bank or outline uses it. Left for `health` or `rights-movements`.
- **Delgado's loophole**: closed by the *Hernandez v. Driscoll* patch above.
- **Boarding-school peak and the Kennedy Report**: re-sourced above (Heard Museum, GSU, ERIC ED034625), with the federal counts beside the 60,000.
- **Rhoads's 1930 circular**: era 8, not touched.
- **East Los Angeles walkouts, 1968** (parked by `rights-movements`, PART B): `rights-movements` tells them. The "No Spanish" span can carry one pointer sentence; the sourced text is in `research/research-rights-movements.md` era 9.


### PATCH 2026-09-28 (T-261d): unit check, the three verified story blocks against the bank

Every fact in `ruby-bridges`, `peter-mills-education` and `dejonge-family-homeschool` was checked against §9b, §9p and §9o. Two facts in the blocks had no bank line. Both are now sourced:

- **Ruby Bridges "went to a segregated kindergarten in 1959."** NPS, "Ruby Bridges," https://www.nps.gov/people/rubybridges.htm (curl works): "Ruby first attended a segregated kindergarten in 1959." Same page: "Ruby and five other students passed the test. Two students decided to stay at their school." (NPS's next sentence says the others "were sent to the all-white McDonough Elementary School," which is wrong for Ruby, who went to Frantz as NPS itself says in the next line. Do not copy that sentence.)
- **Peter Mills: "The school board's answer was partly that it did not have the money."** Disability Justice, "The Right to Education," https://disabilityjustice.org/basic-legal-rights/right-to-education/ (curl works), summarising *Mills*: the defendants accepted the duty to provide "such instruction" "but argued that it was impossible to do so because they lacked the necessary financial resources." The court's own words, quoted on that page and by AUCD (https://aucdpolicytalk.org/2025/07/31/celebrating-mills-and-the-families-who-won-this-important-case/ ): "If sufficient funds are not available to finance all of the services and programs that are needed and desirable in the system, then the available funds must be expended equitably in such a manner that no child is entirely excluded from a publicly supported education consistent with his needs and ability to benefit therefrom." The case was filed on **24 September 1971** (Civil Rights Litigation Clearinghouse, https://clearinghouse.net/case/11084/ ), which supports "later that year."
- Every other fact in the three blocks matches its bank section.


# PART F — Era 10, researched (2000-Today)

**Written 2026-09-09 by the era-10 research agent.** This era is made almost entirely of
perishable numbers, so **every figure below carries two years: the year the number describes
and the year its source was published.** Where the newest published figure is several years
old, that is said plainly rather than dressed up as "today." Items the outline tagged
`[VERIFY]` are marked **[V]** with the tag they close.

**Access notes added by this pass** (running list in PART C-ACCESS): **congress.gov returns
HTTP 403** to WebFetch — **govinfo.gov's `content/pkg/PLAW-<congress>publ<num>/html/` route works
and returns the full statute text**, and that is how Public Law 107-110 was read.
**npr.org times out in WebFetch but `curl` plus a tag-strip returns the whole article.**
**PDFs from ed.gov and apsac.org return binary to WebFetch; download and run `pdftotext -layout`.**
Working this pass: nces.ed.gov (tables and Condition of Education indicators), ed.gov media
documents, govinfo.gov, census.gov, edchoice.org, cdc.gov, npr.org via curl.

## 10a. No Child Left Behind, and the law that replaced it [V — closes "No Child Left Behind, 2001" and "its replacement in 2015 [VERIFY the name]"]

**Both tags are answered, and the first one needs a correction the outline should carry.**

- **The law is the No Child Left Behind Act of 2001, Public Law 107-110, and it was approved on
  8 January 2002.** The name carries 2001 because that is the Congress and session that passed it;
  the signature is January 2002. Print both or the reader meets a contradiction later.
  (Source: govinfo.gov, PLAW-107publ110, full statute text,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-107publ110/html/PLAW-107publ110.htm .)
- **Its stated purpose, verbatim from the act:** "to close the achievement gap with accountability,
  flexibility, and choice, so that no child is left behind." (Same source.)
- **The testing requirement, verbatim from the act:** states must "measure the achievement of
  students against the challenging State academic content and student academic achievement
  standards in each of grades 3 through 8 in, at a minimum, mathematics, and reading or language
  arts," **beginning in the 2005-2006 school year.** Science tests were required "not less than once
  during" grades 3-5, grades 6-9, and grades 10-12. (Same source.)
- **The deadline, verbatim:** "not later than 12 years after the end of the 2001-2002 school year,
  all students in each group ... will meet or exceed the State's proficient level of academic
  achievement." **That is the 2013-2014 school year.** Every child in the country was to be
  proficient in reading and mathematics by then. (Same source.)
- **The replacement is the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), signed by President Obama on
  10 December 2015.** The Department of Education's own page: "The Every Student Succeeds Act
  (ESSA) was signed by President Obama on December 10, 2015"; "The previous version of the law,
  the No Child Left Behind (NCLB) Act, was enacted in 2002"; and NCLB's "prescriptive requirements
  became increasingly unworkable for schools and educators."
  (Source: U.S. Department of Education, "Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA): A New Education Law,"
  https://www.ed.gov/laws-and-policy/laws-preschool-grade-12-education/every-student-succeeds-act-essa/every-student-succeeds-act-essa-new-education-law .)
- **Both are the same 1965 law rewritten.** NCLB and ESSA are reauthorisations of the Elementary
  and Secondary Education Act of 1965, which era 9 covers in PART E §9e. The chain is
  ESEA 1965 → NCLB 2001/2002 → ESSA 2015.

**What is NOT settled and should not be printed as if it were:** whether the testing regime raised
or lowered achievement. Researchers disagree, and this pass did not find a settlement. Keep the
outline to what the law required and what it did to the school year.

---

## 10b. Charter schools — the current share, with its year [V — closes "the share of pupils in them [VERIFY the current figure and its year]"]

**Newest confirmed federal figure: the 2022-23 school year.**

- **2022-23: 7,998 charter schools, 3,717,857 pupils, 7.6 percent of all public school enrolment.**
- 2021-22: 7,847 schools, 3,674,712 pupils, 7.5 percent.
- 2017-18: 7,193 schools, 3,143,269 pupils, 6.2 percent.
- 2012-13: 6,079 schools, 2,267,814 pupils, 4.6 percent.
- (Source: NCES, *Digest of Education Statistics 2023*, Table 216.90,
  https://nces.ed.gov/programs/digest/d23/tables/dt23_216.90.asp . Source note on the table:
  "Common Core of Data (CCD), 'Public Elementary/Secondary School Universe Survey,' 2012-13 through
  2022-23. (This table was prepared February 2024.)")
- **The Condition of Education indicator, published May 2023, gives the fall 2021 figure as
  3.7 million and 7 percent, up from 1.8 million and 4 percent in fall 2010,** and says charter
  enrolment "more than doubled, from 1.8 million to 3.7 million students — an overall increase of
  1.9 million students," while traditional public school enrolment fell 4 percent over the same
  stretch. (Source: NCES, Condition of Education, "Public Charter School Enrollment,"
  https://nces.ed.gov/programs/coe/indicator/cgb/public-charter-enrollment .)
- **Use the Digest table figure — 7.6 percent in 2022-23 — and give the school year with it.**
  A table for 2023-24 (`dt24_216.90.asp`) was not on the NCES site when this pass ran, so
  **2022-23 is the newest year the federal count actually covers, and the book should say so.**
- **Where charters came from is era 9,** PART E §9m: the first charter law, Minnesota 1991; the
  first charter, Bluffview Montessori, Winona, December 1991; the first charter school open,
  City Academy, St. Paul, autumn 1992.

## 10c. Private and parochial enrolment now [V — closes "parochial and private enrollment now [VERIFY]"]

**Newest confirmed federal figure: fall 2021, published May 2024. It is three years older than the
book's writing date, and the book should say the year out loud.**

- **Fall 2021: 4.7 million children in private schools from kindergarten through grade 12,
  9 percent of all American schoolchildren, public and private together.**
- The split by kind of school, fall 2021: **Catholic schools 1.7 million; other religious schools
  2.0 million; nonsectarian (no church) schools 1.1 million.**
- Ten years earlier, fall 2011: 4.5 million in private schools, also 9 percent. Catholic
  1.9 million; other religious 1.7 million; nonsectarian 0.9 million.
- **So the total held steady and the mix changed: Catholic schools lost about 200,000 children in
  ten years while other religious schools gained about 300,000.**
- Private enrolment stayed at 4.7 million between fall 2019 and fall 2021 while public enrolment
  fell 2 percent.
- (Source: NCES, Condition of Education, "Private School Enrollment," published May 2024,
  https://nces.ed.gov/programs/coe/indicator/cgc/private-school-enrollment .)
- **The 2023-24 Private School Universe Survey was still being collected and processed when this
  pass ran, so fall 2021 is the newest complete federal count.** (Source: NCES, Private School
  Universe Survey overview, https://nces.ed.gov/surveys/pss/ .)
- **The long Catholic decline is era 9's,** PART E §9m: the peak of 5,601,000 children in 13,249
  Catholic schools in 1964-65, down to 2,475,439 by 1990-91. The 1.7 million of fall 2021 is the
  end of that same curve — under a third of the 1964-65 peak.

## 10d. Discipline now — corporal punishment, suspension and expulsion [V — closes "the current number of states, the number of children struck in the most recent year counted, and the source year, and must not use a stale figure"]

**The federal count is the Civil Rights Data Collection (CRDC), a survey the Office for Civil
Rights requires every public school district to answer. The newest one covers the 2021-22 school
year and was released in January 2025.** It covers 17,704 public school districts and 98,010
public schools in the fifty states, Washington D.C. and Puerto Rico.
(Source: U.S. Department of Education, Office for Civil Rights, *2021-22 Civil Rights Data
Collection — A First Look: Students' Access to Educational Opportunities in U.S. Public Schools*,
January 2025, https://www.ed.gov/media/document/2021-22-crdc-first-look-report-109194.pdf ,
read with `pdftotext -layout`.)

**The report itself warns readers to remember what year they are looking at**, verbatim: "OCR
cautions readers and data users to consider the impact of the coronavirus pandemic on students and
on education conditions when comparing these data to the 2020-21 CRDC." **2021-22 is the first
full school year back in buildings after the closures, and that is part of what the numbers mean.**

### Corporal punishment — how many children, and in how many states

- **About 24,500 K-12 public school children were hit at school in the 2021-22 school year**, in
  the CRDC's own words: "Approximately 24,500 K-12 public school students received corporal
  punishment." (CRDC First Look, January 2025, p. 25.)
- **NPR's count of the same federal data gives almost 32,000 reported instances in 2021-22 —
  about one reported instance per 2,000 pupils.** The two numbers are not in conflict: the CRDC
  counts *children* who were hit at least once, NPR counts *instances*, so some children were hit
  more than once. **Print both only if you say which is which; otherwise print the children.**
  (Source: Kadin Mills, "15 states have laws that allow corporal punishment in schools. Here's
  where, and why," NPR, 19 August 2026,
  https://www.npr.org/2026/08/19/nx-s1-5803505/school-corporal-punishment-policy-tracker .)
- **Who is hit, from the CRDC's own tables (2021-22), as percentage of enrolment against percentage
  of children hit:**
  - Boys were 51 percent of enrolment and the overwhelming share of those hit. Girls of every group
    were hit at a lower share than their enrolment.
  - **White boys: 23 percent of K-12 enrolment, 47 percent of children who received corporal
    punishment.**
  - **Black boys: 8 percent of enrolment, 20 percent of those hit.**
  - **American Indian or Alaska Native boys: under 1 percent of enrolment, 2 percent of those hit.**
  - Boys of two or more races: 2 percent of enrolment, 3 percent of those hit.
  - Hispanic boys: 15 percent of enrolment, 9 percent of those hit.
  - Children with disabilities served under IDEA: 14 percent of enrolment, 12 percent of those hit
    — the one group hit *less* than its share.
  - English learners: 11 percent of enrolment, 4 percent of those hit.
- **Number of states, 2026:** NPR's own analysis, published 19 August 2026, found **15 states whose
  laws allow schools to use corporal punishment**, and **30 states plus Washington D.C. that have
  passed laws explicitly banning it in all public schools.** The remaining five neither allow it
  outright nor ban it outright: "some have other laws that have been interpreted as bans, and
  others have no laws at all." (NPR, 19 August 2026, as above.)
- **Sources genuinely disagree on the state count, and the disagreement should be stated, not
  resolved.** An alert from the American Professional Society on the Abuse of Children (APSAC
  ALERT Vol. 14, No. 2) counts **32 states banning it and 18 without a ban** — 15 that explicitly
  permit it, plus Connecticut, Indiana and Kansas which simply have no law either way. That alert
  says so itself, verbatim: "Varying estimates of the number of states that still permit corporal
  punishment in public schools demonstrates the uncertainty surrounding current legislation on this
  matter," and notes that the U.S. Department of Education in 2023 counted New Hampshire, South
  Dakota and Maine as states without bans while Gershoff and Font in 2016 concluded Connecticut
  does ban it. (Source: Esaa Mohammad S. Samarah, Lisa Schelbe and Bart Klika, "The State of
  Corporal Punishment in U.S. Public Schools and Trauma-Informed Education Policy," APSAC ALERT
  Vol. 14, No. 2, https://apsac.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/20058.pdf .)
  **Caution for whoever writes the prose: that alert's own list of the "fifteen states" that
  explicitly permit it names only fourteen — Alabama, Arizona, Arkansas, Florida, Georgia,
  Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, Missouri, North Carolina, South Carolina, Oklahoma, Tennessee
  and Texas. Do not print that list as fifteen states, and do not print it as complete.**
- **Safe wording:** "In 2026 the laws of 15 states still let a school hit a child, and 30 states and
  Washington D.C. have banned it outright. Counts differ, because a few states have no law either
  way." That is what the sources support.
- **Where it happens:** "Almost all of the country's reported instances of public school corporal
  punishment happened in eight states — primarily in the South — according to federal data," and
  Arkansas has the second-highest rate in the country, after Mississippi. (NPR, 19 August 2026.)
  **NPR does not list the eight states; do not name them.**
- **The comparison to make with era 9:** at the end of the 1990s an adult at school could still
  lawfully hit a child in twenty-three states (PART E §9l). By 2026 that is fifteen.
- **How current is "current":** the CRDC normally comes out about every two years, and as of
  August 2026 the 2023-24 collection was **more than six months late**. Not every district reports
  corporal punishment. (NPR, 19 August 2026.) **So 2021-22 is the newest year there is, and the
  book must say the year.**

### Suspension and expulsion — who gets them, 2021-22

All figures below are the CRDC First Look, January 2025, for the **2021-22 school year**.

- **About 2.3 million children, 5 percent of K-12 public school enrolment, got one or more
  in-school suspensions. About 2.4 million, also 5 percent, got one or more out-of-school
  suspensions.**
- **Black boys were 8 percent of K-12 enrolment and were 18 percent of children given one or more
  in-school suspensions, 22 percent of those given one or more out-of-school suspensions, and
  21 percent of those expelled.** The report's own sentence: "Black boys were disciplined at higher
  rates, compared to boys of other races."
- **Black girls were 7 percent of enrolment, 11 percent of in-school suspensions, 13 percent of
  out-of-school suspensions and 13 percent of expulsions.**
- White boys were 23 percent of enrolment, 27 percent of in-school suspensions and 24 percent of
  expulsions. Hispanic boys were 15 percent of enrolment, 16 percent of both kinds of suspension
  and 17 percent of expulsions. Boys of two or more races were 2 percent of enrolment and 3 to
  4 percent of each.
- White girls were 22 percent of enrolment, 11 percent of in-school suspensions, 9 percent of
  out-of-school suspensions and 10 percent of expulsions. **Girls of every group except Black girls
  were suspended and expelled at less than their share of enrolment.**
- **Children with disabilities were 17 percent of K-12 enrolment, 27 percent of in-school
  suspensions, 29 percent of out-of-school suspensions and 24 percent of expulsions.**
- English learners were 11 percent of enrolment and 8 to 9 percent of each kind of discipline —
  less than their share.
- **Police at school:** districts referred about 220,000 K-12 children to law enforcement, and about
  17 percent of those referrals ended in a school-related arrest. **Black children were 15 percent
  of enrolment, 28 percent of children referred to police and 33 percent of children arrested at
  school.** American Indian or Alaska Native children were 1 percent of enrolment, 2 percent of
  referrals and 2 percent of arrests. Children with disabilities served under IDEA were 14 percent
  of enrolment and 25 percent of both referrals and arrests.
- **The youngest children:** about 2,700 public preschool children got one or more out-of-school
  suspensions and about 370 preschool children were expelled, out of 1.4 million children in public
  preschool. **Black preschool children were 18 percent of preschool enrolment, 38 percent of those
  suspended out of school and 33 percent of those expelled.** Preschool boys were 54 percent of
  enrolment, 81 percent of those suspended and 84 percent of those expelled. Preschool children with
  disabilities were 23 percent of enrolment, 41 percent of those suspended and **74 percent of
  those expelled.**
- **Bullying and harassment, same year:** over 80,000 public school children reported being harassed
  or bullied because of their sex, race or disability. **Black children were 15 percent of enrolment
  and 38 percent of children who reported being harassed or bullied because of their race.**

**A note on how to say this.** These are shares, not rates per child, and the honest sentence names
the group and both numbers: "Black boys were 8 in every 100 public school children. They were 22 in
every 100 of the children sent home on suspension." Do not compress it to "Black students are
suspended more," which drops the size of the gap, and do not soften "expelled" to "removed."

---

## 10e. Vouchers, education savings accounts and tax-credit scholarships [V — closes "vouchers and education savings accounts [VERIFY]"]

**What they are, in words a child can hold:**
- **A voucher** is a payment from the state that a family can put towards a private school's fees
  instead of sending the child to the local public school.
- **An education savings account (ESA)** is a pot of state money set aside for one named child. The
  family can spend it on several things — private school fees, a tutor, books, an online course —
  not only on one school's fees.
- **A tax-credit scholarship** does the same job through the tax system. A person or a company gives
  money to a scholarship fund, and the state takes the same amount off their tax bill.
- **A tax-credit ESA** is the two combined: the money comes from tax-credited donations and is then
  held as a per-child account.

**The scale, 2024-25 school year, from EdChoice's programme-by-programme count** (EdChoice supports
these programmes and is the organisation that keeps the national tally; label it that way in the
text). The page carried update dates ranging from 18 March 2025 to 12 August 2026:
- **69 programmes in total** — 21 ESA programmes in 18 states, 23 voucher programmes in 15 states,
  22 tax-credit scholarship programmes in 18 states, and 3 tax-credit ESA programmes in 3 states.
- **Children taking part in 2024-25: 488,736 in ESAs; 349,923 on vouchers; 217,194 on tax-credit
  scholarships; 106,442 on tax-credit ESAs (all of the last in Florida). Those four add to
  1,162,295.** EdChoice's own headline figure of about 1.5 million also counts more than 332,000 tax
  returns claiming individual tax credits or deductions, which is households rather than children.
  **Print the four programme numbers and the sum; do not print 1.5 million as a count of children.**
- (Source: EdChoice, "Fast Facts," https://www.edchoice.org/school-choice/fast-facts/ .)
- **Growth:** EdChoice reported private school choice programme use up about 25 percent in a single
  year, with Florida's tax-credit ESA and ESA programmes going from 345,223 children in 2023-24 to
  449,467 in 2024-25, and Indiana's from 82,040 to 87,536. Indiana's Choice Scholarship Program was
  the largest voucher programme in the country by participation, at 75,269 in 2024-25. (Source:
  EdChoice, "2025: Families Are Voting With Their Feet — and Their Tax Dollars,"
  https://www.edchoice.org/2025-families-are-voting-with-their-feetand-their-tax-dollars/ .)

**Not settled, and left out of the outline for that reason:** whether these programmes raise or
lower achievement, and how many of the children using them were already in private school. Studies
disagree and this pass did not find a settlement.

---

## 10f. Homeschooling — the growth, the 2020 jump, and how much stayed [V — closes "the jump during and after 2020 and how much of it stayed [VERIFY the figures and the source year]"]

**Before 2020, from the federal survey that counts homeschooling (the National Household Education
Survey, NHES):**
- 1999: **1.7 percent** of children aged 5 to 17. 2012: **3.4 percent.**
  (Source: NCES Fast Facts, "Homeschooling," https://nces.ed.gov/fastfacts/display.asp?id=91 ,
  which lists NHES administrations in 1999, 2003, 2007, 2012, 2016 and 2019.)
- **2019: 2.8 percent, "approximately 1.5 million homeschooled students."** A further **1.2 percent**
  were in full-time virtual education, and the combined "instruction-at-home rate is 3.7 percent."
  (Source: Lisa Hudson, Talia Kaatz, Danielle Battle, L. Jane Hall, Steven Bahr and Sandy Eyster,
  *2019 Homeschooling and Full-Time Virtual Education Rates*, NCES 2023-101, **September 2023**,
  https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2023/2023101.pdf , read with `pdftotext -layout`. The data year is
  2018-19.)
- **Why parents said they homeschooled, 2019:** concern about the school environment (80 percent of
  homeschooled children had a parent giving this reason), wanting to provide moral instruction
  (75 percent), wanting to emphasise family life together (75 percent), dissatisfaction with
  academic instruction at other schools (73 percent). Asked for the single most important reason,
  the commonest answer was concern about the school environment (25 percent). (Same source.)
- **2019 pattern:** white children were homeschooled more often than Black or Hispanic children,
  children in rural areas more often than children elsewhere, and children in grades K-8 more often
  than children in grades 9-12 (3.4 percent against 2.3 percent). (Same source.)

**The 2020 jump, from the Census Bureau's Household Pulse Survey:**
- **Spring 2020 (survey run 23 April to 5 May): 5.4 percent of households with school-age children
  reported homeschooling. Autumn 2020 (survey run 30 September to 12 October): 11.1 percent** — a
  rise of 5.6 percentage points, a doubling.
- **Black households went from 3.3 percent to 16.1 percent, a fivefold rise.**
- The Census Bureau's own article stresses that the question was worded to exclude a public school
  teaching remotely, so this is homeschooling proper.
- (Source: U.S. Census Bureau, "Homeschooling on the Rise During COVID-19 Pandemic," published
  **22 March 2021**,
  https://www.census.gov/library/stories/2021/03/homeschooling-on-the-rise-during-covid-19-pandemic.html .)

**How much of it stayed — the answer the outline asks for, and it is a solid one:**
- **In the 2022-23 school year, 5.82 percent of school-age children were reported as homeschooled**,
  against **10.06 percent in private schools and 84.12 percent in public schools.** Week by week
  across the twelve survey rounds the homeschool figure ranged from **5.14 percent to 6.66 percent.**
- **The authors' own conclusion, verbatim:** homeschooling "has not only remained steady during the
  following school years but has even increased slightly, at 5.82% for the 2022–2023 school year.
  This counters the prediction of education experts that the homeschool population would decline as
  schools began to re-open after school closures during the height of the pandemic."
- **State spread in the same data:** Alaska highest at **12.6 percent**, then Tennessee **9 percent**
  and West Virginia **8.9 percent**; lowest Rhode Island **2.9 percent**, Massachusetts
  **3.1 percent**, New York **3.2 percent**.
- (Source: Genevieve Smith and Angela R. Watson, "Estimating Homeschool Participation in the U.S. —
  What We Can Learn from the Household Pulse Survey," *Journal of School Choice*, vol. 18, no. 4,
  2024, pp. 501-517. Read from the PDF at educacaodomiciliar.fe.unicamp.br with `pdftotext -layout`.)
- **A caution the authors give and the book should keep:** these are survey responses from
  households that chose to answer, "not the actual enrollment number," and different surveys define
  homeschooling differently, so a Pulse figure and an NHES figure are not the same measurement.
  **Say "about twice the pre-2020 level," which both sources support, rather than putting a Pulse
  number and an NHES number in the same sentence as if they were one series.**
- **A later reading of the same survey puts 2023-24 at about 6 percent**, holding rather than
  falling. (Source: Fordham Institute commentary on Watson's work,
  https://fordhaminstitute.org/national/commentary/new-picture-modern-homeschooling-america .)
  **The outline uses the peer-reviewed 2022-23 figure, which is the strongest one available.**

---

## 10g. How states check homeschooling — the range [V — closes "how states check it, which varies enormously [VERIFY]"]

**There is no national rule. Every requirement below is a state's own, and the gap between the
loosest and the strictest state is the finding.**

**National counts kept by the Coalition for Responsible Home Education (CRHE), page last updated
March 2026** (https://crhe.org/research/current-policy/ ). **CRHE argues for more oversight of
homeschooling; label it that way when citing it, the way the outline does.**
- **"12 states don't require notice at all"** — a family need not tell anyone it is homeschooling.
  Most states require notice, either once or every year.
- **"29 states do not require that homeschooled students be assessed for educational progress at any
  point during their schooling."** Eighteen states require assessment for some options but with
  limits, and **"only 3 states require that every homeschooled child be assessed."** (29 + 18 + 3 = 50.)
- **"Only 31 states require home educators to teach core subjects, like English and math."**
- **"22 states do not require caregivers to keep any academic records on file at all,"** and
  **"only two states require homeschooling caregivers to maintain identity, immunization, and
  academic records."**

**The loose end, Texas** (CRHE state page, last updated June 2026, https://crhe.org/state-by-state/texas/ ):
- "Texas does not require home educators to notify the state that they are homeschooling."
- "Texas requires home educators to teach some core subjects."
- "Texas does not require home educators to assess students for academic progress."
- "Texas does not require caregivers to retain academic records."

**The tight end, New York** (CRHE state page, https://responsiblehomeschooling.org/new-york/ ,
last updated April 2023):
- "Parents must submit annual written notice of intent to the local superintendent by July 1 of each
  school year."
- An individualised home instruction plan (IHIP): "Within four weeks of receiving the IHIP forms,
  parents must submit the completed forms to the school district."
- "Parents must file quarterly reports including the number of hours of instruction during the
  quarter; a description of the material covered in each subject."
- "Parents must file an annual assessment at the same time that they file their fourth quarterly
  report."
- Required subjects are set by grade band; for grades 1-6 they are "arithmetic, reading, spelling,
  writing, the English language, geography, United States history, science, health education, music,
  visual arts, physical education."

**What is NOT settled and stays out of the outline:** how well any of these rules are enforced, and
how many homeschooled children there are in each state, because 15 states and the District of
Columbia collect no homeschool data at all (CRHE notes that 35 states and D.C. report some) and the
survey estimates and the state counts disagree by as much as 7.6 percentage points in Tennessee.
(Source: Smith and Watson, as in §10f.) **Era 9's finding still stands: the record does not support
naming a "last state to legalise homeschooling," and this pass found nothing to change that.**

---

## 10h. The school calendar, experiments with changing it, and start times [V — closes "the school calendar and experiments with changing it [VERIFY]"]

### The 180-day year
- **Of the 38 states that set a minimum number of instructional days, 27 of them plus Washington D.C.
  require 180** — "the closest thing the country has to a national norm."
- **Seven states set a minimum below 180, the lowest being Colorado at 160 days. Four require more
  than 180, the highest being Kansas at 186 days for grades K-11.**
- Some states set hours instead of, or as well as, days, and the hours vary widely (Arizona
  720 hours for eleventh graders, Texas 1,260).
- (Source: Pew Research Center, "In the U.S., 180 days of school is most common, but length of school
  day varies by state," **7 September 2023**, built on Education Commission of the States data that
  Pew double-checked against state statutes.
  https://www.pewresearch.org/short-reads/2023/09/07/in-the-u-s-180-days-of-school-is-most-common-but-length-of-school-day-varies-by-state/ )

### Year-round school — tried, and shrinking
- Percentage of public schools with **all** pupils on a year-round cycle: **4.4 percent in 2007-08,
  3.2 percent in 2011-12, 2.5 percent in 2017-18.** Percentage on any kind of twelve-month cycle:
  5.5, 4.1 and 3.0 percent in the same three years.
- (Source: NCES, National Teacher and Principal Survey,
  https://nces.ed.gov/surveys/ntps/tables/ntps1718_20050401_s1n.asp .)
- **This is the honest shape: the year-round experiment peaked and has been shrinking. Do not write
  it as a growing trend.**

### The four-day week — growing
- **About 2,100 schools in almost 900 school districts across 26 states**, as of **September 2023**,
  "about double what it was 10 years ago—and almost a 40% increase from what it was prior to the
  pandemic and in 2019."
- Counted by **Paul Thompson, an economist at Oregon State University**, quoted by SciLine,
  **12 September 2023**, https://www.sciline.org/social-sciences/4-day-school-week/ .
- **Missouri, counted by Jon Turner of Missouri State University:** "A record 105 Missouri school
  districts—20 percent—embrace four-day weeks" (2020); "A record 128 Missouri school districts—25
  percent—switch to four-day weeks" (2022); an estimated 178 Missouri public (non-charter) schools
  on a four-day week for 2024-25. (Source: the Four-Day School Week Policy project's own page,
  https://sites.google.com/view/four-day-school-week/home . The page itself notes that its list and
  the Missouri education department's list differ, "Due to incomplete data and lack of a definition
  of '4 day week'." **Say the counts are estimates.**)

### Start times
- **The American Academy of Pediatrics said in a 2014 policy statement that middle and high schools
  "should aim for a starting time of no earlier than 8:30 am,"** because starting earlier costs
  adolescents sleep. (Source: American Academy of Pediatrics, "School Start Times for Adolescents,"
  *Pediatrics* 134(3), 2014, p. 642,
  https://publications.aap.org/pediatrics/article/134/3/642/74175/School-Start-Times-for-Adolescents .)
- **In the 2020-21 school year the average American public secondary/high school started at 8:07.**
  The spread: **8.8 percent before 7:30; 27.5 percent from 7:30 to 7:59; 35.5 percent from 8:00 to
  8:29; 28.2 percent at 8:30 or later.** So **71.8 percent started before 8:30.**
  (Source: NCES, National Teacher and Principal Survey 2020-21,
  https://nces.ed.gov/surveys/ntps/estable/table/ntps/ntps2021_srt04_s1s .)
- Earlier reading for comparison: the average public high school start time was **8:00 a.m. in
  2017-18** (NCES 2020-006, *Start Time for U.S. Public High Schools*).
- **California wrote it into law.** Senate Bill 328, **approved by the Governor and filed with the
  Secretary of State on 13 October 2019**: "The schoolday for middle schools, including middle
  schools operated as charter schools, shall begin no earlier than 8:00 a.m." and "The schoolday for
  high schools, including high schools operated as charter schools, shall begin no earlier than
  8:30 a.m." Compliance date: "no later than July 1, 2022, or the date on which a school district's
  or charter school's respective collective bargaining agreement that is operative on January 1,
  2020, expires, whichever is later." And: "This section shall not apply to rural school districts."
  (Source: California Legislative Information, SB-328 (2019-2020),
  https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/billTextClient.xhtml?bill_id=201920200SB328 .)
- **Florida passed the same idea and then took it back before it started.** The 2023 law would have
  barred middle schools from starting before 8:00 a.m. and high schools before 8:30 a.m. **starting
  in 2026.** The **Florida Senate voted unanimously on 27 March 2025** for **Senate Bill 296**, which
  removes the mandate and instead requires a district that does not adopt later start times to file a
  report on what it considered and what got in the way. Districts had said they lacked the buses and
  the drivers. (Source: WUSF, "Florida Senate votes to reverse later school start time mandate,"
  27 March 2025,
  https://www.wusf.org/education/2025-03-27/florida-senate-votes-reverse-later-school-start-time-mandate .
  **Later reporting says the Governor signed the repeal and that districts must report to the state
  by 1 June 2026; that detail was not confirmed from a primary source in this pass, so the outline
  stops at the Senate vote.**)

### A device for every child
- **Spring 2020: 45 percent** of public school principals reported providing computers or digital
  devices for pupils to use at home (NCES, National Teacher and Principal Survey).
- **September 2020: 59 percent** of adults with a child enrolled in school reported that the
  computer was provided by the school or district (Census Bureau, Household Pulse Survey).
- **September 2021: 96 percent** of public schools reported providing digital devices such as
  tablets to pupils who needed them; **70 percent** supplied home internet access and **49 percent**
  offered internet at other locations (NCES, School Pulse Panel).
- (Source for all three: NCES topical study, "Technology Support,"
  https://nces.ed.gov/surveys/annualreports/topical-studies/covid/theme/elementary-and-secondary-education-technology-support/ .)

---

## 10i. Remote schooling in 2020, and what the tests showed afterwards

- **Education Week tracked the closures "first at the school or district level and ultimately,
  state-by-state, from March 6 to May 15, 2020." Verbatim: "At their peak, the closures affected at
  least 55.1 million students in 124,000 U.S. public and private schools."** "Nearly every state
  either ordered or recommended that schools remain closed through the end of the 2019-20 school
  year." (Source: Education Week, "Map: Coronavirus and School Closures in 2019-2020,"
  https://www.edweek.org/leadership/map-coronavirus-and-school-closures-in-2019-2020/2020/03 .)
  **Journalism, not government data — label it. It is the standard national tally and no federal
  count of the closures exists.**
- **The National Assessment of Educational Progress (NAEP) was given again from January to March
  2024.** Grade 4 reading: "the average reading score for the nation at grade 4 was 2 points lower
  compared to 2022 and 5 points lower compared to 2019." "Thirty-one percent of fourth-grade
  students performed at or above the NAEP Proficient level on the 2024 NAEP reading assessment,
  which was 2 percentage points lower compared to 2022 and 4 percentage points lower than 2019."
  Grade 4 mathematics was 3 points below 2019. Grade 12 reading and grade 12 mathematics were each
  3 points below 2019.
  (Sources: The Nation's Report Card, 2024 reading and mathematics reports,
  https://www.nationsreportcard.gov/reports/reading/2024/g4_8/ and
  https://www.nationsreportcard.gov/reports/mathematics/2024/g12/ .)
- **What NOT to write:** do not say the closures caused the whole fall. NAEP scores in reading had
  already turned down between 2017 and 2019, and NAEP itself does not assign causes. State the
  scores and the years and let them sit.
