## 10j. Tabatha Rosproy — the story block for `tabatha-rosproy-2020-teaching` [V — closes the story-block target slot, "a teacher or student's account of schooling in 2020"]

**The slot asked for a real, documented, named teacher or student's account of schooling in 2020.
This is a named teacher with contemporary reporting behind her, published within weeks of the
events. There is no film about her, so `movie` is empty.**

- **Who:** Tabatha Rosproy, preschool teacher, **Winfield Early Learning Center, Winfield, Kansas.**
  Her classroom was not in a school building: it was housed inside **Cumbernauld Village**, a
  retirement community and nursing home in Winfield, and the intergenerational programme is called
  **"Little Vikes."** Her class held preschoolers with disabilities and preschoolers without them
  together, and residents of the home took part daily.
- **What she was named:** **2020 National Teacher of the Year**, announced in **May 2020** on
  *CBS This Morning*. She is **the first early childhood educator to receive the award** in the
  programme's history. (Sources: Education Week, "Tabatha Rosproy Is the First Early Childhood
  Educator to Be National Teacher of the Year," **21 May 2020**,
  https://www.edweek.org/teaching-learning/tabatha-rosproy-is-the-first-early-childhood-educator-to-be-national-teacher-of-the-year/2020/05 ;
  Chalkbeat, **22 May 2020**,
  https://www.chalkbeat.org/2020/5/22/21267995/national-teacher-of-the-year-tabatha-rosproy-preschool-kansas-coronavirus/ .)
- **Her pandemic work, from the Chalkbeat interview of 22 May 2020:** after the buildings closed she
  **pre-recorded and uploaded lessons using an app called Seesaw**, held **weekly one-to-one video
  chats with each child**, and for families who could not get online **called, texted, or delivered
  materials to their homes.** For the families of her pupils with disabilities she **prepared
  instructional materials modelling the techniques** she would have used in the room. She was a
  **co-chair of the Kansas educator task force that wrote the state's continuous learning guidance**
  after Kansas closed its school buildings for the rest of the year in March 2020.
- **Her own words, Chalkbeat, 22 May 2020 (verbatim):** "Nothing will replace what we are able to do
  in person. But we are surviving through this and we are looking for the small victories when we
  have them."
- **Her own words, Education Week, 21 May 2020 (verbatim):** "At first, I was nervous to be named
  National Teacher of the Year during this pandemic because I wondered if I was able to make a
  difference."
- **Also documented in Education Week, 21 May 2020:** "She mailed each student bins with sensory
  toys, which were their favorite part of the classroom, and put pinwheels in her students' yards."
- **Not confirmed and therefore not printed:** the exact calendar date in May 2020 of the
  announcement. The CCSSO National Teacher of the Year page for her returned HTTP 404 on this pass.
  The outline says "In May 2020," which is what the sources support.
- **Movie:** none exists. `movie=""`.
- **The Kansas closure, which the story block now names:** **on 17 March 2020 Governor Laura Kelly
  ordered every K-12 school building in Kansas closed for the rest of the school year, and Kansas
  was the first state in the country to do it.** Kelly closed the buildings rather than cancelling
  the school year; districts were told to run "Continuous Learning" plans instead, which is the
  guidance Rosproy's task force wrote. (Sources: NPR, "Kansas Becomes The First State To End
  In-Person Classes For The Year," 18 March 2020,
  https://www.npr.org/sections/Coronavirus+Live+Updates/2020/03/18/817886198/kansas-becomes-the-first-state-to-end-its-school-year ;
  Lawrence *Journal-World*, "Kansas governor orders K-12 school buildings closed for remainder of
  school year," 17 March 2020,
  https://www2.ljworld.com/news/schools/2020/mar/17/kansas-governor-orders-k-12-schools-closed-for-remainder-of-school-year/ .)

**A second candidate, checked and left unused, recorded so nobody re-researches it:** the
documentary *Homeroom* (2021, directed by Peter Nicks, Hulu) follows the Oakland High School class
of 2020 through the closures, with **Denilson Garibo**, a student director on the Oakland Unified
School District board, as its central figure. It is a real film about a real named student and it
would fill this slot too. It was not used because the reporting reachable in this pass was about the
police-in-schools campaign rather than about his own account of remote schooling, and the strongest
review page (rogerebert.com) returned HTTP 403. **If a later agent wants a student rather than a
teacher here, this is the lead to follow.**

---

## 10k. Teachers and cost — hiring, pay, college and debt

**Hiring, newest federal reading (2024-25 school year, released 17 October 2024).** NCES School
Pulse Panel, 1,392 public K-12 schools in every state and D.C., collected 13-27 August 2024:
- **"74 percent of public schools reported they had difficulty filling one or more vacant teaching
  positions"** for 2024-25. Schools reported **an average of six teaching vacancies**, and were
  **"filling 79 percent of those vacancies with fully certified teachers."**
- Reasons given: **"lack of qualified candidates (64 percent)"** and **"too few candidates applying
  (62 percent)."**
- Non-teaching posts: **69 percent** of schools had difficulty filling at least one, an average of
  five vacancies each, 73 percent filled.
- (Source: NCES press release, "Most U.S. public elementary and secondary schools faced hiring
  challenges for the start of the 2024-25 academic year," 17 October 2024,
  https://nces.ed.gov/learn/press-release/most-u-s-public-elementary-and-secondary-schools-faced-hiring-challenges-start-2024-25-academic-year .)
- **A year earlier**, from the August 2023 round (1,319 schools, collected 8-23 August 2023):
  **86 percent** of public schools reported challenges hiring teachers entering 2023-24, and the
  posts most often vacant were **general elementary (71 percent of schools) and special education
  (70 percent).**
  (Source: NCES press release, 17 October 2023, https://nces.ed.gov/whatsnew/press_releases/10_17_2023.asp .)

**Teacher pay.** **"The national average public school teacher salary for 2023-24 was $72,030,"** a
rise of 3.8 percent on the year before. NEA's own key takeaway: **"Adjusted for inflation, on
average, teachers are making 5% less than they did 10 years ago."** The average beginning teacher
salary in 2023-24 was **$46,526**, up 4.4 percent, and NEA reports real starting salaries
**$3,728 below 2008-09.** (Source: National Education Association, *Rankings and Estimates*, April
2025, and the NEA Teacher Salary Benchmark Report; summary page
https://www.nea.org/resource-library/educator-pay-and-student-spending-how-does-your-state-rank/teacher-2025 .
**NEA is the teachers' union; label it as such. The starting-salary figures come from the benchmark
report and were read from NEA's summary, not from the PDF, in this pass.**)

**College cost, 2022-23 academic year**, average total cost of attendance for a first-time,
full-time undergraduate living on campus at a four-year degree-granting institution:
**public $27,100; private for-profit $33,600; private nonprofit $58,600.** NCES also notes that
average tuition and fees rose by less than inflation from 2021-22 to 2022-23, so in constant dollars
tuition and fees were **lower** in 2022-23 than the year before.
(Source: NCES Fast Facts, "Tuition costs of colleges and universities,"
https://nces.ed.gov/fastfacts/display.asp?id=76 .)

**Student borrowing.** **"Thirty-eight percent of first-time, full-time degree/certificate-seeking
undergraduate students overall were awarded loan aid in 2020-21,"** down from 50 percent in 2010-11.
The average annual loan fell 8 percent over the same decade, **from $8,400 to $7,700.** Students who
completed a bachelor's degree in 2015-16 and had federal loans **owed an average of $45,300 as of
2020.** (Source: NCES Fast Facts, "Student debt," https://nces.ed.gov/fastfacts/display.asp?id=900 .)

**Not used, and why.** The Federal Student Aid portfolio page
(https://studentaid.gov/data-center/student/portfolio ) is the primary source for the national total
of federal student loan debt, but it renders its numbers through JavaScript and returned an empty
document to both WebFetch and curl on this pass. Secondary reporting gives roughly **$1.6-1.7
trillion owed by about 42 million borrowers**, and a Department of Education press release of 2025
gives **5.3 million ED-serviced borrowers in default with nearly $117 billion outstanding as of June
2025**. **None of that was confirmed against the primary table, so none of it is in the outline.**
A later agent should retry the portfolio page or use the quarterly XLS files under
`studentaid.gov/data-center/student/portfolio`.

# PART C-ACCESS — what could not be fetched, so nobody repeats the attempts

Verified 2026-09-08 by the eras 6-7 agent, on top of the eras 3-5 agent's list (britannica.com, monticello.org, Colonial Williamsburg research pages, UNB laws database):

- **loc.gov** — item and resource pages return **HTTP 403** to WebFetch and to curl; the block is a Cloudflare challenge, and it also blocks `?fo=json`. **loc.gov *research guides* at `guides.loc.gov` DO work.**
- **hmdb.org** (Historical Marker Database) — 403.
- **riamco.org** (Rhode Island Archival and Manuscript Collections Online) — 403.
- **perkins.org** — 403.
- **ncpedia.org** — 403.
- **federalregister.gov** HTML document pages — 302 redirect to an "unblock" page. **Workarounds that work:** `https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents/<docnum>.json` and the `full_text/text/...txt` URL it returns.
- **Added 2026-09-08 by the era-8 agent:** **si.edu and americanhistory.si.edu now return 403** behind a "Smithsonian request verification" JavaScript wall (they worked for the eras 6-7 agent; this is new). **guides.loc.gov now 403s too** — the eras 6-7 agent recorded it as working. **supreme.justia.com 403s; oregonencyclopedia.org 403s; scencyclopedia.org returned 503; courtlistener.com HTML returns an empty 202 and its REST API returns 401 without a key.** **law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/<volume>/<page> works and returns full opinion text** — that is the reliable route to Supreme Court opinions here. **archive.org OCR text works but the /download/ redirect sometimes 500s or 404s; read the item metadata at archive.org/metadata/<id> and fetch https://<server><dir>/<file> directly.** **files.eric.ed.gov, nces.ed.gov, nps.gov, archives.gov, va.gov, nber.org, encyclopediavirginia.org, findingaids.library.cofc.edu, deweycenter.siu.edu and lib.uchicago.edu all worked.**
- **Added 2026-09-09 by the era-9 finishing agent:** **law.justia.com and leagle.com both return HTTP 403** — **case-law.vlex.com works** and was the route to *People v. DeJonge*, *State v. Riddle* and *Mills v. Board of Education*. **encyclopediaofarkansas.net is behind a Cloudflare block** (it serves from wpenginepowered.com). **education.jhu.edu returns 403.** **mississippiencyclopedia.org truncates for WebFetch, but `curl` plus a tag-strip returns the whole entry** — that technique got four entries. **Working this pass: sites.ed.gov, nces.ed.gov PDFs, files.eric.ed.gov PDFs, lrl.mn.gov, mshistorynow.mdah.ms.gov, pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov, law.cornell.edu, educationnext.org, southerneducation.org, mackinac.org, crhe.org.**
- **A verification trap worth repeating.** Asked to summarise the Gershoff and Font state-ban table, a fetch returned Massachusetts as **1871**, Hawaii **1873** and Maine **1875**. Asked to copy the digits verbatim, the same page returned **1971, 1973, 1975**. **When a century looks surprising, ask for the digits verbatim before printing them.**
- **Things that DO work well and were used here:** nps.gov (deep and specific), archives.gov, si.edu and Digital Commonwealth, nifa.usda.gov, nces.ed.gov PDFs, archive.org full text (including `_djvu.txt` downloads), the Harvard Library open API at `api.lib.harvard.edu/v2/items.json`, Project Gutenberg plain text, university special-collections pages, and state historical-society projects.
- **Added 2026-09-09 by the era-10 agent:** **congress.gov returns HTTP 403** — use
  `govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-<congress>publ<num>/html/PLAW-<congress>publ<num>.htm` for statutes,
  which works and returns full text. **cnn.com returns HTTP 451. rogerebert.com 403s.
  ies.ed.gov/learn/press-release/... 403s to WebFetch even though nces.ed.gov/learn/press-release/...
  works. education.jhu.edu still 403s. studentaid.gov/data-center/student/portfolio renders its
  numbers in JavaScript and returns an empty document to WebFetch and to curl.** **npr.org times out
  in WebFetch; `curl -A 'Mozilla/5.0'` plus a tag-strip returns the whole article** and that is how
  the August 2026 corporal-punishment piece was read. **Every PDF from ed.gov, apsac.org, nces.ed.gov
  and files.eric.ed.gov came back as binary to WebFetch: download it and run `pdftotext -layout`,
  which worked every time.** **Working this pass: nces.ed.gov (Digest tables, Condition of Education
  indicators, NTPS and School Pulse tables, Fast Facts), ed.gov media documents, govinfo.gov,
  census.gov, leginfo.legislature.ca.gov, pewresearch.org, edchoice.org, crhe.org and
  responsiblehomeschooling.org, fordhaminstitute.org, sciline.org, nationsreportcard.gov, nea.org,
  edweek.org, chalkbeat.org, wusf.org, publications.aap.org (via search).**
- **Two counting traps this pass hit, worth repeating.** (1) **NPR's "almost 32,000" and the CRDC's
  "approximately 24,500" for corporal punishment in 2021-22 are both right**: one counts reported
  *instances*, the other counts *children*. Say which. (2) **The APSAC alert says "Fifteen states"
  and then lists fourteen.** A published count and a published list can disagree inside one
  document; count the list before printing it.
- **Technique worth reusing:** several key facts here came from downloading a PDF or an OCR text file and reading it with `pdftotext -layout` and `grep` rather than asking a fetch tool to summarise it. That is how the 1866 Cambridge school committee pamphlet, the Alvord report, the NCES table and the Dickens text were verified word for word.

# PART B — material parked here by other chapters' agents (unchanged)

## Parked from `native-nations` (2026-08-07) — Indian boarding schools AS SCHOOLS (ruling 4/7: native-nations leads on what was done to the nations; education covers them as part of the schooling system)
- **The model school:** Carlisle Indian Industrial School, opened November 1, 1879, Carlisle, Pennsylvania; founder Richard Henry Pratt. The school day: **half academic classroom, half unpaid labor** that ran the institution (farms, shops, laundry, kitchens) — the "half-day plan." Pratt's **"outing system"** placed students with white families to work summers and school terms. (Sources: Carlisle Indian School Digital Resource Center, Dickinson College; DOI Federal Indian Boarding School Initiative Report Vol. 1, 2022.)
- **Attendance law:** 1891 — Congress authorized the Commissioner of Indian Affairs to compel Native children's school attendance; 1893 — authorized withholding rations, clothing, and annuities from parents who refused. (Sources: Native Philanthropy timeline; LegalClarity boarding-school legal history.)
- **Scale of the system (federal count):** 417 federal Indian boarding schools in 37 states/territories, 1819–1969 (DOI Investigative Report Vol. 2, July 30, 2024; Vol. 1, May 2022, counted 408), plus 1,025 similar institutions outside the four criteria.
- **The two big federal condemnations, for the schooling-reform arc:** the **Meriam Report (1928)**, "The Problem of Indian Administration" — documented the schools' conditions in federal print; the **Kennedy Report (November 1969)**, Senate Report 91-501, *Indian Education: A National Tragedy — A National Challenge* — "coercive assimilation," "disastrous effects," recommended Native control of Native education. (Sources: Britannica; ERIC ED034625; NARF NILL.)
- **Native control of schooling:** Navajo Community College (1968) — first tribally controlled college, today Diné College; the Indian Self-Determination and **Education** Assistance Act (P.L. 93-638, January 1975) let nations contract to run their own schools; boarding-school enrollment peaked ~60,000 in 1973 and fell as nations took schools over. (Sources: Diné College history/UAF; PWNA education history.)
- **Literacy story for the schooling chapter:** Sequoyah's Cherokee syllabary (demonstrated 1821, 86 characters) made the Cherokee Nation broadly literate within a few years — schooling and print in an Indigenous language, decades before compulsory English-only schooling (Cherokee Phoenix, 1828, first Native newspaper). (Sources: Britannica "Sequoyah"; Smithsonian Magazine.)
- **Immersion schooling (modern):** ʻAha Pūnana Leo Hawaiian immersion preschools from 1984; Hawaii's ban on teaching in Hawaiian lifted 1986; first public immersion classes 1987. Wôpanâak Language Reclamation Project (1993) classes/camps in Massachusetts. (Sources: ʻAha Pūnana Leo official history; MacArthur Foundation — Jessie Little Doe Baird.)

## Parked from `home-family` (2026-08-07) — school-calendar, compulsory-attendance, and homeschooling verifications (your VERIFY items; our chapter uses only the household side)
- **The two-term rural calendar (verified):** before ~1900 district schools commonly ran a summer term (roughly May–August; younger children; women teachers) and a winter term (roughly November–April; older boys spared from farm work; men often hired for order). Attendance bent to planting and harvest. Teachers "boarded around" with district families in turn. (Sources: Morning Ag Clips "The One-Room Schoolhouse and American Rural Education"; Colchester (NY) Historical Society; Heritage League "America's One-Room Schools of the 1890s" PDF.)
- **Compulsory attendance (verified):** Massachusetts first, 1852 — ages 8–14, minimum 12 weeks/year, 6 consecutive; 32 states by 1900; **all states by 1918, Mississippi last**. Enforcement strength in the early decades was NOT verified in this pass. (Sources: Boston Magazine TBT; Sage Encyclopedia of Education Law "Compulsory Attendance"; usedulaw.com.)
- **Homeschooling's two roots (verified — clears your [VERIFY] on leading figures):** **John Holt** (secular/school-reform side) started *Growing Without Schooling*, likely the first homeschooling newsletter, **August 1977**; **Raymond Moore** (religious side) argued against early institutional schooling, *Home Grown Kids* (1981). Illegal or effectively illegal in many states into the 1980s; period documentation = GWS reader letters. Early movement was ideologically mixed (progressive unschoolers + fundamentalist Christians). (Sources: johnholtgws.com; Coalition for Responsible Home Education "A Brief History of Homeschooling"; Sutherland Institute history series; Gospel Coalition summary.) [Your remaining VERIFY: named prosecutions/states; the last state to legalize and its year.]
- **2020 homeschooling jump (verified, Census Household Pulse):** households with school-age children homeschooling (not remote public school): **5.4% (spring 2020) → 11.1% (fall 2020)**; Black households **3.3% → 16.1%**; pre-pandemic norm ~3.3%. How much stayed: still unsettled — both chapters state it openly. (Sources: census.gov "Homeschooling on the Rise During COVID-19 Pandemic," March 2021; NPR summary.)

- **By the original G.I. Bill's end (July 25, 1956), 7.8 million of 16 million WWII veterans had used its education or training benefits** (VA, "Born of Controversy: The GI Bill of Rights"). The VA also backed ~2.4 million home loans 1944–1952 (that side parked with `home-family`/`economy`). `economy` keeps one span line on the middle-class economics; the classrooms, campuses, and who was in practice excluded (Black veterans facing segregated colleges and loan discrimination — worth your verification pass) are yours.


## Parked from `rights-movements` (era-7 agent, 2026-09-09) — era 7 material this chapter may want

**7. 1850 to 1900.**
- **Edward H. Clarke, *Sex in Education; or, A Fair Chance for the Girls*, Boston: James R. Osgood and Company, 1873, 181 pages.** Clarke (1820-1877) was a Harvard Medical School professor. Catalogue records: National Library of Medicine, https://collections.nlm.nih.gov/catalog/nlm:nlmuid-61410300R-bk ; Library of Congress, https://www.loc.gov/item/07031164/ . **EDITION TRAP: the Project Gutenberg transcription (https://www.gutenberg.org/files/18504/18504-h/18504-h.htm) has 1875 on its title page and the subtitle "A Fair Chance for Girls." It is a later printing. Date the book 1873.**
  - Verbatim from the text: "The results are monstrous brains and puny bodies; abnormally active cerebration, and abnormally weak digestion." · "Unless the work is accomplished at that period, unless the reproductive mechanism is built and put in good working order at that time, it will never be perfectly accomplished afterwards." · of his case Miss A, "She graduated at nineteen, the first scholar, and an invalid."
  - Smith College's summary of what he warned of: study "in a 'boy's way'" risked "atrophy of the uterus and ovaries, masculinization, sterility, insanity, and even death"; he held women should study no more than four hours a day and not at all while menstruating. https://www.smith.edu/news-events/news/pushing-beyond-gender-boundaries
  - The answers: Smith's founding president **Laurenus Clark Seelye** at the 1875 opening — "It is to preserve her womanliness that this College has been founded." **Mary Putnam Jacobi** answered with an essay in *The Education of American Girls* (1874) and then with data: a thousand questionnaires sent through women's rights networks, and the resulting essay *The Question of Rest During Menstruation*, which won Harvard's **Boylston Prize in 1876** (first woman to win it). https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/mary-putnam-jacobi
  - **NOT SOURCED and NOT to be claimed:** the number of printings the book went through, and an 1885 Association of Collegiate Alumnae health survey. The ACA was founded in Boston in 1882 (Smith College finding aid, https://findingaids.smith.edu/repositories/2/resources/713 ); no survey was confirmed.
- **Coeducation dates, with the trap in each one.** University of Iowa's own archive says two different things and both should be quoted: "Since its founding, the University of Iowa has admitted women as well as men, the first public university in the U.S. to do so," and "Beginning in 1860, women were admitted on an equal basis with men." (https://www.lib.uiowa.edu/sc/archives/faq/faqfirsts/ ; same page: **Mary Hickey Wilkinson** graduated from Iowa's College of Law in **1873**.) **Michigan: February 1870, Madelon Stockwell** (https://heritage.umich.edu/stories/madelons-world/ ). **Cornell: first woman 1870, Jennie Spencer of Cortland, who withdrew because there was nowhere near campus for a woman to live; women admitted regularly from 1872** (https://diversity.cornell.edu/about-us/our-historic-commitment?field_decade_value=1850-1899 ).
- **Vassar:** established 1861 by Matthew Vassar at Poughkeepsie, opened **26 September 1865 with 353 students**, $350 for tuition and residence (https://www.vassar.edu/about/history ). **Spelman: 11 April 1881**, basement of Friendship Baptist Church, Atlanta, founded by **Sophia B. Packard and Harriet E. Giles** with the pastor **Frank Quarles**, first named the Atlanta Baptist Female Seminary, **eleven pupils — ten women, some formerly enslaved, and one girl** (https://www.spelman.edu/about/history-in-brief.html ).
- **San Francisco's segregated schools, from the district itself:** 1851 first public schools, white children only; 1854 first segregated school for Black children; 1859 first for Chinese children; **1870 the district closed the Chinese school, leaving Chinese American children with no public school at all**; 1885, after *Tape v. Hurley*, "SFUSD reopened the segregated Chinese school as a way to avoid integrating white schools." (https://www.sfusd.edu/facing-our-past-changing-our-future-part-i-century-segregation-san-francisco-unified-school-district )
- **Massachusetts outlawed segregated public schools in 1855**, after William Cooper Nell's boycotts and petition drives — 68 years after Prince Hall's 1787 petition. The same NPS page adds that segregation policies still shaped Boston's schools for decades afterwards despite the law. (https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/the-black-struggle-for-equal-education-in-boston-1787-to-1976.htm )
- `rights-movements` carries the campaigns (the Tape family's lawsuit is its story `mary-and-joseph-tape`, verified). `education` has the schools as schools.

## Parked from `rights-movements` era 8 (2026-09-09) — the Lemon Grove case of 1931, and two corrections

**`education` is already researched, so this is offered, not required.** Two items found while
researching `rights-movements` era 8 that `education` does not currently carry.

**1. *Roberto Alvarez v. the Board of Trustees of the Lemon Grove School District*, San Diego
County, 1931 — the first school desegregation case won in the United States, fifteen years before
*Mendez*.** `education`'s era-8 cell has *Mendez* (story `sylvia-mendez`) but not this.
- School board planned it in the summer of 1930. On **5 January 1931** the principal of Lemon Grove
  Grammar School, **Jerome T. Green**, stood in the doorway and admitted every pupil except the
  Mexican American ones, telling them to go to a separate two-room building instead.
- The school had **169 pupils: 87 counted as European American, about 75 Mexican American, 7
  Japanese American.**
- The parents formed the **Comité de Vecinos de Lemon Grove**, kept their children out of the
  separate building, and hired lawyers **Fred Noon and A. C. Brinkley**. Named plaintiff: one of the
  pupils, **Roberto Alvarez** (**his age is not given by the fetched source**).
- **Judge Claude Chambers**, San Diego County Superior Court, ruled for the children on **30 March
  1931**, ordering them admitted "on a basis of equality with other children of said school
  district, and without separation or segregation in a separate school because of race or
  nationality," and saying that separating "all the Mexicans in one group can only be done by
  infringing the laws of the State of California." He rejected the argument that a separate school
  would teach them English faster.
- Source: California Legislature, ACR 146 committee analysis, 25 March 2016,
  https://www.leginfo.ca.gov/pub/15-16/bill/asm/ab_0101-0150/acr_146_cfa_20160325_085955_asm_comm.html
- **Date not settled:** that analysis says 30 March 1931; other accounts say 31 March. The San
  Diego History Center journal article returned 403.

**2. *Watson v. City of Cambridge*, 157 Mass. 561, decided 4 January 1893** — the ruling that let
schools exclude disabled children for the whole of the next era, and the precedent behind
*Beattie* (1919), which `education` already carries. Plaintiff **John A. Watson**, excluded from
the Cambridge public schools in **1885**. Justice Knowlton, quoting the school committee's own
record: he was excluded "because he was too weak-minded to derive profit from instruction," and
was "troublesome to other children, making uncouth noises, pinching others." The court held the
committee's decision final and beyond review by a court.
Source: CourtListener, opinion cluster 6550666,
https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/6550666/watson-v-city-of-cambridge/ — **the full text could
not be fetched** (empty page to WebFetch, 0 bytes to curl, opinions API needs a key); the quoted
opening paragraph came from the CourtListener search API index.

**3. Parent organizing, 1933.** The **Cuyahoga County Council for the Retarded Child**, Cleveland,
1933 — the county's own successor agency calls it "a grass-roots group, led by parents of children
with developmental disabilities ... founded to assist children of the region who had been excluded
from their public schools." https://cuyahogadd.org/about-us/history/ . **The county's own history
names no founders**, so the common "five mothers" detail is not used in `rights-movements` and
should not be used without a source that names them.

## Parked from `rights-movements` era 10 (2026-09-09) — how segregated the schools are now, with the measures and their source years

`education` is fully researched and its era-10 cell covers testing, choice, homeschooling, the school
day, discipline and remote schooling. **It carries no measure of present-day school segregation.**
`rights-movements` era 10 now carries one, sourced as follows. Take what you want; the standing
project rule is that **every figure carries the year it describes AND the year of its source.**

- **The federal measure.** U.S. Government Accountability Office, **GAO-22-104737**, *K-12 Education:
  Student Population Has Significantly Diversified, but Many Schools Remain Divided Along Racial,
  Ethnic, and Economic Lines* — **issued 16 June 2022, publicly released 14 July 2022**. In **school
  year 2020-21**, more than a third of students — **about 18.5 million** — **"attended schools where
  75 percent or more students were of a single race or ethnicity,"** and **"14 percent of students
  attended schools where 90 percent or more of the students were of a single race/ethnicity."** The
  report covers **2014-15 through 2020-21** and **makes no comparison with 1970.**
- **The long series, from a university research centre — label it that way.** Gary Orfield and Ryan
  Pfleger, *The Unfinished Battle for Integration in a Multiracial America — from Brown to Now*, the
  Civil Rights Project / Proyecto Derechos Civiles, UCLA, **April 2024**, using the federal Common
  Core of Data (1968 figures from Orfield's own 1983 analysis of Office for Civil Rights data).
  PDF: civilrightsproject.ucla.edu/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/National-Segregation-041624-CORRECTED-for-1.pdf
  - "Intensely segregated" is defined as **90-100 percent non-white.** Share of American public
    schools in that category: **7.4 percent in 1988** ("out of the approximately 67,000 schools in
    the data, approximately 5,000"), **19.8 percent in 2021.** **The series starts in 1988, not
    1970.**
  - **In 2021, 78 percent of the students in those schools were poor.**
  - **The South, on a measure that does reach 1968:** the share of Black students in majority-white
    schools **peaked at 43 percent in the mid-1980s** and was **"around 16% as of 2021"**; for Latino
    students in the South it fell from **30.4 percent in 1968 to 15.4 percent in 2021.** The report
    ties the turn to *Board of Education of Oklahoma City v. Dowell*, 498 U.S. 237 (1991).
  - **Who is in the public schools:** white **80.7 percent in 1968, 45.3 percent in 2021**; Black
    **14.7 percent in 1968, 14.9 percent in 2021**; Latino **4.7 percent in 1968, 28.2 percent in
    2021.**
- **THE HONEST FINDING, which `rights-movements` states in the outline: no source retrieved on
  2026-09-09 compares school segregation now with 1970.** The federal count begins in 2014-15 and
  the intense-segregation series begins in 1988. **Do not write "compared with 1970."**

### Also parked for `education`: the disability-in-school thread's era-10 statute
The **ADA Amendments Act of 2008 (Public Law 110-325, 25 September 2008)** restored a definition of
disability that the Supreme Court had narrowed in *Sutton* (1999) and *Toyota v. Williams* (2002).
Congress wrote in the statute that lower courts had **"incorrectly found in individual cases that
people with a range of substantially limiting impairments are not people with disabilities."** The
new text says an impairment **"that is episodic or in remission is a disability if it would
substantially limit a major life activity when active"** and that the question is decided **"without
regard to the ameliorative effects of mitigating measures"** — medication, prosthetics, hearing aids
and cochlear implants, mobility devices, oxygen equipment, assistive technology — with **ordinary
eyeglasses and contact lenses expressly excluded from that list.** That is what governs whether a
student counts as disabled for a school's purposes.
